# Front Cover

->[Image of the front cover of the volume]<-

<note>The volume is bound in dark brown, aged leather, showing signs of significant use and historical wear.</note>

The cover features a classic rectangular design, characteristic of early modern bookbinding. A central panel of darkened leather is framed by a delicate, stippled border created through <term>blind-tooling</term> <note>a technique where decorative patterns are pressed into leather without the use of gold leaf or ink</note>. 

From the corners of this central rectangle, diagonal lines of stippled dots extend toward the outer edges of the cover. At each of the four outer corners, the binder has embossed a decorative motif resembling a stylized <term>fleur-de-lis</term> <note>a decorative lily symbol often used in heraldry and book ornamentation</note> or a floral ornament.

### BY CHRISTIAN
# HUYGENS
### OF ZUYLICHEM, SON OF CONSTANTYN
# THE PENDULUM
# CLOCK
### OR
## GEOMETRIC DEMONSTRATIONS
## REGARDING THE MOTION OF PENDULUMS
### AS APPLIED TO
#### TIMEPIECES.

<summary>The title page of Christiaan Huygens' landmark scientific treatise, "Horologium Oscillatorium," which presents the mathematical and mechanical foundations of the pendulum clock. Published in Paris in 1673, it is dedicated to King Louis XIV and represents a pinnacle of 17th-century physics and horology.</summary>
<keywords>Christiaan Huygens, pendulum clock, horology, geometry, physics, Louis XIV, François Muguet, Paris, 17th century science</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page serves as the table of contents for Christiaan Huygens' seminal 1673 work on the pendulum clock, outlining the five-part structure of the treatise.</meta>

->This book is divided into five parts,<-
->of which<-

The First *contains the Description of the* **OSCILLATING CLOCK.** <note>original: "Horologium Oscillatorium." This refers to the pendulum clock, which Huygens invented in 1656.</note>

The Second *deals with the* Fall of heavy bodies, *and* *their motion in a* Cycloid. <note>A cycloid is the curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line; Huygens discovered it was the ideal path for a pendulum.</note>

The Third *concerns the* Evolution *and* *Measurement of curved lines.* <note>Huygens developed the theory of "evolutes" to ensure the pendulum followed the necessary cycloidal path.</note>

The Fourth *concerns the* Center of Oscillation or Agitation. <note>This section addresses the mathematical point in a pendulum where its entire mass can be considered concentrated.</note>

The Fifth *presents the construction of another Clock, in which the motion of the pendulum is circular, and* Theorems *on* Centrifugal Force. <note>original: "Vi Centrifuga." This is the first formal mathematical treatment of the force that acts on an object moving in a circular path.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the five sections of Huygens' masterpiece, covering the mechanical description of the pendulum clock, the physics of falling bodies, and the mathematics of centrifugal force.</summary>
<keywords>pendulum clock, cycloid, center of oscillation, centrifugal force, Christiaan Huygens, horology, classical mechanics</keywords>

# TO LOUIS XIV,

## OF FRANCE AND NAVARRE

### ILLUSTRIOUS KING.

WE owe the rebirth and restoration of Geometry in this century, greatest King, chiefly to France. For from here arose those who first restored it—when it was lost in its greatest and better part and, as it were, buried—and brought it back into the light. Following in their footsteps, men of the most subtle intellect throughout all Europe have since so cultivated it, that now few things seem left by them for the industry of posterity; indeed, they have far surpassed the discoveries of the ancients. In this science, which I have always admired and loved most dearly, whenever I have applied my mind to it, I have set before myself for investigation those things above all others which, once discovered, might prove useful either for the conveniences of life or for the knowledge of Nature. Then, truly, I considered my labor best spent when I happened upon those matters in which utility was joined with the difficulty of discovery and some degree of subtlety...

<summary>The author dedicates his mathematical work to King Louis XIV, crediting French scholars with the modern revival of geometry and stating his own preference for practical and natural applications of the science.</summary>
<keywords>Louis XIV, France, Geometry, History of Science, Scientific Discovery, Nature</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of Christiaan Huygens' work on the pendulum clock to King Louis XIV of France.</meta>

which would be joined together. But if I may be permitted to claim some small recommendation for my gift, so that it does not appear entirely unworthy of your greatness; I profess that I have achieved both goals most successfully in this invention of the Clock <note>original: "Horologii"</note>. For, while on one hand it is a mechanical invention; on the other hand—and by far the most important—it consists of geometric principles. Regarding this latter part, it had to be sought with no small effort from the innermost recesses of the art: so much so, that among all the subjects I have treated with more intense study until now, I would undoubtedly grant the first place to this speculation.

Indeed, there is no need for me to labor at length, most powerful King <note>original: "Rex potentissime"</note>, to show you the utility in these things. For you have not only discovered through long experience, since our Automata <note>self-moving machines, specifically pendulum clocks</note> deserved to be received into the inner chambers of your palace, how much they excel other machines of this kind in the steady demonstration of the hours: but you are also aware of their more important uses, those for which they were destined by me from the very beginning. Namely, those uses which they are fit to provide both in Celestial observations and in measuring the Longitudes of places while navigating. For by your command, our Clocks have been carried across the sea more than once. Under your auspices, many of these same clocks, dedicated to the uses of Astronomy, are seen in that famous citadel of Urania <note>The Paris Observatory, named for the Muse of Astronomy</note>, which you recently cared to have built with such remarkable magnificence, and on a scale greater than any king before. Whenever I reflect upon these things, I think of the fortune of this—

<summary>Huygens argues that his pendulum clock is his greatest achievement because it combines mechanical skill with deep geometric theory, specifically for use in navigation and astronomy. He praises King Louis XIV for testing the clocks at sea and installing them in the new Paris Observatory.</summary>
<keywords>pendulum clock, geometry, longitude, navigation, astronomy, Paris Observatory, Louis XIV, automata</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of Christiaan Huygens' work on the pendulum clock (the Horologium Oscillatorium) to King Louis XIV of France. Huygens is expressing his gratitude for the King's patronage and explaining why he has dedicated his scientific labors to him.</meta>

ã iij

the fortune of this discovery, which occurred during your reign, I often count myself quite fortunate. Nor do I think anyone who understands how much that invention owes to you will now ask why I decided to dedicate these long nights of study <note>original: "lucubrationes," referring to scholarly work performed by candlelight</note>—in which I have explained its entire theory and design—to your most noble Name.

Even less will he wonder when he learns that it is through your kindness that I have enjoyed the peaceful leisure necessary to contemplate these and other matters. For I felt I must strive to give some account of myself to you; I had to try, in whatever way possible, to fulfill my duty with some expression of gratitude, having been blessed by your many and continuous favors. 

I am well aware that, occupied as you are with the most important affairs of state—those duties fitting for one placed at the very pinnacle of power—you are hardly free to turn your attention toward these types of contemplations, even though your mind is capable of grasping all subjects. Yet I believe these works will be no less welcome or approved by you, most noble King <note>original: "Rex augustissime"</note>; for we see that those things which benefit the public the most please you above all. Nothing concerns you more than ensuring that the finest sciences <note>original: "optimæ disciplinæ"</note> continue to grow and are enlightened by new discoveries. 

This is clearly shown by your extraordinary and singular generosity <note>original: "liberalitas"</note>, both in promoting the sciences themselves and in rewarding those who distinguish themselves through their knowledge of them. Neither the immense and unusually high costs of war <note>original: "bellorum impensæ"</note> diminish this generosity, nor do the borders of your France <note>original: "Galliæ"</note> limit it. It is plain to see that your goal is not only that under...

<summary>Huygens expresses his gratitude to King Louis XIV, noting that the King's patronage provided the leisure needed for his scientific discoveries. He praises the King's support of the sciences, which remains steadfast despite the high costs of war.</summary>
<keywords>patronage, Louis XIV, scientific discovery, gratitude, royal generosity, France, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication of Christiaan Huygens' "Horologium Oscillatorium" (The Pendulum Clock) to King Louis XIV. It follows the previous page where Huygens praised the King's generosity toward the sciences and the arts.</meta>

not only those living under your rule, but also the whole world—wherever it is worthy of your favor—may become more learned, more refined, and more prosperous under your reign. To this most true and illustrious glory of yours, perhaps these literary monuments <note>original: "literaria monumenta"; here Huygens refers to his published scientific findings as a lasting memorial or "monument" in writing</note> may also contribute; so that, if they can testify to posterity that these studies and arts flourished in this age, they may simultaneously teach future generations that this success is to be credited, above all else, to your virtue and greatness of spirit.

->Paris; March 25, 1673.<- <note>original: "Lutetiæ Pariſiorum ; xxv. Mart. A. CIƆIƆCLXXIII." The date is written in a late Renaissance style where CIƆ represents 1,000 and IƆ represents 500, totaling 1673.</note>

<summary>In this concluding section of the dedication, Huygens expresses his hope that his scientific work will serve as a lasting tribute to King Louis XIV's reign. He attributes the flourishing of the sciences directly to the King's character and patronage.</summary>

While the previous page focused on royal patronage, this poem celebrates Huygens' scientific achievements—specifically his astronomical and horological discoveries—using the imagery of classical Greek and Roman poetry.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal headpiece consisting of two rows of fleur-de-lis and floral ornaments.</image-desc>

# THE POETRY OF HADRIAN VALLIUS
## DAPHNIS,
### AN ECLOGUE.

->To Christiaan Huygens of Zulichem,<-
->Son of Constantijn.<-

<margin>* Scheveningen, a village among the Dutch, adjacent to the sea.</margin>

Guardians of the coast, and at the same time a delightful pleasure, 
O daughters of the ocean at Scheveningen * beloved by Apollo; 
Prosper this Pierian labor <note>original: "Pierium... laborem"; the Pierides were the Muses, so this refers to the work of poetic composition</note> for me: 
we shall tell with what song the sailor Ancon passed the 
wakeful night: so may Pan never wash in your 
whirlpool, nor may shameful Fauns defile the waters. 
You, whom Fame carries in her chariot above the golden stars, 
let it not irk you to lend us a favoring ear, 
O SON OF HUYGENS, glory of the Huygens family, of your brothers and your father: 
We bring gifts not unworthy of your perception, 
adapted to Sicilian measures <note>original: "Siceliſin... modis"; a reference to the style of Theocritus, the Greek father of pastoral poetry who lived in Sicily</note> by a Dutch poet, 
mixing Solensian inventions <note>original: "commenta Solenſia"; referring to Aratus of Soli, a Greek poet famous for "Phaenomena," a poem about the stars—appropriate for the astronomer Huygens</note> with ancient verse, 
and weaving in you and your famous discoveries. 
Now Phoebe <note>the Moon</note>, vying with her brother’s rays <note>the Sun's light</note>, 
had raised her head from the Ocean, surrounded by smaller stars, 
when the pastoral youth were preparing their return, 
who had delighted in gathering sea shells under the full 
moon and catching crabs clinging to the shallows. 
Yet they notice Ancon lingering on a high 
hill, so many times accused of his promised song. 
Thestylis and Corydon themselves, followed by the rest of the crowd, 
come upon him from behind and encircle him in a ring. 
They gently ask what he is doing: then they pray for favorable things; 
they demand his vow, and call for the promised poems. 
He replies: “O boys, I sat as a scout for what tomorrow’s dawn 
might bring: gulls in a swarming troop, 
or a solitary-wandering crow, or lonely kingfishers <note>original: "alcyones"; halcyons, mythical birds associated with calming the seas</note>...”

<summary>This page opens a pastoral poem dedicated to Christiaan Huygens, framing his modern scientific discoveries within the classical tradition of a Greek eclogue. The poet calls upon the Muses and local sea-nymphs of Scheveningen to witness a song performed by a sailor named Ancon.</summary>

<keywords>Hadrian Vallius, Christiaan Huygens, Scheveningen, pastoral poetry, eclogue, astronomy, Muses, mythology, Dutch literature</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the Latin eclogue (pastoral poem) by Hadrian Vallius. The previous page established the setting at Scheveningen and introduced the poem as a tribute to Christiaan Huygens, blending the style of Virgil’s shepherd songs with the astronomical observations of Aratus. Here, the speaker, a sailor named Ancon, observes the night sky and begins a song about the origins of the gods and the constellations.</meta>

<header>DAPHNIS, AN ECLOGUE.</header>

If any signs are granted to me, tomorrow the surface of the sea must be plowed. 
Until now, the clemency of Jupiter has held the sky in clear stillness, 
And the heavens are painted all around with so many living things. 
The monsters that we marvel at in the glassy deep, 
The radiant ether also carries: the faces and forms of swimmers. 
The Crab <note>the constellation Cancer</note> is there, and the Dolphin; and the Whale with its massive body. 
Toward the North are the Fish <note>Pisces</note>, and you may observe beneath the South 
The Fish; where lately they are said to have increased in number. 
There is the Urn <note>Aquarius</note>, and the River <note>Eridanus</note>, and the Keel adorned with its stern-ornament <note>Argo Navis, a large southern constellation</note>. 
Indeed, there are many likenesses of your own work, 
And lights in the sky that owe their names to the flock. 
There are the Kids and the little Sows <note>the Hyades</note>, and the Mother Goat <note>Capella</note>. 
And the path that glows white with spilled milk <note>the Milky Way</note>. 
Guarding the entrance, shining with golden fleece, 
Is the leader, the Ram <note>Aries</note>, and the Great Bull with gilded horns <note>Taurus</note>. 
Two Dogs <note>Canis Major and Minor</note> are seen, and the Herdsman <note>Boötes</note> who stays awake all night; 
And the Wagons <note>the Big and Little Dipper</note>; and the Charioteer <note>Auriga</note> shaken from his reins. 
The winged Horse <note>Pegasus</note> flies through the starry void: 
And near their manger the little Asses <note>Aselli, stars in the constellation Cancer</note> are stalled. 
There is the Virgin <note>Virgo</note>, her hand bright with an ear of grain <note>the star Spica</note>, 
And, his face transformed, Pan himself <note>Capricorn, often depicted as a goat-fish</note> shines back; 
Loving your Daphnis <note>here referring to Huygens</note>, in the shadow of a secret rock, 
Just as Urania <note>the Muse of Astronomy</note> taught me: and Daphnis taught me each detail. 
Each detail which (since you ask for a song) I shall reveal in order. 
    Straightway he tries his voice: and weighing the numbers and measures, 
He soothes the breezes with various harmonies. 
Then the winds fell still. The sea lies without waves; 
Silence was made upon the lands, and silence made upon the deep. 
Soon he speaks: "May it be prosperous; from great Jupiter 
I shall begin: inspired bards are accustomed to begin from Jupiter. 
For you—since no brief order of things is born for me here— 
Ward off the nocturnal cold from your bodies with a dance." 
    Then he celebrates the cradle of great Jupiter, and the cruel 
Command of ancient Saturn, and the trickery of Cybele; 
And the Corybantian <note>the Corybantes were armed dancers who guarded the infant Zeus/Jupiter</note> rites begun in the hiding places of Dicte: 
How a wife was chosen as a nurse for the boy from a fragrant husband <note>a reference to the goat Amalthea</note>; 
She herself having recently given birth to twin kids; 
Accompanied by whom, she now frequently haunts the sky as a bright star, 
The beast who previously bleated in the Olenian fields; 
And now she treads the threshold of beautiful Olympus beneath her feet.

<summary>A sailor describes the constellations as a reflection of the sea and land before beginning a mythological song about the infancy of Jupiter and the origin of the constellation Capella. The poem uses astronomical cataloging to honor Christiaan Huygens’ scientific interests through a classical pastoral lens.</summary>

<keywords>astronomy, constellations, Zodiac, Jupiter, Saturn, Cybele, Milky Way, mythology, Urania, Daphnis, Amalthea, Corybantes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Latin eclogue (pastoral poem) by Hadrian Vallius. # DAPHNIS, AN ECLOGUE.

So great was the love of Jupiter, and the benefit of the milk he received. Yet it was not without reason that this snowy brilliance flowed in two divided paths, clear to the eyes of those who see, where the path glows white leading to the dwellings of the Thunderer <note>original: *Tonantis*, an epithet for Jupiter, the god of thunder</note>; instead, he sang of a triple night and of Hercules <note>original: *Alciden*, a name for Hercules referring to his grandfather Alceus</note> brought forth for the world. He could not have been considered an immortal god unless the father <note>Jupiter</note> had moved the small lips of his sleeping son to the breasts of his wife <note>Juno</note>: She, as soon as she woke, and at once terrified, rising up, pulled back her moist breasts from the tender mouth. Indignant, as the falling liquid—rushing through the curves and revolving upon itself—then stained the pavement and the floors of heaven with white spots. From this arises the forked path of swan-like color, drawn through equal parts with a middle division, which welds the blue axis of the sky as if with silver: An axis which, when it tired the neck of Atlas, rested as a weight no longer heavy on the Herculean neck; and after so many labors, and the spirits of the underworld subdued, he now adorns a great part of it with his triumphs; rising up against the Serpent <note>the constellation Draco</note>, the guardian of the rich garden of the Hesperides, pressing down with his foot; and behind him, caring nothing for the terrible open jaws of the Lion <note>the constellation Leo</note>; and the Crab <note>Cancer</note> that dared to aid the Lernean Hydra; monsters that continuously testify to a stepmother’s wrath and the fruitlessly raging hatred of the unjust Juno.

From here he recounts another stepmother who acted with deceit <note>Ino, who plotted against her stepchildren</note>; and of Helle, who was far too afraid of the sea she had to cross: how she was received into your chambers, Neptune: and he leads the flock of Phrixus <note>Aries, the Ram with the Golden Fleece</note>, and the Argo pregnant with heroes, to the waves of the Phasis and to the heavens with his song.

Nor is he silent about the rewards of Europa’s carrier <note>the bull, Taurus</note>; or you, Myrtilus, the driver of the chariot of the perjured Pelops. You had previously marked the Myrtoan sea with your falling death; now the horns of the Bull lift you on high.

Not far from these, he notes the Hyades blazing: but what are the Hyades to the Greeks, the Latins call the *Suculae* <note>the "Little Sows"</note>; and he notes that the two Bears <note>Ursa Major and Minor</note> have changed into the Seven Oxen <note>original: *Trionibus*, the source of the word "Septentrio" for North</note>; with the Bear-watcher <note>Arctophylax, also known as Boötes</note>, a slow Herdsman, following his wagons; for in the ancient custom they used to call a plowing ox a *trio*,

<meta>catchword: e</meta>

<summary>This page narrates the mythological origins of the Milky Way, created from Juno's spilled milk, and describes constellations associated with the labors of Hercules and the voyage of the Argonauts. It also explores the etymology of Roman star names, such as the transformation of the "Bears" into "Plowing Oxen."</summary>

# DAPHNIS, AN ECLOGUE.

Pitying this lot so deeply, he sighs;
and he addresses the cattle-kind <note>original: *bucerium genus*, literally the "horn-ox race"</note> with a futile lament:
“Ah, unhappy beasts, my herd! There were ages 
when you did not groan under the heavy weight of the plow, 
nor did the sailor call the stars by your names. 
In those days, the pious Virgin <note>The constellation Virgo, often identified with Astraea, the goddess of Justice who was the last immortal to leave earth</note> had not yet left the lands; 
she who carries the ear of grain <note>original: *spicum*, referring to the star Spica</note> in her hand. 
Perhaps she is Erigone, the daughter of Icarius <note>original: *Icariotis*</note>, whose faithful little dog <note>original: *Canicula*, referring to the dog Maera</note> 
showed her where her father lay slain by a shameful murder; 
and she, the companion of her mistress, was found by the lesser 
stars as a companion to the master Orion <note>original: *Oarioni*</note> among the greater. 
Or perhaps she is the child born of the blood of Astraeus <note>A Titan and father of the stars</note>, 
who brought an eternal name to the stars from her own father. 
Or she might be the most just offspring of ancient Themis <note>Goddess of divine law and order</note>, 
who turned away, burdened by the sight of you under the yoke, 
while the better times were driven out and the Age of Bronze arose. 
Or perhaps she is bountiful Ceres <note>Goddess of agriculture and the harvest</note>; who fled in horror, 
unwilling to see you also being slaughtered; for the Iron Race, 
enemies of the gods themselves, leaves no evil unattempted. 
As if it were not enough to have driven the gods from the earth 
(for you gods once dwelled there too), they even tried to drive them from heaven.” 
Then he curses the Giants supported by serpents <note>referring to the *Anguipedes*, giants with snakes for legs</note>: 
Porphyrion, terrifying all things with his threatening stance; 
and Rhoecus; and the monstrous Gyges, and the mighty Mimas; 
and Enceladus; and Aegeon, who brandished a hundred hands; 
and Typhon the Dire, whom no one could equal in ferocity. 
These dared to attack the gods who had been driven from the earth, 
and filled the whole sky with a great tumult, 
fiercely hurling uprooted mountain-ash trees from every side, 
cast from the heaps of mountains they had piled up. 
He adds how the gods sought to deceive the earth-born youths 
as they broke into the holy inner chambers of the heavens, 
using false faces; and how the gods were scattered in fear, 
until, by the waters of the wide-flooding Nile, 
Pan took on the terrifying shape of the Goat-Horned One <note>original: *Aegocerotis*, the constellation Capricorn</note>; 
and with a strange cry, he encouraged the gods to take up arms, 
and forced all the snake-footed ones to turn their backs in fear. 
He tells how the Donkeys <note>The stars Asellus Borealis and Asellus Australis in the constellation Cancer</note>, who were to be rewarded with a place in heaven, 
increased the terror with the huddle of their voices and their earth-shaking, crashing bray.

<summary>This page features a poetic lament for the labor of oxen, linking their plight to the departure of the Goddess of Justice (Virgo) and the violent uprising of the Giants against the Olympian gods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mythological and astronomical catalog within Hadrian Vallius’s pastoral poem "Daphnis." Following the discussion of the constellation Virgo and the Age of Bronze, the narrative now shifts to the Gigantomachy (the war between the Gods and the Giants) and the origins of various constellations, eventually honoring the history of astronomy leading up to the French Bourbon dynasty.</meta>

# DAPHNIS, AN ECLOGUE.

For he told how, in that storm of war, the Satyrs and the troop of Sileni <note>Wild, drunken forest spirits who followed Dionysus</note> were an aid to the sky-dwellers. They came riding upon little donkeys with Bacchic howls, armed with ivy-wrapped staffs <note>original: *thyrsis*</note> and shielded by the rinds of gourds <note>original: *colocynthide parma*</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Meanwhile, he tells how little winged Cupid <note>The god of love</note> came with his mother, fleeing to the waves of the Assyrian Euphrates; and how they took on the forms of the scaly flock—spirits later sacred to the Syrian people. Now they are the golden lights of the twin Pisces <note>The constellation of the Fish</note>, joined through the star-bearing circle to Capricorn, unless Aquarius separates them in the middle with his poured-out Urn.
    He relates as well the rains of Deucalion <note>The Greek "Noah" who survived a great flood; his constellation is often identified with Aquarius</note>, and how many cups of nectar Ganymede <note>The cupbearer to the gods</note> pours out; or whether that figure is Cecrops <note>The mythical first king of Athens</note>, distinguished by the sacred robe of Athena; or perhaps the shepherd Aristaeus <note>The god of beekeeping</note> carrying his full beehives, beneath which you bees fly in a dense swarm.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He sings how the curved Dolphin carried Arion <note>A poet saved from drowning by a music-loving dolphin</note>, and tells of Perseus, the son of Danae, carried through the air in another way; he sings of Cepheus, and Andromeda, and mournful Cassiopeia <note>Figures from the myth of Perseus, all placed among the stars</note>, and the vast, gaping maw of the Sea-Monster <note>original: *Pistricis*, referring to the constellation Cetus</note> set into the pole. Near this, the river Eridanus—made famous by Phaëthon <note>Who fell into this river after losing control of the Sun's chariot</note>—shines in a long, winding curve, closest to the sloping South.
    Recently, near the axis of the hidden pole, the Dutch <note>original: *Batavos*</note> saw new images of scaly creatures <note>A reference to the Southern constellations mapped by Dutch navigators like Pieter Dirkszoon Keyser in the late 16th century</note> shimmering there: the skillful Dutch, whether their task is to hunt fish in the deep whirlpool or to hunt stars in the high heavens.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then he sings how Daphnis saw Urania <note>The Muse of Astronomy</note> teaching beneath a sacred rock: how the clear-voiced woods and the hollow mountains learned to repeat her songs. He tells how ancient Chaldea <note>An ancient region of Mesopotamia famous for early astronomy</note> and the Babylonian crowds stood around as listeners, captured by the wonderful sweetness; then those whom the Greek land produced, or the land of the Nile [Egypt], those from Italy, and beautiful Rome with her Caesar.
    Afterward came men from the Arabian stock and the Spanish ruler; and finally those whom Germany sent, experts in the stars and the sky, who are themselves stars of their own land. Nor is France <note>original: *Gallia*</note> found wanting, inferior to none; France, proud in the splendor of her magnanimous King, for whom the high heavens bring forth the Bourbon fires <note>A tribute to the French Royal house, suggesting their glory is written in the stars</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then he sings of the love with which the Goddess embraced Daphnis, and Daphnis the Goddess—a love of which even the rocks of Mount Latmos <note>The site where the Moon goddess Selene visited her lover Endymion</note> were not so deeply aware.

<meta>This page continues the astronomical and mythological celebration within Hadrian Vallius’s pastoral poem. Having previously discussed the constellations and early ages of man, the poet now transitions to the modern era of scientific discovery, specifically honoring the invention of the telescope and the pendulum clock, personifying the great Dutch scientist Christiaan Huygens as the shepherd "Daphnis."</meta>

# DAPHNIS, AN ECLOGUE.

And he told how Conon <note>Conon of Samos (c. 280–220 BC), a Greek astronomer and mathematician</note> gave the youth a rod <note>original: *radium*; here referring to an astronomical instrument or staff</note>, adorned on both sides with much gold and transparent crystals; through which, whatever you look at becomes close and increases in size. And he said: "Behold, what a gift the clever Dutchman <note>Referring to the invention of the telescope in the Netherlands (c. 1608) by Hans Lippershey or Jacob Metius</note> first prepared; but with which the Tuscan <note>original: *Tyrrhenus*; referring to Galileo Galilei</note>, the glory of the Etruscan Arno, being first in the city of Antenor <note>Padua, legendary home of the Trojan prince Antenor, where Galileo made his first observations</note>, measured out the kingdoms of Jove <note>Jupiter</note>, bringing from the heavens news of miracles never known to you before: the mountains of the Moon; the three faces <note>phases</note> shown to you, O Phosphorus <note>The Morning Star, or Venus</note>; or with what satellite-escort the star of the king of the Gods [Jupiter] wanders through the blue temples above in the glimmering night. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You, too, bring forth miracles not known before: a more skillful use of this instrument was reserved for you; and you shall reveal every secret of Chronos <note>Saturn</note> to mortals. Receive this gift, once desired by us in vain." 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At the pleasing name of Daphnis <note>The pastoral pseudonym for the scientist Christiaan Huygens (1629–1695)</note>, the eager boys leap in a swift dance; nor does he himself proceed more slowly. He describes how the star of Saturn—previously believed in vain to be an oblong disk with such varying shapes—was now revealed to have a smooth ring <note>original: *annulus*</note> shining outside the smooth globe, with the blackness of space separating them both within; and how a little star <note>Titan, Saturn's largest moon, discovered by Huygens in 1655</note> wanders around them in a small circle. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;All these things Daphnis brought forth, relying on a divine gift and made known through a new art. And he adds that Saturn, charmed by these merits, allowed him to enter into his great inner sanctuaries. Here, Daphnis surveyed everything with his eyes; and from there he brought forth the subtle invention for cutting time <note>The pendulum clock, patented by Huygens in 1656</note>, by which he divides the smallest parts and moments of the hour, suspending gentle swinging weights <note>original: *oscilla*; the pendulum</note> from a thin thread. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is this device that directs the labyrinthine courses of the ship <note>original: *alni*; literally "alder-wood," a poetic term for a boat</note>, and does not allow the master of the vessel to be ignorant of his way <note>A reference to the "Longitude Problem," which Huygens hoped to solve using accurate sea-clocks</note>. For the shipmaster, no daily authority could promise with such certainty how high the Sun <note>original: *Titan*</note> stands above the Ocean, or at what shores he rises or sets at that same moment; but those taught by the excellent endeavor of Daphnis shall know these things. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He sings: the chorus shakes their arms in rhythm and beats the ground with alternating feet. Meanwhile, the merry tunnies <note>Tuna fish</note> balanced themselves with a leap above the damp seas.

<summary>This page celebrates the astronomical breakthroughs of the 17th century, specifically the telescope's refinement by Galileo and the discovery of Saturn's rings and the pendulum clock by Christiaan Huygens (Daphnis).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the pastoral poem "Daphnis" by Hadrian Vallius, moving from the description of the telescope’s invention to the appearance of a comet in 1664–1665. The poem addresses Christiaan Huygens directly, blending classical mythology with the era's cutting-edge astronomy.</meta>

->In the year 1665<-

# DAPHNIS, AN ECLOGUE.

It is believed that the long-eared folk of the hares then willingly left their homes among the holm-oaks and the beloved quiet of the nearby grove; it is told that never at any time did the rabbit, that nibbler of sedge-grass, come forth more frequently from his caves—if the girls of the coast tell the truth, who happened to be sitting here and there guarding their nets spread out to dry in the winds. The Nereids <note>Sea nymphs in Greek mythology, daughters of Nereus</note> with naked breasts, a playful company, were seen (or thought to have been seen) through the flickering Moonlight, along with Triton and Glaucus <note>Deities of the sea</note>, far off under the faint light; and you, too, O most faithful guardian of Neptune’s herds <note>Referring to Proteus, the prophetic old man of the sea who tends to Poseidon's seals</note>, reclining near the seals stretched out along the shores. Nor did anyone remember to yield to the late night. 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Meanwhile, the shadows thicken; and while Cynthia <note>The Moon</note>, hidden by a cloud, remains concealed, a star with unusual hair <note>original: *non solitis crinibus astrum*; a literal translation of the Greek *kometes* (long-haired), referring to the Great Comet of 1664-1665</note> stepped forth from a clear part of the sky, trailing a long train <note>original: *syrma*; a term for a tragic actor's trailing robe, here describing the comet's tail</note> notable for its whiteness. The astonished choir marvels at it; and he [Daphnis] marvels as well, especially when the Moon, soon returned, took away such great honor from its head and all its following ornament. 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Then spoke Ancon: "Return, each of you, to your own familiar huts <note>original: *mapalia*; simple dwellings or cottages</note>, not at all troubled by this omen, nor nursing any anxiety. At another time, we shall sing of such unusual fires and of the common folk trembling in vain with fear." 

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;These were the words of Ancon: which seemed to me worthy to be reported to you, O HUYGENS <note>original: *Hugenide*; a patronymic form addressing Christiaan Huygens</note>, glory of the Huygens family, to whom the stars are a care; nor is it right for you to despise Phoebus Apollo or the goddesses of Pimpla <note>The Muses, associated with the Pimplaean spring</note>, to whom your whole house, your brothers, and your father are dedicated. Thus, let the face of this foreign star not terrify you, whether it be this same comet or another with a different brightness.

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a Latin eclogue celebrating Christiaan Huygens’ astronomical observations. This page transitions from the poetic front-matter to the formal legal authority of the book: the "Privilege du Roy" (Royal Privilege), which served as an early form of copyright protection in 17th-century France.</meta>

### ->ROYAL PRIVILEGE.<-

LOUIS, by the grace of God King of France and Navarre; To our beloved and loyal Counselors, the members of our Courts of Parliament, Masters of Requests <term>Maistres des Requestes: high-ranking judicial officers who handled petitions to the King</term> ordinary of our Household, Bailiffs, Seneschals, Provosts, their Lieutenants, and all other Justices and Officers to whom it shall appertain, Greetings. Our dear and well-beloved FRANÇOIS MUGUET, our ordinary Printer, has very humbly represented to Us that there has been placed in his hands a Book entitled, *The Pendulum Clock, or Geometrical Demonstrations Concerning the Motion of Pendulums as Applied to Clocks, by Christiaan Huygens of Zulichem, son of Constantijn* <note>original: *Christiani Hugenii Zulichemii Const. F. Horologium Oscillatorium, sive de motu Pendulorum ad horologia aptato demonstrationes Geometricæ*</note>, which he desires to give to the public if it should please us to grant him permission, for which he humbly petitions. FOR THESE REASONS, wishing to treat the Petitioner favorably, We have permitted and granted, and by these presents do permit and grant him to print or cause to be printed the said Book in such form, typeface, volume, and as many times as shall seem good to him, during the time of six full and consecutive years, beginning from the day it is finished printing for the first time; strictly forbidding all persons of whatever quality and condition they may be, to print or cause it to be printed, sold, or distributed during the said time in any place in our Kingdom, without the consent of the Petitioner, or those who shall have rights from him, under any pretext whatsoever, upon penalty of fifteen hundred livres <term>livre: the currency of France at the time</term> in fine, applicable one third to Us, one third to the General Hospital of our city of Paris, and the other third to the Petitioner, as well as the confiscation of the counterfeit copies, and all costs, damages, and interests; on the condition that two copies shall be placed in our ordinary Library, one in that of the cabinet of our Louvre, and another in that of our beloved and loyal Keeper of the Seals, the Lord Daligre. ACCORDINGLY, we command you that you allow the Petitioner and those who shall have rights from him to enjoy and use the contents of these presents fully and peacefully, ceasing and causing to cease all troubles and hindrances to the contrary; it being our will that by inserting these presents or an extract of them in each copy, they shall be held as well and duly notified; We command our first Usher or Sergeant required for this purpose, to carry out all necessary acts for the execution of these presents. FOR such is our pleasure. GIVEN at Versailles the last day of September in the year of grace one thousand six hundred seventy-two. And of our Reign the thirtieth. Signed, LOUIS. By the King, COLBERT <note>Jean-Baptiste Colbert was the powerful First Minister of State to Louis XIV and a great patron of the sciences.</note>.

*Registered in the Book of the Community of Booksellers and Printers of Paris, the 4th of November 1672, following the Decree of Parliament of the 8th of April 1653, and that of the Private Council of the King of the 27th of February one thousand six hundred sixty-five. Signed,* D. THIERRY, *Syndic* <term>Syndic: an elected official representing the guild of printers and booksellers</term>.

Finished printing for the first time the first day of April 1673.

->*The Copies have been provided.*<-

It outlines the legal protections, penalties for piracy, and the requirement to deposit copies in royal libraries.</summary>

<sig>A</sig>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut headpiece featuring a central basket filled with flowers and fruit, flanked by symmetrical scrolling acanthus leaves, smaller flowers, and birds.</image-desc>

# BY CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS
## OF ZULICHEM, SON OF CONSTANTIJN <note>original: "Const. F." (Constantini Filius). Huygens’ father, Constantijn, was a celebrated diplomat and poet.</note>

# THE PENDULUM 
# CLOCK,

->OR<-

### ON THE MOTION OF PENDULUMS
### APPLIED TO CLOCKS

->*Geometrical Demonstrations.*<-

---

<image-desc>Large historiated woodcut initial 'A' with a floral and architectural background.</image-desc>

**I**t is now the sixteenth year since I first made public the construction of clocks—then recently invented by me—by publishing a small book <note>Huygens refers to his 1658 work, *Horologium*.</note>. Since that time, however, I have made many discoveries pertaining to the perfection of this work, and it seemed appropriate to set them all forth individually in this volume. Indeed, these additions are so essential to the perfection of the invention that they may be considered its most important part, providing the very foundation of this branch of mechanics which was previously lacking. 

For a reliable and uniform measure of time is not naturally inherent in a simple pendulum, as it is observed that wider swings are slower than narrower ones <note>This phenomenon is known as "circular error." A simple pendulum only remains regular if its swings are kept very small.</note>. But with geometry as my guide, I have discovered a different and previously unknown method of suspending the pendulum. This was achieved by observing the curvature of a certain line <term>the cycloid: a curve traced by a point on the rim of a wheel as it rolls along a straight line; Huygens discovered that a pendulum swinging in this shape is perfectly regular regardless of the width of the swing</term>, which is matched to the pendulum in a truly marvelous way to achieve the desired uniformity.

<meta>catchword: Than</meta>

<summary>This is the title page and opening text of Christiaan Huygens' seminal 1673 work on the pendulum clock. He introduces his discovery of the cycloidal path, which allows a pendulum to keep perfect time regardless of how far it swings.</summary>

<keywords>Christiaan Huygens, Horologium Oscillatorium, pendulum clock, geometry, cycloid, isochronism, mechanics, timekeeping</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with Huygens describing how he discovered a new method of suspending a pendulum by observing the mathematical properties of a specific curve. This page continues that explanation, identifying the curve as the cycloid and outlining the contents of the book, including the theory of evolutes and centers of oscillation.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>BY CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS</header>

...we applied to clocks, the motion of which turned out to be so constant and certain that, after numerous experiments conducted on both land and sea, it is now manifest that they offer the greatest assistance to the study of Astronomy and the art of Navigation. This is the very line which a nail, fixed into the circumference of a rolling wheel, describes in the air through a continuous revolution; it was given the name ***cycloid*** <note>From the Greek *kykloeides*, meaning "circle-like." It is the path traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as the wheel rolls along a straight line without slipping.</note> by the Geometers of our age, and has been diligently examined for many of its other properties. We, however, have discovered a property inherent in it—of which no one had previously suspected—regarding the capacity for measuring time, which we found while following in the footsteps of the mechanical arts. Since we made this known some time ago to friends who understand these matters (for it was noticed not long after the first publication on the clock <note>Huygens refers to his earlier, shorter work, *Horologium* (1658).</note>), we now present the same to be read by all, confirmed by a demonstration as accurate as we could manage. Therefore, the most significant part of this book will be occupied with providing this demonstration. In this regard, it was first necessary to establish and further advance the doctrine of that greatest of men, ***Galileo***, concerning the descent of falling bodies, with some new demonstrations; the most desired fruit and, as it were, the highest peak of this doctrine is this very property of the ***cycloid*** which we have found.

Furthermore, so that this might be adapted for the use of pendulums, a new consideration of curved lines had to be employed—specifically, those which generate other curves by their *evolution* <note>Huygens is introducing his theory of "evolutes" and "invoutes." If you "unwrap" a string from a specific curve (the evolute), the end of the string describes a new curve (the involute). He proved that the evolute of a cycloid is another cycloid.</note>. From this arises a comparison between the length of curves and straight lines, which I have pursued further than immediate necessity required, on account of the elegance and novelty of the theory, as it seemed to me.

Moreover, to explain the nature of the ***Compound Pendulum*** <note>Unlike a "simple" pendulum (a weight on a massless string), a compound pendulum is a rigid body swinging about a pivot, where the weight is distributed throughout.</note>, the utility of which I demonstrate in the construction of these ***automata*** <note>Here used in the classical sense of "self-moving machines" or mechanical clocks.</note>, I had to add an investigation of the ***Centers of Oscillation***. This has been attempted by many before, but with less success; in this section, many theorems will be found which are, unless I am mistaken, worthy of notice, pertaining to linear, plane, and solid figures. Before all of this, however, I have placed the mechanical construction of the clock itself and the application of the pendulum, in the form found most suitable for astronomical uses, according to the pattern of which all others may easily be organized, with whatever changes are necessary.

But because it happened, as usually occurs with the remarkable success of such an invention—and as I had predicted would happen—that many desired to be called its authors themselves, or, if not for themselves, wished the honor to be attributed to someone of their own nation rather than to us, I must finally here confront their unjust attempts...

<summary>Huygens introduces the cycloid as the mathematical key to perfect timekeeping, acknowledging Galileo's influence while outlining his own original theories on "evolutes" and compound pendulums. He also notes that he is publishing this full account partly to defend his priority as the inventor against rival claims.</summary>
<keywords>cycloid, Galileo Galilei, pendulum clock, navigation, astronomy, evolution of curves, center of oscillation, compound pendulum, scientific priority</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with Huygens describing his discovery of the cycloidal curve and its application to pendulums, asserting that this mathematical breakthrough allows for a more constant and accurate measurement of time than previously possible.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>THE PENDULUM CLOCK</header>

I believe. Indeed, there would be little need to offer anything against them <note>Huygens is addressing rival claimants who argued they had invented the pendulum clock first.</note> except for this one fact: that sixteen years ago <note>Counting back from the 1673 publication of this book to his first invention in 1657.</note>, when there was no mention or rumor at all of such clocks made by anyone in speech or writing (I am speaking of the use of the **simple pendulum** adapted for clocks, for I believe no one will dispute the addition of the **Cycloid**), I discovered their construction through my own reflection and saw that it was brought to completion. In the following year, which was 1658, I published the drawing and description of the machine in print, and I sent copies of both the work itself and the booklet <note>The 1658 work titled *Horologium*.</note> in every direction. 

Since these facts are so well known to everyone that they do not even require the testimony of scholars or the official records of the **States of Holland** <note>original: *Bataviæ Ordinum actis*; the governing body that granted Huygens his patent.</note> (though they could be confirmed by them), it is easily apparent what should be thought of those who, seven years later, advertised the same construction in their books as if it had proceeded from themselves or their friends. 

Indeed, those who attempt to grant the first honors here to **Galileo** <note>Galileo Galilei (1564–1642) discovered the principle of the pendulum but never completed a functioning pendulum clock.</note> seem to detract from his praise more than mine if they say he attempted the invention but did not perfect it; for I am the one who investigated the same matter with a more successful outcome than he. However, when they contend that it was brought to a conclusion by Galileo himself or by his son (as a certain learned man recently claimed), and that such clocks were actually displayed, I do not know how they expect to be believed. It is hardly likely that such a useful invention could have remained unknown for eight whole years until it was brought to light by me. 

If they say it was intentionally kept hidden, they must realize that this same excuse could be offered by anyone else who wishes to claim the origin of an invention for themselves. Therefore, it would certainly have to be proven; and yet even then, it would not concern me at all, unless it were also shown that what was hidden from everyone else was somehow known to me alone. These things had to be said for the sake of a necessary defense. Now, let us proceed to the construction of the machine itself.

->[Image: A decorative woodcut tailpiece depicting a basket filled with various fruits, including grapes and apples, with foliage.]<-

<summary>Huygens defends his intellectual priority over the invention of the pendulum clock, dismissing claims that Galileo or others had perfected the device before his 1658 publication.</summary>

<header>THE PENDULUM CLOCK</header>

-><term>Center of oscillation<note>original: "Centr. osc."; the point in a pendulum where the entire mass could be concentrated without changing the period of the swing.</note></term><-
-><term>Center of gravity<note>original: "Centr. gr."; the point at which the weight of the pendulum is perfectly balanced.</note></term> 1 2 3'<-

I believe. Indeed, there would be little need to offer anything against them <note>Huygens is addressing rival claimants—specifically those associated with the Royal Society or Italian makers—who argued they had invented the pendulum clock first.</note> except for this one fact: that sixteen years ago <note>Counting back from the 1673 publication of this book to his first invention in 1657.</note>, when there was no mention or rumor at all of such clocks made by anyone in speech or writing (I am speaking of the use of the **simple pendulum** adapted for clocks, for I believe no one will dispute the addition of the **Cycloid** <note>A mathematical curve that ensures the pendulum takes the same amount of time to swing regardless of the width of the arc.</note>), I discovered their construction through my own reflection and saw that it was brought to completion. In the following year, which was 1658, I published the drawing and description of the machine in print, and I sent copies of both the work itself and the booklet <note>The 1658 work titled *Horologium*.</note> in every direction. 

Since these facts are so well known to everyone that they do not even require the testimony of scholars or the official records of the **States of Holland** <note>original: *Bataviæ Ordinum actis*; the governing body of the Dutch Republic that granted Huygens his patent.</note> (though they could be confirmed by them), it is easily apparent what should be thought of those who, seven years later, advertised the same construction in their books as if it had proceeded from themselves or their friends. 

Indeed, those who attempt to grant the first honors here to **Galileo** <note>Galileo Galilei (1564–1642) discovered the principle of isochronism in pendulums but died before successfully building a fully functional pendulum clock.</note> seem to detract from his praise more than mine if they say he attempted the invention [but failed to bring it to fruition].

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
## PART ONE,
### *Containing its description.*

The accompanying **FIGURE** <note>Huygens refers to Figure I shown on the previous pages, illustrating the internal gears.</note> provides a view of the clock from the side. First, there are two plates, **A A** and **B B**, about half a foot or slightly more in length and two and a half inches wide; their four corners are joined by small pillars so that the plates stand an inch and a half apart. The axles of the principal wheels are inserted into these plates on both sides. The first and lowest wheel is marked **C**, cut with 80 teeth. A small disc **D** is also fixed to its axle, roughened with iron spikes to grip the cord and its attached weights—the manner in which these are arranged will be explained later.

By the force of the weight, then, wheel **C** is turned. This moves the adjacent <term>pinion<note>original: "tympanum"; a small gear wheel, often shaped like a drum or lantern, that engages with a larger wheel.</note></term> **E** of eight teeth, and simultaneously wheel **F**, which is fixed to the same axle and has 48 teeth. This wheel is followed by another pinion **G**, and on the same axle, wheel **H**; these have the same number of teeth as the preceding pinion and wheel <note>i.e., 8 and 48 teeth respectively</note>.

But this wheel is of the type that our craftsmen call <term>crown wheels<note>original: "coronarias"; a wheel with teeth protruding at a right angle from its face, used to change the axis of rotation from horizontal to vertical.</note></term> because of their shape. By its teeth, pinion **I** is driven, along with wheel **K**, which is held on the same axle, set vertically. The pinion has 24 teeth; the wheel has 15, and these are cut like the teeth of a saw. Above the middle of wheel **K** lies a transverse <term>verge<note>original: "axis pinnatus"; the horizontal axle with two pallets that regulates the rotation of the escape wheel.</note></term> **L M**, the ends of which are supported on either side by the <term>brackets<note>original: "gnomones"; structural supports or mounts for the axles.</note></term> **N Q** and **P**, which are attached separately to plate **B B**.

One should note, however, the part **Q** on bracket **N Q** which protrudes downward; it opens with an oblong hole to pass the axle **L M**, and at the same time it holds the axle that we said was shared by wheel **K** and pinion **I**, with its lower part resting on bracket **N**. In plate **B B**, a large hole is hollowed out through which the verge **L M** extends beyond it. This axle, inserted with a fine point into bracket **P**, moves more freely than if it were supported by plate **B B** itself while also protruding beyond it. For it must necessarily protrude so that the <term>crank<note>original: "clavula"; the small arm or pin that connects the verge to the pendulum rod, transmitting the "tick-tock" motion.</note></term> **S** can be attached to it, to rotate in unison with it.

This motion is reciprocal—moving now in this direction, now in that—as the teeth of wheel **K** alternately encounter the <term>pallets<note>original: "pinnulis"; the two small flags on the verge axle that catch and release the escape wheel teeth.</note></term> **L L**, according to a commonly known method <note>This refers to the standard verge escapement used in earlier clocks, but here adapted for a pendulum.</note> which therefore requires no further detailed explanation.

Furthermore, the small pin *s*, which is bent at its lower end and pierced with an oblong hole, embraces the iron rod of the pendulum to which the lead weight *x* is attached. This rod is suspended at the top by a double thread between two small plates—only one of which, *τ* <note>The Greek letter tau</note>, is visible here. For this reason, we have provided another drawing nearby to illustrate the shape and bend of both plates and the entire method of suspending the pendulum. However, we shall discuss the true curvature of these plates in greater detail later. <note>Huygens is referring to his discovery of the cycloidal curve, which ensures the pendulum swings in equal time regardless of the width of the arc.</note>

But now, to speak of the motion of the clock—for we will address the remaining parts of the illustration afterward—it is quite clear that the motion of the pendulum *v x* is sustained by the force of the wheels (which are pulled by the weight) once it has been set in motion by hand. Simultaneously, the steady swings of the pendulum prescribe the law and standard of movement for all the wheels, and indeed for the entire clock. For the pin, driven by even a slight impulse from the wheels, not only yields to the pulling pendulum but also assists its motion slightly with each swing. This makes the motion perpetual; otherwise, the swing would fail little by little of its own accord—or more accurately, due to the resistance of the air—and would eventually come to rest. On the other hand, since the nature of a pendulum is such that it always moves at the same pace and cannot be altered in any way except by changing its length, it follows that once we have achieved that equality through the bend of the plates between which it is suspended, the wheel *κ* <note>The Greek letter kappa; specifically the escape wheel</note> is by no means permitted to turn now faster and now slower, even if it often attempts to do so (as happens in common clocks). Instead, each of its teeth is necessarily forced to pass in equal intervals of time. From this it is manifest that even and steady revolutions of the preceding wheels—and finally of the hands themselves—are achieved, since everything moves proportionally. Therefore, if there should be any defect in the construction, or if the axles of the wheels should turn with more difficulty due to a change in the air's temperature, no inequality or slowing of motion need be feared, provided it is not so severe that the entire motion of the clock is interrupted. It will always either measure time correctly or not measure it at all.

Furthermore, the hands <note>original: "indices"</note> are turned and arranged in this manner. A third plate, *γ γ* <note>The Greek letter gamma</note>, sits parallel to the previous ones, a quarter of an inch distant from the plate marked **A A**. Upon it, the hour circles are described, sharing the same center *x* as the axis of wheel *c* extends through. Of these circles, the interior one contains the division of twelve hours, and the other contains 60 <term>minutes<note>original: "scrupulorum"; in early horology, "scrupula" (scruples) referred to the first small divisions of an hour.</note></term>. To the axis of wheel *c*, beyond plate **A A**, is fitted wheel *β* <note>The Greek letter beta</note>, connected to a tube that continues as far as *E* through plate *γ γ*; and thus

...rests upon that axis so that it is carried around with it; however, it can be turned independently when necessary <note>This refers to the ability to set the clock's time manually without forcing the gears.</note>. At point *E*, an index <note>A clock hand</note> is placed, which will complete its circuit in the space of an hour and thus demonstrate the "first scruples," or sixty parts of the hour <note>original: "scrupula prima" — these are what we now call minutes</note>. Moreover, the wheel we previously called *β* <note>The Greek letter beta</note> drives another wheel with the same number of teeth as itself, and with it, a pinion <note>original: "tympanum," a small cogwheel</note> with six teeth is attached to their common small axis, supported on one side by plate *A* and on the other by the gnomon *f*. Finally, wheel *ζ* <note>The Greek letter zeta</note> is moved by this pinion; it has 72 teeth and an attached tube which itself extends beyond plate *γ* <note>The Greek letter gamma</note> to point *θ* <note>The Greek letter theta</note>, ending just before the tube of wheel *β*, which it encloses within itself. At the far end *θ*, the hour hand is attached, being somewhat shorter than the one we said marks the minutes, as it must be carried within an inner circle. To ensure the "second scruples" <note>original: "scrupula secunda" — our modern seconds</note> are shown without error, a disk *λ* <note>The Greek letter lambda</note> is placed on the axis of wheel *H*, which extends to plate *γ*; on this disk is inscribed a circle divided into sixty parts. Through a hole cut in plate *γ* at point *z*, these divisions are noted as they pass by a pointer fixed at the top of the hole. This entire arrangement of hands and hour-circles can be seen more clearly in the smaller figure, which shows the exterior form of the clock.

Furthermore, the length of the pendulum, with the wheels arranged as we have described, must be such that it measures seconds by each of its swings. This length is three feet, and since it could not be conveniently shown in the diagram, we have depicted only its fifth part, from the top suspension where the bend of the plate *τ* <note>The Greek letter tau</note> begins, down to the center of weight *x*. I say three feet, not with respect to any foot in use among this or that European nation, but according to a certain and eternal module of a foot taken from the very length of this pendulum, which may henceforth be called the **HORARY FOOT** <note>original: "PEDEM HORARIUM." Huygens is proposing a universal standard of length based on the physics of a pendulum that takes exactly one second to swing.</note>. Indeed, the measurements of all other feet which we wish to pass down to posterity uncorrupted should be referred to this standard. For example, the measure of the Parisian foot will never be unknown to future ages as long as it is established that its ratio to the Horary Foot is as 864 to 881. But we shall deal with the most exact constitution of this measure at greater length in the section concerning the Center of Oscillation. For now, we shall briefly designate the times of rotation for each wheel and hand, so it may be understood that everything fits correctly with the number of teeth described above.

Therefore, in a single rotation of wheel *c*, wheel *f* will be seen to go around ten times, wheel *H* sixty times, and the topmost wheel *m-κ* <note>The Greek letter kappa</note> one hundred and twenty times. Since this topmost wheel has fifteen teeth, and they are struck alternately by the pin-

...of the pins <note>original: "nulae"</note> *L L*, with one single revolution of the wheel *κ* <note>The Greek letter kappa; the crown wheel or escapement wheel</note>, 30 beats will be counted, which correspond to the same number of swings back and forth of the pendulum *v x*. Therefore, in 120 revolutions, there will be 3600 simple oscillations, a number which is equal to the "second scruples" <note>original: "scrupula secunda" — our modern seconds</note> that make up one hour. Thus, in the space of one hour, wheel *c* completes one circuit, and with it the hand placed at *E*, which demonstrates the "first scruples" <note>original: "scrupula prima" — our modern minutes</note>. And since in that same span of time wheel *β* <note>The Greek letter beta</note>—and through it wheel *γ* <note>The Greek letter gamma</note>—is also turned, it carries a small pinion <note>original: "tympanidio" — a small gear or lantern pinion</note> of six teeth. Because the number of teeth on wheel *ζ* <note>The Greek letter zeta</note> is twelve times this number <note>i.e., 72 teeth</note>, it is clear that it takes twelve hours for this wheel to be led around, as well as the hour hand joined to it at *θ* <note>The Greek letter theta</note>. Finally, since we have shown that sixty revolutions of wheel *H* correspond to each single revolution of wheel *c*, it follows that wheel *H*, along with the attached disk *λ* <note>The Greek letter lambda</note>, will be carried around sixty times every hour; that is, once in the time of one minute. Therefore, the sixty divisions of the small disk *λ* will show the seconds by their passage; and thus it is manifest that all things are correctly arranged.

The weight *x* at the bottom of the pendulum <note>original: "perpendiculo"</note> weighs three pounds; it is made entirely of lead, or of a brass shell containing lead. One must provide not only for the weight of the metal but also for its shape (for this matters a great deal) so that it feels the least possible hindrance from the resistance of the air. For this reason, it is fashioned into the shape of a horizontal, oblong cylinder, sharpened at both ends, as seen in the smaller diagram of the clock at *a*. However, in those clocks prepared for navigation at sea, the shape of an upright lens <note>A double-convex or "lenticular" shape</note> has seemed more suitable.

Furthermore, in the same diagram, the method for suspending the other weight, *b*, which keeps the clock in motion, is shown. It was necessary for us to investigate this method, which was previously unknown, so that while the weight is being pulled back up <note>i.e., during winding</note>, the course of the clock does not stop or suffer any interference, which had to be entirely avoided here. Thus, a continuous rope is prepared which returns upon itself, with its ends neatly joined together. First, it circles the small disk joined to the lowest wheel, marked *D* in the larger diagram; descending from there, one part of it passes under the pulley *c*, from which the weight *b* is hung. From here it ascends over the small disk *d*, which is attached to the outside of the clock; this disk has iron spikes around its circumference and is furthermore fitted with iron teeth so that it may be turned when the cord *e* is pulled, but can by no means be turned back in the opposite direction. From this disk, the rope descends to another pulley, *f*, from which a tiny weight *g* is hung—just enough to hold the larger weight *b* so that it does not descend except when the disk is turned. For the rope returns from pulley *f* back to the same disk *d* from whence it descended. By these means...

<sig>B</sig>

<margin>
DESCRIPTION
OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>
...it is evident that the weight *b* always attempts, by half of its own heaviness, to drive the gears of the clock. This effort does not cease even when the weight is forced to ascend by a hand pulling the rope *e*; thus, the motion of the clock is never interrupted, nor is a single moment of time lost. <note>This describes Huygens' "endless chain" invention, which allows the clock to keep running while it is being wound, a feature called "maintaining power."</note>

The precise amount of weight at *b* cannot be fixed with certainty, but the smaller the weight that suffices to maintain the motion, the better and more accurately crafted the machine <term>automaton; a self-moving machine or clockwork</term> is proved to be. In our clocks—the best we have produced thus far—this weight has been reduced to six pounds, provided that the diameter of the small pulley *D* is about one inch, as was shown; likewise, the weight of the pendulum bob <note>original: "perpendiculi"</note> is three pounds, and the length of the pendulum is three feet. This length, we should also mention, hangs down through the clock case, passing through an oblong hole as far as is necessary to complete its swings <note>original: "oscillationibus"</note>. The clock itself, when suspended at the height of a man, continues to move for 30 hours.

It remains now to describe the shape of the plates <term>lamellarum; often called "cheeks" in clockmaking</term> between which we said the pendulum is attached. The function of these plates is of the highest importance for providing a uniform motion to the clock. Without these, the oscillations of a simple pendulum will not be of equal duration (even if it has seemed otherwise to some), but those swings that travel through smaller arcs will take less time. This is first easily detected by an experiment of this kind: if two strings of the same length are taken, and equal weights are tied to the bottom, let each be suspended separately. Then, if one of them is pulled far from the perpendicular line, and the other only a little, and both are released from the hand at the same time, it will be seen that they do not travel together in the same direction for long. Instead, the one whose returns are smaller will outpace the other. <note>Huygens is describing "circular error," where a pendulum's period varies based on the width of its swing.</note>

Furthermore, the ratios of the times for any given arcs can be defined by numbers based on certain science, and indeed as close as one might wish. For example, the time of descent through a whole quadrant of a circle <note>a 90-degree swing</note> is to the time through a very small arc roughly as 34 is to 29. Therefore, this difference is by no means to be blamed on air resistance, as some have wished to believe, but arises from the very nature of the motion and the properties of the circle. This can also be concluded by another argument from the construction of the isochronous <term>isochroni; "equal-timed," meaning the swing takes the same time regardless of width</term> pendulum itself, where one departs significantly from a circular line, as will soon be made clear.

But it might perhaps seem that in these clocks of ours, where the width of the oscillations is always the same, the inequality we have mentioned would be of no importance, and thus no correction of the pendulum would be needed. This would certainly be the case if the width were exactly the same in all...
