<meta>This page transitions from the general mechanical description of the clock to the specific mathematical and geometric "remedy" for the pendulum's inaccuracy. It explains why a standard circular swing fails to keep perfect time and introduces the method for calculating pendulum lengths and constructing the cycloidal "cheeks."</meta>

# CHRISTIAN HUYGENS

<margin>
DESCRIPTION
OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>
...it would remain constant. But since it sometimes slightly exceeds or falls short, a significant error eventually builds up from many tiny differences, and this is proven both by the nature of the thing and by experiments. For although the force of the weight always remains the same relative to the gear nearest to it, yet when transmitted through so many others—no matter how carefully they have been polished—the same force does not always reach the pendulum <note>original: "perpendiculum"</note>. Besides this, the motion of the gears is made more difficult by cold; likewise, as the oil added to them evaporates or becomes fouled. But especially unequal are the oscillations in clocks carried at sea, due to the continuous tossing of the ship, so that all clocks in general, but these most of all, need a remedy <note>Huygens refers to the "circular error," where wider swings take longer than narrow ones.</note> by which the time of the pendulum's return becomes equal for both wider and narrower swings.

To define the shape of the plates <term>lamellarum; the curved "cheeks" that guide the pendulum string</term> in which this remedy is placed, one must first determine the length of the pendulum. This is easily obtained from the fact that the lengths of pendulums are to each other as the squares of the times spent on each swing. Thus, since we have defined the length of the pendulum that measures seconds <term>scrupula secunda; the "second" small division of a minute</term> as three feet, a quarter part of it, or nine inches <term>unciae; inches</term>, is required for a pendulum that marks half-seconds. Likewise, if the length of a pendulum is sought whose simple swings number 10,000 in the space of an hour, the calculation is made in this way: We know that for a three-foot pendulum, 3,600 swings are counted per hour; therefore, the individual times of these swings are greater than the times of the sought pendulum in the ratio of 10,000 to 3,600, or 25 to 9. Therefore, as the square of the number 25 is to the square of 9—that is, as 625 is to 81—so will the length of 3 feet be to the one sought, namely 4 and 66/100 inches.

Having established the length of the pendulum—let us say three feet in the clock proposed by us—the cycloid <term>Cycloïs; a mathematical curve discovered to be the secret to perfect pendulum timing</term> line, which will give the curvature of the plates, shall be described in this manner.

Upon a flat board, let a ruler *A B* be fixed, a half-finger's width in thickness. Then let a cylinder *C D E* be made of that same height, having a base diameter equal to half the length of the pendulum; and let *F G H E* be a small strip, or rather a thin plate <note>original: "bractea"</note>, fixed to the ruler at *F*, and to the cylinder at some point on the circumference *E*, so that it is partly wound around it and partly extended along the side of the ruler *A B*. Let an iron point *D I* be fixed into the cylinder, protruding slightly beyond the lower base, exactly corresponding to its circumference.

<summary>Huygens explains that friction, temperature, and motion (especially at sea) cause pendulum clocks to drift, requiring a "remedy" to ensure equal time for all swings. He then provides the mathematical ratios for determining pendulum length and begins describing the geometric construction of the cycloidal cheeks.</summary>
<keywords>isochronism, pendulum length, second, half-second, cycloid, mechanical error, timekeeping at sea, geometric construction</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of the "circular error" in standard pendulums. Having established that a simple circular swing is timing-dependent on the width of the arc, Huygens now introduces his groundbreaking mechanical solution: the cycloidal "cheeks" that modify the pendulum's path to ensure every swing, narrow or wide, takes exactly the same amount of time.</meta>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
B ij

<margin>
DESCRIPTION
OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>

Given these circumstances, if a cylinder is rolled along the ruler *A B*, with only the thickness of a thin metal strip *F G* intervening—and with that strip always kept as stretched as possible—the point *I* will describe a curved line *K I* on the underlying plane of the board, which is called a **Cycloid** <note>original: "Cyclois". A curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line.</note>. The "generating circle" will be *C D E*, the base of the cylinder used. Now, if we apply the plate *K L* to the ruler *A B*, having first traced on it a portion of the cycloid *K I*, we then invert the plate and cut a similar line *K M* on the opposite side, starting from the same point *K*. Then we shall accurately form the figure *M K I* according to those lines. The gap between the plates—between which the pendulum is suspended—must be fitted to this figure. However, small portions of the arcs *K M* and *K I* are sufficient for the use of clocks; the remaining curvature would be useless, as the pendulum string cannot reach it.

But, so that the nature and effect of this marvelous line may be more fully understood, it seemed appropriate to represent the full semi-cycloids *K M* and *K I* here in another diagram. Between these, a Pendulum *K N P* is suspended and moved; its length is twice the diameter of the generating circle. Its oscillations, of whatever width—up to the greatest of all along the arc *M P I*—will be completed in the same amounts of time. This occurs in such a way that the center of the attached sphere *P* always moves along the line *M P I*, which is itself a complete cycloid. This is a remarkable property—I do not know if it is granted to any line other than this one—namely, that it describes itself by its own **evolution** <term>evolution; the geometric "unfolding" or "unrolling" of a curve, where a string unwinding from one curve traces a second curve</term>. These things which have been stated will each be demonstrated in the following sections, where we shall treat the descent of heavy bodies and the evolution of curves.

<summary>Huygens describes how to geometrically construct cycloidal plates (or "cheeks") that, when placed at the top of a pendulum, force the weight to follow a cycloidal path rather than a circular one.</summary>
<keywords>cycloid, pendulum, isochronism, evolution of curves, geometry, clockmaking, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>Following the previous page's introduction to the mechanical concept of the cycloid, Huygens now provides a practical geometric method for draftsmen and clockmakers to plot the curve accurately using only a compass and straightedge.</meta>

# ->CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS<-

<margin>
DESCRIPTION
OF THE
CLOCK
</margin>

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a semi-circle or circle construction used to define a cycloid. A horizontal line connects points M and I. A vertical line intersects it at the center and extends to point K at the top. A curve (a cycloid) is drawn passing through points including N and P. Various points on the circular arc and the resulting curve are part of the geometric proof.</image-desc>

However, it is also possible to design the cycloid <note>original: "cycloidem"; the specific curve that allows for perfectly regular pendulum swings</note> in another way, using points found through geometry. Let a circle be drawn with diameter AB, which is equal to half the length of the pendulum <note>original: "perpendiculi"</note>. On its circumference, take any number of equal parts—AC, CD, DE, EF, AG, GH, HI, IK—and let the lines GC, HD, IE, and KF be joined; these will be parallel to one another. Next, let a straight line LM be taken equal in length to the arc AF, and let it be divided into as many equal parts as there are in arc AF. Let individual segments CN and GO on line CG be set equal to one of those parts. Then, let DP and HQ on line DH be made equal to two parts of line LM. Let ER and IS on line EI be equal to three parts, and so on if more parts were used. Finally, let FT and KV on the outermost line FK be made equal to the whole length of line LM. Now, if curves are drawn through the points AOQSV and ANPRT, these will again be the parts of the desired cycloid between which the pendulum must be suspended.

The straight line LM, which is equal to the arc AF, is found in this way: first, let XY be set equal to two straight lines that span half of arc AF <note>Huygens is using the lengths of "chords"—straight lines connecting two points on a curve—to approximate the curve itself</note>. Then, from the same starting point, let XZ be taken equal to the chord of the whole arc AF. Finally, let a third of the difference between them (YZ), marked as Z Δ, be added to the total length XZ. For this total length X Δ will be so nearly equal to the whole arc AF that, even if the arc were a sixth of the entire circumference (and no more is ever required here), it would not fail by even one six-thousandth part of its length. This was demonstrated in the work I previously wrote concerning the *Magnitude of the Circle* <note>original: "De Circuli Magnitudine"; referring to Huygens's 1654 treatise on calculating the value of Pi and arc lengths</note>.

Having explained the matters pertaining to the construction of the clock, it must now be shown how it should be adjusted to the true measurement of the hours. Therefore, first, we shall examine whether its motion is correct in the following manner.

<summary>Huygens provides a step-by-step geometric method for plotting a cycloid and explains a highly accurate mathematical approximation for measuring the length of a circular arc.</summary>
<keywords>cycloid construction, pendulum length, geometric proof, arc measurement, clock regulation, De Circuli Magnitudine</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the geometric description of how to construct the cycloidal curves for the clock's suspension. This page transitions from theoretical geometry to the practical calibration of the clock using astronomical observations.</meta>

### ->THE OSCILLATING CLOCK<-

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram is centered at the top of the page. It features a large circle with a vertical diameter labeled A at the top and B at the bottom. Several points are marked on the circumference: C, D, E, F on the right side and G, H, I, K on the left side. Four horizontal chords are drawn across the upper half of the circle, with labels N-O, P-Q, R-S, and T-V at their endpoints. Below the circle is a horizontal line segment labeled L and M at its ends, featuring several small vertical division marks. Below this scale are the labels X, a set of marks resembling triple crosses, then Y, Z, and A.
</image-desc>

<margin>
DESCRIPTION
OF THE
CLOCK
</margin>

A fixed place should be chosen for the observer's eye, from which the stars can be looked down upon, and at the same time the roofs or walls of neighboring buildings, so positioned that when certain stars from the number of the fixed stars <note>original: "fixarum"; stars that appear to maintain their positions relative to one another, unlike planets</note> reach them, they simultaneously cease to be seen. In that place, an aperture should be established, about the size of a pupil, so that on following days the eye can be returned to the same point without error. Now, at the very moment when one of the stars leaves the field of view, the time indicated by the clock should be noted. And let the same be done on the following day, or rather with several days interceding. For if a space of only one day has passed between two observations, the clock time in the latter observation ought to fall short of that which was noted in the first observation by 3 minutes <note>original: "scrupulis primis"</note> and 56 seconds <note>original: "secundis"</note>. For in this way it will be established that the length of the pendulum <note>original: "perpendiculi"</note> is correctly set; since any revolution of the fixed stars is surpassed by this much by a mean solar day <note>term: "mean solar day" (dies solaris mediocri); the average time it takes for the sun to return to the same meridian, which is slightly longer than the time it takes for stars to return</note>. I say "mean," because solar days, from noon to noon, are not all equal to one another, as will soon be more fully explained. But if the observation is repeated only after several days, the same amount of difference must be calculated for each day.

Let it be, for example, that in the first observation, at the moment of the star's disappearance, the clock recorded the hour of 9, with 30 minutes and 18 seconds; then, on the seventh day afterward, as the same star vanishes, let it indicate the hour of 8, with 50 minutes and 24 seconds. This time falls short of the former by 39 minutes and 54 seconds. These, divided into seven, give a daily retardation of 5 minutes and 42 seconds. It ought, however, to have been 3 minutes and 56 seconds; which is less than that amount by 1 minute and 46 seconds.

<summary>Huygens explains how to calibrate the pendulum clock by observing the transit of "fixed stars" through an aperture, noting that a perfectly tuned clock should lose 3 minutes and 56 seconds daily relative to sidereal time.</summary>
<keywords>calibration, pendulum length, sidereal time, solar time, astronomical observation, minutes and seconds, star transit</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on calibrating the clock. The previous page explained how to use the fixed stars and sidereal time (the 3 minute and 56 second daily difference) for calibration. Here, Huygens moves to the more complex method of using the sun.</meta>

# CHRISTIAN HUYGENS

<margin>
DESCRIPTION
OF THE
CLOCK.
</margin>

Therefore, the clock falls short of the true, or mean, measurement of days by that same amount every day. <note>Referring to the 3 minutes and 56 seconds difference between solar and sidereal time mentioned on the previous page.</note>

Moreover, it will be possible to examine the movement of the clock in another way: by the sun. However, in this case, account must be taken of the inequality of natural days. For as I have already said, not all days of this kind are equal to one another; and although the difference is small, it often grows over an interval of many days to such an extent that it can by no means be ignored. For even if one possesses a sundial <note>original: "solarium"</note> described as perfectly as possible, and the movement of the clock mechanism <note>original: "automati"</note> is adjusted to the truest measure of the days and does not deviate from it, it will nonetheless necessarily happen that, at certain times of the year, they will often differ from each other by a quarter of an hour, or even half an hour, and then again they will agree of their own accord at fixed times. That this is so will be understood from the "Equation of Time" table <note>original: "tabula temporis æquatoria"; a table used to reconcile the difference between apparent solar time (sundial) and mean solar time (clock)</note> which we provide below, after we have shown its use, which is as follows.

One should take the "equation" <note>the correction value</note> from the table assigned to the day on which we first made the clock agree with the sun, or with the sundial <note>original: "sciotherico"</note>. Likewise, take the equation for the day on which one wishes to know how well the clock is regulated to the measure of the days. If the first equation is greater than the following one, the hour of the clock mechanism ought to exceed the hour of the gnomon <note>the shadow-casting part of a sundial</note> by the amount that those equations differ from each other. But if the equation of the later day is found to be greater, the excess will belong to the hour of the gnomon—that is, the time observed from the sun. For example, if on the 5th of March the sundial and the clock mechanism agree on the same hour, the equation for that day is found in the table to be 3 minutes <note>original: "scrupulorum primorum"</note> and 11 seconds <note>original: "secundorum"</note>. Suppose one wishes to know on the 20th of the same month whether the clock is correctly measuring equal hours or not: on the later day, the assigned equation is found to be 7 minutes and 27 seconds. Because this exceeds the preceding one by 4 minutes and 16 seconds, the hour of the sundial ought to be later by that much than the hour indicated by the clock mechanism. From this, if a different result is found, it will be easy to gather how much the clock gained or lost each day.

In calculating this table, I have applied a twofold cause, both known to astronomers: namely, the obliquity of the Ecliptic <note>the tilt of the Earth's axis relative to its orbit</note> and the anomaly of the solar motion <note>the apparent change in the sun's speed due to the Earth's elliptical orbit</note>. Reason demands this, and experience also proves it—experience built upon these very clocks, which could not have been obtained without them. Indeed, with the equation proposed here, observations of the sun (which we conducted often over several months, daily at the moment the sun occupied the meridian circle) were found to agree most clearly.

<summary>Huygens explains the "Equation of Time," noting that solar days vary in length, and provides instructions for using a table to calibrate mechanical clocks against sundials by accounting for the Earth's tilt and orbital eccentricity.</summary>
<keywords>Equation of Time, sundial, clock calibration, solar anomaly, obliquity of the ecliptic, mean time, apparent time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Huygens's technical description of his pendulum clock. # CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>
DESCRIPTION OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>

Now, once the trial has been conducted in either of the two ways we have described (though preferably by the first), if the clock is found to deviate significantly from the mean length of days—to the point where the difference exceeds three or four minutes <note>original: "scrupula prima"; Huygens uses "first scruples" to mean minutes and "second scruples" for seconds.</note>—the remedy shall be applied by increasing or decreasing the length of the pendulum itself. In this matter, the following rule must be held: the clock’s motion will be accelerated or retarded by as many minutes each day as there are 7/10 of a line <note>A "line" was a traditional unit of length, approximately 2.25 millimeters (1/12 of a French inch).</note> subtracted from or added to the pendulum. And when it has been brought nearly to the true measure by this method, the remaining correction will be conveniently accomplished by moving a small weight, **Δ**, which is attached to the rod **v v**. This weight has the shape of a lens, the cross-section of which through its axis we have shown in **Figure I**. Because it weighs only a twentieth or thirtieth part of the main weight **x**, it follows that even if it is moved a considerable distance from its original position, it does not affect the movement of the pendulum very much. Specifically, it speeds up the motion whenever it is moved toward the middle of the rod’s length, and slows it down when moved away from there, whether up or down.

To ensure that one does not spend too much time searching for that exact point which will give the truest measurement of days, we have divided the lower half of the rod according to a certain ratio derived from the laws of motion—assuming, of course, that the weight **Δ** is one-fiftieth the weight of **x**, and that the rod **v v** itself is of equal weight. These divisions are shown in **Figure IV**, where the lower portion of the pendulum is seen divided into three parts; the one to be placed at the very bottom is marked **A B**. Point **A** is the center of gravity of weight **x**. From point **c**, each individual division produces a daily difference of fifteen minutes when the lens-shaped weight **Δ** is moved by such an interval. However, the demonstration of these divisions and the method of their discovery will be provided in the section concerning the **Center of Oscillation**. <note>The "Center of Oscillation" is a landmark concept in physics developed by Huygens; it is the point on a pendulum where it acts as if all its mass were concentrated.</note>

Furthermore, for the sake of investigating longitudes for those who travel by sea, we would describe their form here if we had explored and determined which design was best suited for this purpose as thoroughly as we have with the preceding ones. Nevertheless, the matter has now reached a point where very little seems lacking to complete an invention of such great utility. I will not be reluctant to explain what has been attempted here, with what success, and what remains to be tried hereafter.

The first two clocks of this kind were carried on a British ship in the year 1664; a noble gentleman from Scotland and a friend of ours <note>This refers to Alexander Bruce, 2nd Earl of Kincardine, who collaborated with Huygens on marine clocks.</note> had arranged for them to be manufactured according to our model. These utilized a coiled steel spring in place of a weight, by the force of which the wheels were driven,

<meta>catchword: driven,</meta>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
## C

<margin>
DESCRIPTION OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>

...were driven, just as is customary in those small automatons <note>Huygens uses "automata" to refer to any self-moving mechanical device, in this case, portable watches or clocks.</note> that are usually carried about. In order to withstand the tossing of the ship, he had suspended the clocks from a steel ball enclosed in a brass cylinder <note>This describes a gimbal system, allowing the clock to remain upright regardless of the ship's motion.</note>, and the pin which maintains the pendulum's motion—the pendulum being half a foot long—he had extended downward and doubled, so that it resembled the shape of an inverted letter F. This was done specifically so that the motion of the pendulum could not wander into a circular path, which would risk the clock stopping. After this ship, along with three others that had been its companions on the journey, returned to Britain, the Commander of the fleet reported the following: 

That is, when he had set sail from the coast of Guinea and reached the island called Saint Thomas <note>São Tomé, located on the equator.</note>, which lies under the equinoctial circle <note>The Equator.</note>, he synchronized the clocks there with the sun and set a course toward the west. Having advanced about seven hundred miles on a continuous path, he then turned back toward the coast of Africa with a favorable south-southwest wind <note>original: "Libonoto"</note>. When he had held that course for two or three hundred miles, the masters of the other ships, fearing they would run out of drinking water before they reached Africa, urged him to turn aside toward the American islands called Barbados for the sake of taking on water. 

Then, having held a council of the navigators, he ordered each one to produce their logs <note>original: "Ephemeridas"</note> and calculations. He found that the others' calculations differed from his own: one by 80 miles, another by a hundred, and a third even more. He himself, however, concluded from the evidence of the clocks that they were no more than about thirty miles away from the island called *del Fuego* <note>Fogo, one of the Cape Verde islands. Not to be confused with Tierra del Fuego in South America.</note>, which is one of those islands not far from Africa that take their name from the Green Cape <note>Cape Verde.</note>. He believed it could be reached the next day; trusting in his pendulums, he commanded the course to be directed there, and on the following day around noon, that very island came into sight, and a few hours later provided a harbor for the ships. This much is from that Commander's report.

From that time, experiments were repeated several times through the efforts of both the Dutch and the French, by order of the Most Serene King <note>King Louis XIV of France, who patronized Huygens's work.</note>, with varying success—though in such a way that the negligence of those to whom the clocks were entrusted could be blamed more often than the mechanical devices themselves. The greatest success, however, was in the Mediterranean Sea during the expedition to the island of Crete, where the most illustrious Duke of Beaufort <note>François de Vendôme, Duke of Beaufort, a French naval commander.</note> had been sent with French troops to bring aid to Candia <note>Modern-day Heraklion, Crete.</note>, which was then besieged by the Turks, and where he also fell in battle. He had clocks on the ship in which he was carried for the sake of this experiment, and had put a man skilled in astronomy in charge of them. From his observations, taken daily, the longitudes of the places they either reached or touched upon in that voyage...

<meta>This page continues Huygens's account of sea trials for his marine pendulum clocks. # CHRISTIAN HUYGENS

<margin>
DESCRIPTION OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>

...ships, or what they had been able to discern with their eyes while passing by, we found to have been exactly measured by the operation of the clocks, and in such a way that the very same differences in longitude were found to be designated as are found in geographical descriptions of the better sort. For between the port of Toulon <note>original: "Toloni portum"</note> and the town of Candia <note>The Venetian name for Heraklion, Crete.</note>, a difference of 1 hour and 22 minutes was found, which is 20 degrees and 30 minutes of longitude. And again, returning from Candia to Toulon, almost the same difference was found. This agreement is the surest indication of the truth.

Between the same port of Toulon and a certain island named *Maretimo* <note>A small island off the western coast of Sicily.</note>, near the promontory of Sicily that looks toward the West, formerly called Lilybaeum, a time difference was observed of 25 minutes and 20 seconds, to which correspond 6 degrees and 20 minutes of longitude. Likewise, from Toulon to the island called *Sapienza*, which is near the Peloponnese toward the West, the difference was 1 hour, 5 minutes, and 45 seconds, to which correspond 16 degrees and 26 minutes of longitude.

The clocks had been checked against the sun, in the morning toward the East and in the evening toward the West, with the moment of time in each case computed from the given altitude of the pole <note>The "altitude of the pole" refers to the latitude of the observer's location.</note>. And this method seems the best of all when ships are at anchor, because these observations are performed by sight of the sun alone, without the aid of other instruments.

Now, a pendulum nine inches in length was inside these clocks, with a weight of half a pound <note>original: "pondere femiffis"</note>. The wheels were driven by the pull of weights, and they were enclosed in the same case with them, four feet in length. At the bottom of the case, a lead weight of a hundred pounds and more was additionally added, so that the suspended machine might better maintain its perpendicular position while hanging in the ship.

Although a very even and consistent motion of the automaton <note>Huygens uses "automaton" to describe the self-moving mechanical clockwork.</note> was discovered through these experiments, we nevertheless set about to perfect it further by another method, which was of this kind: To that wheel which has iron teeth and is closest to the pendulum <note>The escapement wheel.</note>, we hung a small weight from a skillfully constructed little chain, by which it alone would be moved; the rest of the whole machine did nothing else than restore that small lead weight to its former height every half-minute <note>This describes a "remontoire," a mechanism that ensures a constant force reaches the pendulum, isolated from the irregularities of the main gears.</note>. This is done in almost the same manner as can be seen in the construction of the clock explained above, where the weight is lifted by one cord while it imparts its gravity to the motion of the clock by the other. When these things were so constructed, as if all parts were reduced to a single wheel, an even greater regularity of the clocks appeared than before. And a thing occurred worth mentioning: when we had suspended two clocks constructed in this form from the same beam, and the beam was placed on two supports, the motion of the pendulum...

<meta>This page transitions from reports of sea trials to a crucial discovery in physics and a significant redesign of the marine clock. The previous page detailed longitudinal measurements in the Mediterranean; here, Huygens explains the phenomenon of "coupled oscillation" and introduces a triangular pendulum designed to withstand the motion of a ship at sea.</meta>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK

<margin>
DESCRIPTION OF THE CLOCK.
</margin>

...the beats of both clocks agreed with each other in such opposite strokes <note>This is the first historical account of "synchronization" or "entrainment," where two nearby oscillators influence each other to beat in unison.</note> that they never departed from it even in the slightest, but the sound of both was always heard as one. Indeed, if that harmony were disturbed on purpose, it would restore itself in a short time. Having wondered for a while at so unusual a thing, I finally discovered, by conducting a diligent examination, that the cause was to be sought from the movement of the beam <note>original: "tignum"</note> itself from which the clocks hung, although that movement was not at all perceptible.

Namely, the oscillations <note>original: "reciprocationes"</note> of the pendulums communicate some motion to the clocks, however heavily weighted they may be; and this motion, impressed upon the beam itself, necessarily brings it about that if the two pendulums move in any way other than with perfectly opposite strokes, they nonetheless eventually and necessarily arrive at that state, and only then does the motion of the beam completely subside. However, this cause would not have enough efficacy unless the motion of the clocks was already very regular and consistent with each other from other factors.

Furthermore, from experiments conducted in ocean navigation, and especially while a more violent storm was agitating the waters, it was found that the first and primary care must be given to maintaining the motion of the clocks without interruption, because it was observed that they could then hardly endure such great tossing of the ship. For this reason, we have finally changed both the form of the pendulum and the way the clocks themselves are suspended according to a new method. The pendulum has the shape of a triangle, at

<image-desc>A technical diagram illustrating a triangular pendulum system. The main structure is an inverted triangle labeled A, B, and C at its corners. The bottom vertex, B, holds a circular weight described as a lead lens. The top corners, A and C, are attached by lines to suspension points labeled E D and F G, which feature curved "laminæ cycloidales." Above the triangle, a mechanism labeled N and H consists of a horizontal gear and a vertical shaft. At the base of this shaft is a forked pin (clavula bifurcata), labeled K, which interacts with the center of the triangle's top horizontal bar. Small adjustment rings, labeled L, are positioned on the slanted sides of the triangle.</image-desc>

the downward-facing vertex of which a lead lens <term>lens: a double-convex weight, shaped like a lentil, used as a pendulum bob</term> is attached. The remaining two angles are suspended by threads between cycloidal plates <note>Huygens’s signature invention: curved plates that ensure the pendulum’s period remains constant regardless of how wide it swings.</note>. The base of the triangle receives a small forked pin <note>original: "clavulam bifurcatam"</note> at its midpoint and is moved by it; that pin, in turn, receives its motion from a toothed wheel parallel to the horizon. The movement of all the wheels takes its start not from a weight, but from a steel plate <note>A mainspring.</note> enclosed in a drum. In the attached figure, the triangular pendulum is **A B C**; the lead lens is **B**; the cycloidal plates are **E D**, **F G**. The small pin is

...the forked part H K; the wheel with iron teeth N, which is lower than the other clock wheels. Lens-shaped weights <note>original: "Lenticulae". These are the pendulum bobs, shaped like lenses (discs) to reduce air resistance.</note> for regulating the motion of the pendulum L L.

<image-desc>A technical diagram illustrating the suspension mechanism for a marine clock. The central clock housing (A B) is mounted within a rectangular iron frame (D E) which pivots on an axis (C). This entire unit is suspended from an outer support frame or bracket (F H K G), allowing it to remain level. A heavy weight (L) is attached to the bottom of the case to provide stability and maintain a vertical orientation against the motion of a ship.</image-desc>

This second figure displays the method of suspension; where the case A B, first by two axes—of which only one, C, is visible—is inserted into an iron rectangle D E; which rectangle is then in turn supported by its own axes F G by an iron bracket <note>original: "gnomone". While this usually means a sundial's pointer, Huygens uses it here to describe the large, stationary support frame.</note> F H K G, which is fixed immovably to the ship's timber. At the bottom of the case, a weight of 50 pounds is hung. With things arranged in this way, the clock maintains a perpendicular position despite any tilting of the ship.

However, axis C and the one opposite to it are so placed that they correspond in a straight line with the suspension points of that pendulum which we have described: the result being that its own oscillatory motion cannot disturb the machine at all—otherwise, nothing more effectively destroys the motion of a pendulum. Furthermore, the thickness of the axes C C and F G, which equals an inch <note>original: "pollicem", literally "a thumb's breadth."</note>, and the weight of the lead hung below, take away excessive freedom of movement from the clock, and ensure that if it is perhaps moved by a heavier jolt of the ship, it immediately returns to rest and its perpendicular state.

And indeed, this machine is so adapted that it remains only to be taken out to sea and committed to experience <note>Huygens uses "experientia" in the sense of an experimental trial or testing.</note>, which offers almost a certain hope of success, because in those experiments which it has been possible to conduct so far, it has been found to withstand every variation of motion much better than those previous ones.

<summary>Huygens describes a gimbal suspension system (a "box within a box" design) weighted with 50 pounds of lead to keep a marine clock vertical. He explains that the axes of the suspension are aligned with the pendulum's swing to prevent the clock's own motion from interfering with its timekeeping.</summary>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
## PART TWO.

->*On the descent of Heavy Bodies & their motion in a Cycloid.*<-

### HYPOTHESES.

->I.<-

**If** gravity did not exist, and air did not hinder the motion of bodies, each one of them, once motion had been initiated, would continue with a uniform velocity along a straight line. <note>This is a statement of the law of inertia, which Huygens used as a foundational principle before Newton’s *Principia* was published.</note>

->II.<-

Now, however, it happens by the action of gravity—wherever that may originate—that bodies are moved by a composite motion, consisting of the uniform motion they have in this or that direction, and the downward motion resulting from gravity.

->III.<-

And each of these can be considered separately, and neither is hindered by the other.

Suppose a heavy body C is released from rest; in a certain time, which shall be called F, it crosses the space C B by the force of gravity. And again, let it be understood that the same heavy body has received from elsewhere a motion by which, if there were no gravity, it would cross the straight line C D in an equal time F with uniform motion. Therefore, when the force of gravity is added, the heavy body will not arrive from C at D in the said time F, but at some point E, situated directly below D, such that the space D E is always equal to the space C B. For in this way, both the uniform motion and that which arises from gravity will perform their parts, with neither hindering the other. Which line the heavy body traverses by that composite motion, when the uniform motion is directed not straight up or down but at an angle <note>original: "in obliquum". Huygens is setting the stage to describe parabolic trajectories and curved paths.</note>, can be determined from what follows. But when the uniform motion C D occurs vertically downward...

<summary>Huygens begins the theoretical section of his work by establishing three fundamental hypotheses regarding inertia and the independence of vertical and horizontal motion. He explains how gravity acts upon a body already in motion to create a composite path.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, inertia, composite motion, kinematics, uniform velocity, falling bodies</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of composite motion introduced at the bottom of page 21. Huygens is explaining how the path of a falling body can be understood as the sum of a constant-speed motion (the velocity it already had) and the downward acceleration caused by gravity.</meta>

<header>->CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS ON THE DESCENT OF HEAVY BODIES.<-</header>

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a central point C on a vertical axis. Above C, points D and E are marked on the axis. Below C, points B, D, and E are marked. To the right of the axis, five rays extend from C, each ending at a point D. From each point D, a vertical dashed line drops to a point E. The points E are connected by dotted curved lines, illustrating the path of a body under combined uniform motion and gravitational acceleration.</image-desc>

it appears that the line CD, with the addition of motion from gravity, is increased by the straight line DE. Similarly, when the uniform motion <note>original: "motus æquabilis"</note> CD tends upward, that same CD is diminished by the line DE, so that, once the time F has elapsed, the heavy body is always found at point E. If, in both of these cases, we consider these two motions separately—as we have said—and conceive that neither is hindered by the other, we may then discover the cause and the laws of the acceleration of falling bodies. And first, we shall demonstrate these two things together:

### PROPOSITIO I.

***In equal intervals of time, equal parts of velocity are added to a falling heavy body, and the spaces traversed in equal times from the beginning of the descent increase continually by an equal excess.*** <note>Huygens is describing the law of constant acceleration. The "equal excess" refers to the fact that the distance fallen in each successive second increases by a constant amount (following the sequence of odd numbers 1, 3, 5, 7...).</note>

Let it be supposed that some heavy body, starting from rest at A, has fallen through the space AB during the first interval of time; and when it arrives at B, it has acquired a velocity by which it could subsequently, in a second interval of time, traverse a certain space BD with uniform motion <term>uniform motion; motion at a constant speed</term>. We know, therefore, that the space to be covered in the second interval of time will be greater than the space BD, because even if all action of gravity ceased at B, the space BD would still be traversed. Indeed, it will be carried by a motion composed of the uniform motion by which it would have traversed the space BD, and of the motion of falling bodies, by which it must be depressed through the spa—

<summary>Huygens establishes the geometric proof for the law of falling bodies, asserting that gravity adds equal increments of velocity in equal time intervals. He introduces the concept of composite motion, where a body's position is the result of its current velocity plus the added effect of gravitational acceleration.</summary>
<keywords>acceleration, gravity, uniform motion, law of falling bodies, composite motion, velocity, kinematics</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the proof of Proposition I from page 22. Huygens is using a step-by-step geometric logic to demonstrate that a falling body's velocity increases uniformly over time and that the distances fallen in equal time intervals follow a predictable arithmetic progression.</meta>

<header>### THE OSCILLATING CLOCK: ON THE DESCENT OF HEAVY BODIES.</header>

<image-desc>In the left margin, a vertical diagrammatic line shows the path of a falling body with points labeled from top to bottom: A, B, D, E, F, G, H, and K. Faint lowercase letters (a, d, e, f, g) appear between some of the primary points.</image-desc>

...space <note>Completing the word "spatium" from the previous page.</note> equal to AB itself. Therefore, with DE (which is equal to AB) added to BD, we know that in the second time interval the heavy body will reach point E.

If indeed we inquire what velocity <note>Huygens uses "velocitas" and "celeritas" interchangeably to mean speed.</note> it has at E at the end of the second time interval, we will find that it must be double the velocity it had at B at the end of the first interval. For we have said that it moves with a composite motion <note>A movement made of two parts: the speed it already had plus the new speed added by gravity.</note> consisting of the uniform motion with the speed acquired at B, and the motion produced by gravity. Since gravity is clearly the same in the second interval as in the first, it must have conferred an equal speed to the heavy body during the course of the second interval as it did at the end of the first. Therefore, since it has preserved entirely the speed acquired at the end of the first interval, it appears that at the end of the second interval twice the speed is present than was acquired at the end of the first interval—that is, double.

Now if, after it reaches E, it were to continue moving from then on only with a uniform speed <note>original: "celeritate æquabili"</note> as great as it acquired there, it appears that in a third interval of time (equal to the previous ones), it would traverse the space EF, which would be double the space BD. This is because we said that BD was traversed at half this speed with uniform motion in an equal part of time. However, with the action of gravity added again, it will traverse in the third interval, in addition to space EF, the space FG, which is equal to AB or DE. Thus, at the end of the third time interval, the heavy body will be found at G. Here it will truly have a velocity triple that which it had at B at the end of the first interval: because in addition to the speed acquired at E, which we said was double that acquired at B, the force of gravity, during the course of the third interval, again conferred a speed equal to that at the end of the first. Wherefore, both speeds together, at the end of the third interval, will constitute triple the speed that existed at the end of the first interval.

In the same way, it will be shown that in the fourth time interval, it must cover both the space GH, triple the space BD, and the space HK, which is equal to AB. Thus, the velocity at K at the end of the fourth interval will be quadruple that which was at B at the end of the first interval. And so, for any number of subsequent spaces considered, which are completed in equal times, it is manifest that they increase by an equal excess—which is equal to BD—and simultaneously, the velocities also increase equally through equal times.

## PROPOSITION II.

> *The distance covered by a heavy object in a given time, starting its fall from rest, is half the distance it would travel in the same amount of time with a uniform motion <note>A motion at a constant, unchanging speed.</note> at the speed it acquired at the final moment of its fall.*

Let the same conditions be assumed as in the preceding proposition, where indeed AB was the distance covered by a heavy object falling from A in a certain time. Let BD be the distance that we understand would be covered in an equal amount of time at a uniform speed <note>original: "celeritate æquabili"</note> equal to the speed acquired at the end of the first time interval—that is, at the end of distance AB. I state, therefore, that distance BD is double that of AB.

<image-desc>A vertical diagram situated to the left of the following paragraph, depicting a vertical line with several horizontal tick marks. To the left of the line, labels for points are arranged vertically from top to bottom: A, B, D, E, F, G, H, and K.</image-desc>

For since the distances passed through by the falling object in the first four equal time intervals are AB, BE, EG, and GH <note>Huygens is referencing the "Law of Odd Numbers," where a falling body covers distances in a ratio of 1, 3, 5, 7, etc., in successive equal intervals of time.</note>, among which there is a certain fixed proportion: if we take double the time for those intervals—namely, if for the first time period we now take those two in which distances AB and BE were completed, and for the second time period we take the remaining two in which distances EG and GK were completed—it follows that the distances AE and EK, which were completed in equal times starting from rest, must be in the same ratio to each other as the distances AB and BE, which were likewise traversed in equal times starting from rest.

Since, therefore, AB is to BE as AE is to EK; and by inversion, as EB (or DA) is to AB, so KE is to EA: it will also be, by division <note>A geometric method of comparing ratios by looking at the difference between terms.</note>, that DB is to BA as the excess of KE over EA is to EA. However, from what was shown in the preceding proposition, KE is equal to both twice AB and five times BD; whereas EA is equal to both twice AB and once BD; it appears that the said excess of KE over EA is equal to four times BD. Therefore, just as DB is to BA, so four times DB will be to EA: from which it follows that EA will be four times BA itself. But this same EA is equal, as we said, to both twice AB and once BD. Therefore, BD will be equal to twice AB; which was to be demonstrated.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut tailpiece centered at the bottom, featuring symmetrical floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: PROPOS. (Proposition)</meta>

<summary>In this proposition, Huygens uses geometric ratios to prove that the total distance an object falls is exactly half the distance it would have traveled if it had maintained its final top speed for the entire duration of the fall.</summary>
<keywords>Huygens, kinematics, free fall, uniform acceleration, geometry, gravity, Merton Mean Speed Theorem, Law of Odd Numbers</keywords>

## PROPOSITION III.

*The two distances covered by a falling heavy body in any two time intervals, both measured from the very beginning of the descent, are to each other in the duplicate ratio <note>A "duplicate ratio" is the square of the ratio; for example, if the times are in a ratio of 2:3, the distances are in a ratio of 4:9.</note> of those times—that is, as the squares of the times—or also as the squares of the velocities acquired at the end of each time interval.*

For since it was shown in the preceding proposition that the distances AB, BE, EG, GK, however many there may be, are completed by a falling body in equal time intervals and increase by an equal excess—which excess is equal to the first distance AB itself—it is now clear that since BD is double AB, the distance BE will be three times AB; EG will be five times AB; GK will be seven times; and the others thereafter will increase according to the progression of odd numbers starting from unity: 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, etc. And since the sum of any number of these consecutive numbers results in a square whose root is the quantity of numbers added (for instance, if the first three are added, they make nine; if four, sixteen <note>Huygens is describing a geometric property: 1+3=4 (2²), 1+3+5=9 (3²), 1+3+5+7=16 (4²). This proves that the total distance fallen is proportional to the square of the total time.</note>), it follows from this that the distances covered by a falling heavy body, both beginning from the start of the fall, are to each other as the squares of the times during which the fall lasted—provided, of course, that the times taken are commensurable <note>Commensurable means the two time periods can both be measured exactly by a third, smaller unit of time (they have a common ratio).</note>.

However, the demonstration is easily extended to incommensurable times as well. For let there be times of this sort, whose ratio to one another is that of the lines AB to CD, and let the distances covered in these times be E and F, both starting from the beginning of the descent. I state that as the square of AB is to the square of CD, so is distance E to F.

For if this is denied, let it be assumed first, if possible, that distance E has a greater ratio to F than the square of AB has to the square of CD—specifically, the same ratio as the square of AB to the square of some line CG (taking CG to be smaller than CD). Then, let a part DH be subtracted from CD that is smaller than the excess of CD over CG (DG), such that the remaining part HC is commensurable to AB; for it is established that this can be done. Therefore, CH will be greater than CG. Yet, as the square of time AB is to the square of time CH, so is distance E (covered in time AB) to the distance covered in time CH, according to what was shown above. This distance is indeed greater than that which is covered in time CD, namely distance F. Therefore, the ratio of distance E to distance F is less than the ratio of the square of AB to the square of CH. However, the ratio of distance E to F was assumed to be as the square of AB to the square of CG; therefore, there will also be a lesser ra-

<summary>Huygens proves that the total distance a body falls is proportional to the square of the time elapsed, deriving this from the "Law of Odd Numbers." He begins a geometric proof to show this law holds true even for time intervals that do not share a common measure.</summary>

...the ratio <note>original: "ratio"</note> of the square of AB to the square of CG than the ratio of the square of AB to the square of CH; and therefore the square of CG would be greater than the square of CH. This is absurd, since CH was stated to be greater than CG. Therefore, the ratio of distance E to distance F is not greater than the ratio of the square of AB to the square of CD.

Now let us suppose, if possible, that it has a lesser ratio; and let the ratio of distance E to F be the same as that of the square of AB to the square of CL, where CL is taken to be greater than CD. Let LK be subtracted from CL, being less than the excess LD by which CL exceeds CD; and let this be done such that the remaining length KC is commensurable <note>In 17th-century geometry, "commensurable" means two magnitudes have a common measure—essentially, they can be expressed as a ratio of whole numbers.</note> to AB. Therefore, because the square of time AB is to the square of time CK as the distance E (traversed in time AB) is to the distance traversed in time CK. But the distance traversed in time CD—namely, distance F—is less than this distance <insert>traversed in time CK</insert>. Consequently, the ratio of distance E to F will be greater than the ratio of the square of AB to the square of CK. However, the ratio of distance E to F was assumed to be the same as the ratio of the square of AB to the square of CL. Therefore, the ratio of the square of AB to the square of CL will be greater than the ratio of the same square AB to the square of CK, and thus the square of CL would be less than the square of CK. This is absurd, since CL is greater than CK. Therefore, the distance E does not have a lesser ratio to F than the square of AB has to the square of CD. For this reason, it is necessary that it has the same ratio. Furthermore, since the velocities acquired at the end of times AB and CD are to each other as the times themselves; it appears that the ratio of distances E to F is also the same as that of the squares of the velocities <note>original: "celeritatum"</note> acquired by the end of times AB and CD, during which the distances were traversed. Thus, the proposition stands proved.

## PROPOSITION IV.

> *If a heavy body begins to move upward with the same velocity it acquired at the end of its descent, it will happen that in equal parts of time, it will traverse the same distances upward that it previously traversed downward; and thus it will ascend to the same height from which it had fallen. Likewise, in equal parts of time, it will lose equal increments <note>original: "momenta"</note> of velocity.*

For let there be, as in Proposition II, any number of distances, in equal...

For the sake of brevity, let each speed <note>original: "celeritas"</note> henceforth be designated by the length of the space that a heavy body would traverse in one part of time with that uniform motion <term>uniform motion|Movement at a constant speed in a straight line, without acceleration or deceleration.</term>, such as those we considered in the descent.

Therefore, from what was shown in the stated proposition, when the heavy body has arrived at **K**, it has the speed **G H** increased by the speed **B D**, which is the speed **K F**, because **K F** is equal to **H G** and **B D** themselves; for the individual parts **H K** and **F G** are equal to **A B** itself, and therefore both together are equal to **B D**, which we showed to be double **A B** in Proposition 2. Thus, by turning the speed acquired at the end of the descent at **K** upwards, if the heavy body were carried with a uniform motion, it would complete the space **K F** in one part of time. But, with the action of gravity <note>Huygens treats gravity as a constant force that subtracts from the upward speed in the same way it adds to the downward speed.</note> being added, the ascent will be diminished by the space **F G** equal to **A B**, as is clear from what was said regarding the hypothesis taken at the beginning. Therefore, in the first part of time, the heavy body will ascend only through **K G**, which is the same space it had descended in the very last part of time. At the same time, however, it is necessary that just as much speed has been lost as is acquired in one part of time by falling downward, that is, speed **B D**. Therefore the heavy body, when it has ascended to **G**, has the remaining speed **H G**, whereas at the beginning of the ascent it had speed **H G** together with speed **B D**. Moreover, **G D** is equal to **H G** itself; since it is equal to **F E** together with **D B**, that is, together with double **A B**, that is, together with the two **F G** and **E D**. Therefore, if from **G**, with uniform speed, as much as it has there, it should proceed upwards, it would complete the space **G D** in one part of time. But with the action of gravity being added, that ascent will be diminished by the space **D E**, equal to **A B** itself. Therefore, in this second part of time, it will ascend through the space **G E**, which it had also traversed by falling in a similar part of time. At the same time, however, just as much speed must again have been lost as is acquired from a fall in one part of time, namely speed **B D**. Thus, when it reaches up to...

<summary>Huygens demonstrates the symmetry of motion, proving that a body projected upward will decelerate and cover distances in equal time intervals that exactly mirror the acceleration and distances of its descent.</summary>

...if it has ascended to **E**, it possesses only the speed **F E**, which is surely what remains when the speed **B D** is taken away from the speed **G D**. For **B D**, as we have already said, is equal to the two segments **D E** and **F G**. 

Moreover, **E A** is equal to this **F E**, since **F E** is equal to **B D** taken twice—that is, to **B D** together with the double of **A B**, which is to say, together with the two segments **A B** and **D E**. Therefore, if it were to continue upwards from **E** with a uniform speed <term>uniform speed|Movement at a constant rate, where equal distances are covered in equal times without acceleration.</term> equal to what it has at that point, it would cover the space **E A** in one part of time. But as the action of gravity <note>original: "actione gravitatis"</note> intervenes, this ascent is diminished by the space **A B**. Consequently, in this part of time, it will ascend only through the space **E B**, which it had also traversed in a similar part of time while descending. Here, however, it is necessary that the speed has decreased by just as much as is acquired in one unit of time by falling downwards—namely, the speed **B D**. 

Thus, when the heavy body has ascended as far as **B**, it has the speed **B D** remaining, since at **E** it had the speed **F E**, which is double that of **B D**. If, therefore, it were to continue upwards from **B** with a uniform speed equal to what it has there, it would cover the space equal to **D B**—which is double **A B**—in one part of time. But as the action of gravity intervenes, this ascent is diminished by a space equal to **A B**. Therefore, in this part of time, it will ascend only through the space **B A**, which it had also traversed in the first period of its descent. 

And indeed, at the end of this final period of time, the heavy body will necessarily be found at point **A**. But it might be said, perhaps, that it ascended higher than **A**, and then fell back to it. Yet this would be absurd, since a motion proceeding from gravity cannot ascend higher than the point from which it fell. Furthermore, since the speed it held at **B** has again decreased by the speed **B D**, it is clear that for the heavy body now situated at **A**, no speed remains, and therefore it will travel no higher. 

Thus it has been shown that it arrives at the same height from which it fell, and that it has retraced the individual spaces—which it had passed through in equal times of descent—in the same number of equal times of ascent. It is also apparent that in equal times, equal increments <note>original: "momenta"</note> of speed were lost. Therefore, the proposition stands proven.

But because in the demonstration of the second proposition, upon which the preceding one depends, it was assumed that there is a certain proportion of spaces traversed by a falling heavy body in continuous equal times, and that this proportion is the same regardless of which equal times are chosen (which must be so by the nature of the matter, and if it were denied, one would have to admit that investigating the proportion of these spaces is in vain); nevertheless, because the proposition can also be demonstrated without this by following the method of **Galileo** <note>original: "Galilei"</note>,

<summary>Huygens completes the geometric proof that the ascent of a heavy body is the perfect inverse of its descent, reaching its original height as its speed is reduced to zero by gravity. He concludes by acknowledging his reliance on Galilean principles regarding the proportions of falling bodies.</summary>
<keywords>kinematics, law of falling bodies, Galileo, symmetry of motion, gravity, uniform speed, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

## PROPOSITION V.

**T**he space traversed in a certain time by a heavy body beginning its fall from rest is half of that space which it would cross in an equal time with a <term>uniform motion|Movement at a constant speed in a straight line, without acceleration or deceleration.</term>, at the speed which it acquired at the last moment of its fall.

It must be shown, therefore, that the plane **P** is half of the rectangle **MH**—that is, with the diagonal **AL** drawn, it is equal to the triangle **AHL**.

If the plane **P** is not equal to the triangle **AHL**, it will therefore be either less than it or greater. ...of the triangle **AHL** over the plane **P**. For it is clear that this can be done, since the entire excess of the <term>circumscribed figure|A series of rectangles drawn outside the triangle's boundary, representing a speed slightly higher than the actual falling body.</term> over the <term>inscribed figure|A series of rectangles drawn inside the triangle's boundary, representing a speed slightly lower than the actual falling body.</term> is equal to the lowest rectangle, which has the base **HL**. Therefore, the excess of the triangle **AHL** itself over the inscribed figure will be entirely less than its excess over the plane **P**, and consequently, the figure inscribed in the triangle will be greater than plane **P**. Furthermore, since the straight line **AH** represents the time of the whole descent, its equal parts **AC**, **CE**, **EG** will represent equal parts of that time. And since the speeds of a falling body increase in the same proportion as the times of descent <note>original: "celeritates mobilis cadentis crescant eadem proportione qua tempora descensus"</note>; and let the speed acquired at the end of the whole time be **HL**; then the speed acquired at the end of the first part of time **AC** will be **CK**, because as **AH** is to **AC**, so **HL** is to **CK**. Similarly, the speed acquired at the end of the second part of time **CE** will be **EO**, and so on.

It is evident, moreover, that in the first time interval **AC**, some space is traversed by the moving body that is greater than nothing. In the second time interval **CE**, the space traversed is greater than the rectangle **KE**, because the space **KE** would have been traversed in time **CE** by <term>uniform motion|Motion at a constant, unchanging speed.</term> at the speed **CK**. For spaces traversed in uniform motion have a <term>compound ratio|A mathematical relationship derived from multiplying two or more ratios together—in this case, time multiplied by speed.</term> of the ratio of the times and the ratio of the velocities; therefore, since we have established that in time **AH**, at the uniform speed **HL**, the space **MH** is traversed, it follows that in time **CE**, at the uniform speed **CK**, the space **KE** is traversed, since the ratio of rectangle **MH** to rectangle **KE** is compounded of the ratios of **AH** to **CE** and **HL** to **EO**.

Since, therefore—as I have said—the space **KE** is that which would be traversed in time **CE** at the uniform speed **CK**, and the moving body is carried in time **CE** by an accelerated motion which already has the speed **CK** at the very beginning of this time; it is manifest that by this accelerated motion, in time **CE**, a greater space than **KE** will be completed. By the same reasoning, in the third time interval **EG**, it will complete a greater space than **OG**, because it would have completed exactly **OG** in that same time **EG** at the uniform speed **EO**. And so thereafter, in each individual part of the time **AH**, the moving body will cover greater spaces than the rectangles of the inscribed figure adjacent to those parts. Therefore, the total space completed by accelerated motion will be greater than the inscribed figure itself. But that total space was set as equal to plane **P**. Thus, the inscribed figure would be smaller than space **P**, which is absurd; for it was just shown to be greater than that same space. Therefore, the plane **P** is not less than the triangle **AHL**. Nor will it be shown to be greater.

For suppose, if it were possible, that it is greater; and let **AH** be divided into equal parts, and at their heights, let figures again be inscribed and circumscribed...

As before, let a figure composed of rectangles be constructed for triangle **AHL**, such that one exceeds the other by an excess smaller than that by which plane **P** exceeds triangle **AHL**; therefore, the <term>circumscribed figure|A series of rectangles drawn outside the triangle's boundary, representing a speed slightly higher than the actual falling body.</term> will necessarily be smaller than plane **P**. It is already established that in the first part of time **AC**, less space is traversed by the moving body than the area **BC**, because this space would be covered in the same time **AC** with a uniform speed **CK**, which the moving body only reaches at the very end of time **AC**. Similarly, in the second part of time **CE**, less space will be traversed by the accelerated motion than the area **DE**, because this would be covered in the same time **CE** with a uniform speed **EO**, which the moving body only attains at the end of time **CE**. And so on, in each individual part of the time **AH**, smaller spaces will be crossed by the moving body than the rectangles of the circumscribed figure adjacent to those parts. Therefore, the total space completed by the accelerated motion will be less than the circumscribed figure itself. But that space was stated to be equal to plane **P**; therefore, plane **P** would also be less than the circumscribed figure. This is <note>original: "absurdum"</note> absurd, since this figure was just shown to be smaller than plane **P**. Therefore, plane **P** is not greater than triangle **AHL**, and it has already been shown not to be less. Therefore, it is necessary that they are equal; which was to be demonstrated.

And indeed, it should be understood that all these things demonstrated so far apply equally to heavy bodies descending and ascending through inclined planes as they do to those moving perpendicularly: since the principles laid down regarding the effect of gravity must be admitted by the same reasoning in both cases.

From this, it will now not be difficult to demonstrate the following proposition, which Galileo requested be granted to him as if it were somehow self-evident. For that demonstration which he later attempted to provide, and which appears in the later edition of his works, seems in my judgment to be <note>Huygens is critiquing Galileo's *Two New Sciences* (1638). Huygens felt Galileo's proof that speed depends only on vertical height was logically circular or lacked rigor.</note> insufficiently firm. The proposition is as follows:

### PROPOSITIO VI.

*The speeds acquired by heavy bodies descending over different inclinations of planes are equal, if the elevations of the planes are equal.*

We call the elevation of the plane its height according to the <term>perpendicular|The vertical distance from the top of the ramp to the level of the base, regardless of the ramp's angle.</term>.

Let there be, therefore, inclined planes, the sections of which are made by a plane erected to the horizon, **AB**, **CB**; and whose elevations are **AE**, **CD**...

From this it is also clear that for an object moving down the circumference of a circle, or along any curved line (for curves may here be considered as being composed of an infinite number of straight lines <note>This reflects the early development of calculus, where curves were treated as a series of infinitely small straight segments.</note>), the same speed is always acquired if it has descended from the same height: and that speed is the same as it would obtain in a perpendicular fall from that same height.

### PROPOSITION IX.

> *If a heavy body, after descending, turns its motion upward, it will ascend to the same height from which it came, regardless of the number or inclination of the joined plane surfaces over which it travels.*

Let a heavy body fall from height **ab**, and from point **b** let the planes **bc**, **cd**, and **de** be inclined upwards, so that the end point **e** is at the same height as point **a**. I say that if the moving object, after its fall through **ab**, redirects its motion so that it continues to move along the aforementioned inclined planes, it will reach all the way to **e**.

<summary>Huygens proves that an object falling along any path (segmented or curved) gains a speed determined only by its vertical drop, and argues that this speed is sufficient to carry the object back to its original starting height.</summary>

### PROPOSITION X.

*If a moving body falls perpendicularly, or descends through any surface, and then, having gained momentum <note>Latin: *impetu concepto*. Huygens uses "impetus" to describe the quantity of motion or kinetic energy the object has "gathered" during its fall.</note>, is carried upward through any other surface, it will always have the same velocity at points of equal height during its ascent and descent.*

For example, if a moving body falling from the height AB then continues its motion through the surface BCD, in which point C is at the same height as point E is on [the vertical line] AB. I say that the moving body possesses the same velocity at C as it had at E.

## PROPOSITION XI.

If a moving body travels downward along some surface, and then, with its motion reversed, is carried upward along the same surface or another similar and similarly positioned one, it will descend and ascend through the same distance in equal times.

For example, if a moving body descends through the surface AB, and when it reaches B,

->[Image: A geometric diagram showing a curve ABC starting at point A on the left, curving downward to point B at the bottom, and ascending to point C on the right. Points A and C are at the same horizontal elevation.]<-

it ascends with its motion reversed through the same AB, or through BC (which is similar and similarly positioned with respect to the horizontal plane), it is established from the previous demonstrations that it will reach the same height from which it originated. Since, moreover, at every point of the same height, it possesses the same velocity in ascending as it did in descending <note>Huygens is describing what we now call the conservation of mechanical energy; the speed of the object is determined solely by its vertical position, regardless of whether it is going up or down.</note>; it is apparent that the same line is traversed twice with the same velocity at each of its parts. From this, it is necessarily the case that the times of both motions are equal; which was to be demonstrated.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a circle with a vertical diameter labeled A at the top and C at the bottom. A horizontal line FG passes through the circle, intersecting the diameter at point D. A point B is on the upper-left arc of the circle. A line AF is drawn from point A through point B to point F on the left side of the horizontal line. A point E is marked on the segment FD. Various construction lines connect these points, including BC and BE.</image-desc>

ference be drawn, which will necessarily meet the straight line FG between F and D. Therefore, the angle BAC in the circle is equal to the angle EBC <note>Latin: *angulus BAC in circulo æqualis angulo EBC*. This is a standard theorem in Euclidean geometry where the angle between a tangent and a chord is equal to the angle in the alternate segment.</note>. For which reason the angle FBE, which together with EBC constitutes the right angle FBC <note>Since the angle in a semicircle, ABC, is a right angle, and BE is tangent at B.</note>, will be equal to angle BCA. Moreover, because the triangles ABC and AGF are similar, angle F will also be equal to angle ACB. Therefore, that same angle F is equal to angle FBE. Thus triangle FEB is isosceles, having equal legs <note>Latin: *crura*. Literally "legs," referring to the two equal sides of an isosceles triangle.</note> FE and EB. Therefore, when the straight line ED is added to both, the total line FD becomes equal to the two segments BE and ED. Now, it is well known that these two segments taken together are greater than the arc BD, which is intercepted by the same endpoints and is concave in the same direction <note>Huygens is invoking an axiom from Archimedes: a path consisting of straight lines that "envelops" a concave curve is always longer than the curve itself.</note>. Therefore, FD will also be greater than the same arc BD; thus the proposition is established.

## PROPOSITION XIII.

*With the same conditions established, if the straight line AB meets the line DG inside the circle; I say that the arc BD, intercepted by the lines GD and AB, is greater than the straight line DF.*

For let DC be joined, and let the chord DB <note>Latin: *subtensa DB*. A "subtense" or chord is a straight line connecting the two endpoints of an arc.</note> be drawn for the arc DB. Therefore, since angle ABD is equal to ACD <note>These are angles subtended by the same arc of the circle.</note>, which is to say, equal to angle ADG; and since angle DFB is greater than angle ADF, or ADG; it will be...

### PROPOSITION XIV.

**L**et there be a *cycloid* <term>cycloid: the curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line. Huygens discovered this was the "isochronal" curve, meaning a pendulum following this path always takes the same amount of time to swing, regardless of its width.</term> *ABC, with base AC and axis BD. I believe it is already quite clear how this is generated based on the definition and mechanical description provided earlier. Let a circle BGD be drawn around the axis BD, and from any point E taken on the cycloid, let a line EF be drawn parallel to the base AC, which meets the axis BD at point F and intersects the circumference of circle BGD at G. I say that the straight line segment GE is equal in length to the arc GB.*

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing the generation of a cycloid. A curve ABC represents the cycloid with its base on the line AC. Two circles of equal size are shown: the right circle is centered on the axis BD and tangent to the base at D; the left circle is shifted along the base, tangent at point K. Points E, G, F, N, L, and B are marked to demonstrate the relationship between the generating circle's rotation and the points on the cycloid curve.</image-desc>

For let a circle LEK, equal in size to circle BGD, be drawn through point E so that it touches the base of the cycloid at point K, and let the diameter KL be drawn. Consequently, the straight line AK is equal to the arc EK <note>original: "recta A K arcui E K æqualis". In the geometry of a rolling circle, the horizontal distance traveled by the center is exactly equal to the length of the arc that has touched the ground.</note>; but the entire segment AD is equal to the semi-circumference KEL; therefore, the remaining segment KD is equal to the arc EL, which is the same as arc GB. Furthermore, the segment KD (or NF) is equal to EG, because EN is equal to GF and the segment NG is common to both. Therefore, it is established that GE is also equal to the arc GB.

<summary>This page concludes a lemma on arc lengths and proves a fundamental property of the cycloid: that a horizontal line segment from a point on the curve to the generating circle's circumference is equal in length to the arc of that circle.</summary>
<keywords>cycloid, geometry, circle properties, Christiaan Huygens, proof, arc length, horizontal segment</keywords>

This is a crucial step in understanding the curve’s properties for the pendulum.</meta>
