## PROPOSITION XV.

Given a point on a Cycloid, to draw a straight line through it which touches the Cycloid.

Let the cycloid be ABC, and the point given on it B, through which it is necessary to draw a tangent.
Around the axis of the cycloid AD, let the <term>generating circle: the circle that rolls along a flat surface to create the cycloid curve.</term> AED be described, and let BE be drawn parallel to the base of the cycloid, meeting the said circle at E; and let AE be joined. Finally, let HBN be drawn through B parallel to AE. I say that this line touches the cycloid at B.
For let any point be taken on the line, different from B—first, toward the upper part, such as H—and through H let a straight line be drawn parallel to the base of the cycloid, which meets the cycloid at L, the circle AED at K, and the line AE at M. 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a cycloid curve beginning at point C on a horizontal baseline and ascending to vertex A. A vertical axis AD connects the baseline to the vertex. A generating circle is shown with diameter AD. Point B lies on the cycloid curve; a horizontal line BE connects it to the circle at point E. A diagonal line AE is drawn. A tangent line HBN passes through point B. Two additional horizontal lines are shown: HLKM above BE, and NQOP below BE. Points are labeled C, B, N, Q, H, L, K, M, A, E, O, P, and D.</image-desc>

Because KL is equal to the arc KA, and the straight line KM is less than the arc KE <note>This follows from the geometric properties of the circle and the parallel lines established in previous propositions.</note>, the straight line ML will be less than the arc AE—that is, less than the straight line EB, or MH. From this, it is clear that point H lies outside the cycloid.
Next, let the point N be taken on the line HN lower than B, and through N let a line be drawn parallel to the base as before, meeting the cycloid at Q, the circle AED at O, and the line AE produced at P. Since, therefore, OQ is equal to the arc OA, and OP is greater than the arc OE, PQ will be less than the arc EA—that is, less than the straight line EB, or PN. From this, it appears again that the point N is outside the cycloid. Since, therefore, every point except B taken on the line HBN is outside the cycloid, it is established that the line touches the cycloid at point B; which was to be demonstrated.

I hesitated whether I should leave a place for this demonstration here, because I find one not much different from it published by the most illustrious Wren <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723), the famed architect and scientist who also worked on the mathematics of the cycloid.</note> in the book by Wallis <note>John Wallis (1616–1703), an English mathematician; the book mentioned is likely *De Cycloide* (1659).</note> on the Cycloid. However, the proposed problem can also be resolved by a universal construction, which applies not only to the cycloid but also to other curves generated by the <term>circumvolution: the act of rolling or rotating one figure along another.</term> of any figure, provided the figure is concave in the same direction and belongs to those called geometric.

<summary>This page presents Proposition XV, demonstrating how to geometrically construct a tangent line to any point on a cycloid curve. Huygens acknowledges that similar work was published by Christopher Wren but includes his own proof for completeness.</summary>
<keywords>tangent, cycloid construction, generating circle, Christopher Wren, John Wallis, geometry of curves, circumvolution</keywords>

<meta>catchword: then.</meta>

<margin>
ON THE DESCENT OF HEAVY BODIES.

For let there be a curve **N A B**, arising from the <term>rolling: the motion of a shape turning along a line without slipping, like a wheel on a road.</term> of a figure **O L** upon a <term>straight line: referred to here as a 'regula' or ruler, acting as the horizontal base.</term> **L D**; namely, with the point **N** describing the curve as it is moved along the circumference of the figure **O L**. And suppose it is necessary to draw a tangent to the curve at point **A**. Let a straight line **C A** be drawn from point **C**, where the figure touched the straight line when the describing point was at **A**. This point of contact can always be found, since the problem is reduced to this: that two straight lines must be drawn parallel to each other, one of which passes through the describing point given on the perimeter of the figure, and the other touches the figure, such that the distance between them is equal to the distance of the given point **A** from the line **L D**. I say that this line **C A** meets the curve at right angles <note>In modern geometry, this means CA is the 'normal' to the curve.</note>, or that the circumference **M A F** described with center **C** and radius **C A** touches the curve at point **A**; therefore, a perpendicular to **A C** drawn through point **A** will touch the curve at that same spot.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a curve labeled N-A-B above a horizontal base line L-C-H-D. A circle is centered at point K, with points E and B on its circumference. Another partial circle or arc M-A-F is centered at point C on the base line. Lines connect various points: C to A, C to B, C to E, K to E, K to B, and a tangent line K-H-D. The diagram illustrates a geometric proof concerning the generation of a curve by a rotating figure along a straight line.</image-desc>

For let **C B** first be drawn to a point **B** on the curve, which lies further beyond point **A** from the line **L D**, and let the position of the figure be understood as being at **B E D** when the describing point was at **B**, and the contact with the straight line at **D**. And let the point of the curve which was at **C**, when the describing point was at **A**, now be raised to **E**; and let **E C** and **E B** be joined, and let the straight line **K H** touch the figure at **E**, meeting the base line at **H**.

Because, therefore, the straight line **C D** is equal to the curve **E D** <note>This is a fundamental property of rolling: the distance traveled on the ground equals the length of the arc that has rolled over it.</note>; and that same curve is greater than both **E H** and **H D** taken together; it follows that **E H** will be greater than **C H**. Thus, the angle **E C H** is greater than **C E H**, and consequently **E C L** is less than **C E K**. However, by adding the angle **K E B**, which is equal to **L C A**, to **K E C**, the angle **C E B** is formed; and by subtracting the angle **L C B** from **E C L**, **E C B** is formed. Therefore, the angle **C E B** is altogether greater than the angle **E C B**. Thus, in triangle **C E B**, the side **C B** will be greater than **E B**. But it is clear that **E B** is equal to **C A**, since it is the very same line transposed along with the figure.

<summary>Huygens provides a geometric proof that a line drawn from the point of contact of a rolling shape to the point generating the curve is a normal (perpendicular) to that curve. This principle is essential for finding the tangent of a cycloid, which is the path of a pendulum's swing.</summary>

From this it is apparent that point **N** falls outside the circle **M A F**, which therefore touches the curve at point **A**. Which was to be demonstrated.

Moreover, the construction and the demonstration are the same whether the generated curve is produced by a describing point taken inside or outside the perimeter of the rolling figure. <note>Huygens is referring to what are now called "curtate" or "prolate" cycloids, where the point is not on the rim of the circle but on a spoke or an extension.</note> The only difference is that, in this latter case, a certain part of the curve descends below the ruler, which causes some variation in the demonstration.

### PROPOSITION XX.

If a circular arc **A B**, which is less than a semicircle, is divided into any number of parts by parallel straight lines—which are spaced at equal intervals from each other and from the parallels drawn through the ends of the arc—such as **C D**, **E F**, **G H**, **K L**, etc.; and if straight lines touching the circumference <note>These are tangents: lines that touch the curve at a single point.</note> are drawn toward one end of the arc **A** and to all the remaining points where the parallels meet the arc, all pointing in the same direction so that each one meets the next parallel line; such as tangents **A C**, **D E**, **F G**, **H K**, etc.: I say that these tangents, if we exclude the first one **A C**, are together less than the proposed arc **A B**. However, all of them taken together, including **A C**, are greater than arc **A B** minus the outermost part **N B**, which is to say, greater than arc **A N**.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a circular arc AB above a horizontal base line with center Z. The arc is divided by several vertical parallel lines (CD, EF, GH, KL, MN, OB). Points on the arc are labeled A, C, E, G, K, M, O, and a point N is marked on the arc near its left extremity. Points on the base line correspond to the feet of the parallels, labeled B, N, L, Z, H, F, D. Tangent segments are drawn between the parallels, labeled AC, DE, FG, HK, LM, NO.</image-desc>

<margin>* Proposition 16 of this book.</margin>
<margin>* Proposition 17 of this book.</margin>
For let us first assume that some of the parallels pass on either side of the center **Z**, and let **G H** be the one closest to the center among those on side **B**, or let it pass through the center itself. Therefore, all tangents contained between **G H** and **B O**, such as **H K**, **L M**, and **N O**, are each less than their respective arcs *. Furthermore, tangent **G F** is less than the following arc **F D** *, and similarly tangent **E D** is less than arc **D A**. Thus, all the tangents placed between **B O** and **C D** are less than the arcs **B H** and **F A**, and consequently altogether less than the sum of arcs **B H** and **H A**, or the whole arc **B A**, which was the first thing to be shown.

Next, we will demonstrate that all the tangents between **B O** and **A** are greater than arc **A N**. Indeed, the parallel **G H** passes either closer to the center **Z** than the parallel **E F**, which I assume to be the nearest...

From these points, the proof is also clearly manifest in other cases, whatever arc of the semi-circumference is taken, since the logic is either the same everywhere or is merely a part of the preceding proof.

### PROPOSITION XXI.

*If a moving object descends in continuous motion through any number of connected inclined planes, and then descends again from an equal height through just as many connected planes, arranged so that each one corresponds in height to each of the former planes, but with a greater inclination <note>Meaning the second set of planes is steeper than the first.</note> than those. I say that the time of descent through the less inclined planes is shorter than the time of descent through the more inclined planes.*

Let there be two series of planes contained between the same horizontal parallels, **A B C D E** and **F G H K L**, and in such a way that every two corresponding planes of each series are enclosed by the same horizontal parallels; let each plane of the series **F G H K L** be more inclined to the horizon <note>Steeper relative to the ground.</note> than the plane corresponding to it in height in the series **A B C D E**. I say that the descent through **A B C D E** is completed in a shorter time than the descent through **F G H K L**.

<summary>Huygens concludes a geometric proof regarding the length of tangents relative to circular arcs and begins a new physical proposition proving that an object falls faster through a series of shallow inclined planes than through a series of steep planes of the same total vertical height.</summary>

For first of all, it is established that the time of descent through *A B* is shorter than the time of descent through *F G*, since the ratio of the times is the same as the ratio of the straight lines *A B* to *F G* <margin>\* Proposition 7 of this work.</margin>\*, and *A B* is shorter than *F G* because of its lesser inclination <note>By "inclination," Huygens refers to the angle or steepness of the slope relative to the horizontal.</note>. Now let the straight lines *C B* and *H G* be extended upwards until they meet the horizontal line *A F* at points *M* and *N*. Thus, the time taken to travel through *B C* after having already traveled through *A B* is equal to the time through the same *B C* after traveling through *M B*,

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing two segmented paths of descent, labeled A-B-C-D-E on the left and F-G-H-K-L on the right, falling from a common horizontal line A-F. Horizontal auxiliary lines connect corresponding vertices (B to G, C to H, D to K, E to L), indicating they are at equal heights. since at point *B* the same speed is attained, whether the object descends through *A B* or through *M B* \*. Similarly, the time through *G H* after *F G* will be equal <margin>\* Proposition 6 of this work.</margin> to the time through the same *G H* after *N G*. Moreover, the ratio of the time through *B C* after *M B* to the time through *G H* after *N G* is as the length of *B C* is to *G H*, or as *C M* is to *H N*, since the times through the whole lengths *M C* and *N H*, and through the parts *M B* and *N G*, share this same ratio \*, <margin>\* Proposition 7 of this work.</margin> and therefore the remaining times do as well. And since *B C* is smaller than *G H* because of its lesser inclination, it is therefore clear that the time through *B C* after *M B* (or after *A B*) is shorter than the time through *G H* after *N G* (or after *F G*).

In a similar manner, it will be shown—by extending *D C* and *K H* upwards until they meet the horizontal line *A F* at *O* and *P*—that the time through *C D* after *A B C* (or after *O C*) is shorter than the time through *H K* after *F G H* (or after *P H*). And finally, the time through *D E* after *A B C D* is shorter than the time through *K L* after *F G H K*. Therefore, the total time of descent through *A B C D E* will be shorter than the time through *F G H K L*. Which was to be demonstrated <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum"</note>.

From this, it is truly manifest—by considering curved lines as being composed of an infinite number of straight lines—that if there were two surfaces inclined according to curved lines of the same height, where at any points of equal height the inclination of one is always greater than the other, a heavy object will descend in a shorter time through the less inclined path than through the more inclined one.

As if there were two surfaces inclined according to the curves *A B* and *C D*, of equal height, and at any points *E* and *F* of equal height, the inclination of *C D* is greater than that of *A B*, that is, so that...

### PROPOSITION XXII.

> *If, in a cycloid <term>cycloid: the curve traced by a point on the rim of a wheel as it rolls along a straight line</term> whose axis stands vertically with the vertex pointing downward, two portions of the curve of equal vertical height are taken, but one of which is closer to the vertex; the time of descent through the upper portion will be shorter than the time through the lower portion.*

...stand as far apart as *E G* and *F K*, which enclose the lower portion *E F*. I say that the time of descent through the curve *B D* will be shorter than the time through *E F*.

For let any point *L* be taken on *B D*, and a point *M* on *E F*, such that the height of *E* above *M* is the same as *B* above *L*. And with a semicircle described on the axis *A C*, let the horizontal lines *L N* and *M O* meet it at *N* and *O*, and let *N A* and *O A* be joined. Thus, since point *N* is higher than point *O*, it is manifest that the line *N A* is less inclined toward the horizontal <note>Original: "minus ad horizontem inclinari." In the geometry of this period, "leaning less toward the horizontal" means the line is steeper, or closer to the vertical.</note> than *O A*. Moreover, the tangent of the curve at point *L* is parallel to *N A* <margin>* Proposition 15 of this work.</margin>, and the tangent of the curve at *M* is parallel to *O A*. Therefore, the curve *B D* at point *L* is less inclined toward the horizontal <note>Meaning it is steeper.</note> than the curve *E F* at point *M*.

If, therefore, the portion *E F*—with its inclination remaining unchanged—is understood to be raised higher as if to *e f*, so that it is contained between the same parallels as portion *B D*, point *M* will be found at *m*, at the same height as point *L*. The inclination of the curve *e f* at point *m* (which is the same as the inclination of the curve *E F* at *M*) will be greater than the inclination of the curve *B D* at *L*. <note>Here, "greater inclination" refers to leaning more toward the horizontal, i.e., being flatter/less steep.</note> Similarly, in any other point of curve *e f*, the inclination will be shown to be greater than that of curve *B D* at an equally high point. Thus, the time of descent through *B D* will be shorter than the time through *e f* <margin>* The preceding Proposition.</margin>, or, what is the same thing, through *E F*. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XXIII.

Let *ABC* be a Cycloid <note>A curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line; Huygens discovered this is the ideal path for a pendulum.</note>, *whose vertex* A *is turned downward, with the axis* AD *erected vertically; and taking any point* B *upon it, let the straight line* BI *be drawn downward from there so as to touch the Cycloid <note>A tangent line.</note>, ending at the horizontal line* AI. *Let the straight line* BF *be drawn perpendicular to the axis, and with* FA *divided in half at* X, *let the semicircle* FHA *be described upon it. *These things being so, I say that the time in which a heavy body <note>Original: "grave." In 17th-century physics, this refers to any object with mass falling under gravity.</note> traverses the line* MN, *at a uniform speed equal to that acquired by descending through the arc of the Cycloid* BG, *is to the time in which the line* OP *is traversed, at a uniform speed equal to half of that acquired by descending through the whole tangent* BI, *as the tangent segment* ST *is to the axis segment* QR.

For let a semicircle *DVA* be described on the axis *AD*, cutting the line *BF* at *V* and *ΣG* at *Φ*, and let *AV* be joined, cutting the lines *OQ*, *PR*, and *GΣ* at *E*, *K*, and *Λ*. Likewise, let *HF*, *HA*, *HX*, and *AΦ* be joined; let the last of these cut the lines *OQ* and *PR* at points *Δ* and *Π*.

The ratio of the speed acquired from fall <term>FA</term><note>Falling from height F to A</note> to the speed from <term>FΣ</term> is in the square root ratio <note>original: *subduplicata ratione*; a common 17th-century term for the ratio of square roots</note> of the lengths FA to FΣ, and therefore the same ratio as FA to FH. Therefore, the ratio of half the speed from FA to the speed from FΣ will be as Fx to FH. Thus, the ratio of the time taken to travel through the segment MN to the time taken through OP will be composed of the ratios of MN to OP, and Fx to FH. Indeed, the first of these ratios, namely MN to OP, will be shown to be the same as FH to HΣ.

<image-desc>
A complex geometric diagram showing a cycloid curve and a circle used for its derivation. A vertical axis AD is labeled with points including F, Σ, and x. A semicircle FHA is drawn on the lower part of the axis. Lines intersect the curve at points M, G, N and B. Tangent lines and horizontal parallels are used to show proportional segments (ΔΠ, εκ, sT, QR). This diagram is the visual proof for Huygens’ "tautochrone" discovery—that a body takes the same time to fall to the bottom of a cycloid regardless of its starting height.
</image-desc>

<margin>
* Previous lemma.
</margin>

For the tangent of the Cycloid BI is parallel to the line νA, and similarly the tangent MGN is parallel to the line ΦA; and therefore the line MN is equal to ΔΠ, and OP is equal to εκ. Therefore, the stated ratio of the line MN to OP is the same as ΔΠ to εκ; that is, ΔA to εA; that is, ΦA to ΛA; which is to say, νA to ΦA. Furthermore, as νA is to AΦ, so FA is to AH; for because the square of νA is equal to the rectangle <note>Meaning the area of a rectangle formed by these two lines</note> DAF, and the square of AΦ is equal to the rectangle DAΣ—which rectangles are to each other as FA to ΣA, that is, as the square of FA to the square of AH—it will therefore also follow that the square of νA is to the square of ΦA as the square of FA is to the square of AH; and also the length νA is to AΦ, as FA is to AH. Therefore, the ratio MN to OP will be the same as FA to AH, which—on account of the similar triangles FAH and FHΣ—is the same as FH to HΣ, as was stated. Thus, the stated ratio of the time through MN to the time through OP is composed of the ratios Fx to FH and FH to HΣ, and therefore it will be the same as Fx (or xH) to HΣ. Now, just as the radius xH is to HΣ, so is the tangent sT to the line QR; for this is easily seen. Therefore, it is established that the time of motion through MN, as we have described it, is to the time through OP as sT is to QR. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XXIV.

Let there be again, as in the preceding proposition, a Cycloid ABC, whose vertex A <note>The "vertex" here refers to the lowest point of the curve, as it is oriented downward.</note> points downward, and let the axis AD be set perpendicular to the horizon; and having taken any point B upon it, let a straight line B ☉ <note>The symbol ☉ denotes a specific point on the horizontal line.</note> be drawn downward touching the Cycloid, meeting the horizontal line A ☉ at ☉: let the straight line BF be drawn perpendicular to the axis, and upon FA let a semicircle FHA be described. Then, let another straight line GE, parallel to FB, intersect the Cycloid at E, the straight line B ☉ at I, the circumference FHA at H, and finally the axis DA at G.

I assert that the time of descent through the cycloidal arc BE is to the time through the tangent BI at half the speed acquired from falling the height B ☉, as the arc FH is to the straight line FG.

For if this is not true, the time through the arc BE will have to the said time through BI either a greater ratio than the arc FH has to the straight line FG, or a lesser one. Let it first have, if possible, a greater ratio.

---

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram illustrating mathematical properties of a cycloid. A vertical axis is labeled with points A (at the bottom), G, R, Q, P, F, and D (at the top). A cycloid curve A-E-B-C originates from point A and curves upwards and to the right. A semicircle FHA is drawn on the lower part of the axis. A tangent line is drawn from point B on the cycloid, passing through point I and meeting a horizontal line extending from A at a point marked with the symbol ☉ (a circle with a center dot). Points on these lines and curves are labeled with Roman letters and Greek letters, including Δ, Σ, Γ, Φ, Ξ, N, M, S, T, V, X, and Y.
</image-desc>

---

Therefore, some time shorter than the time through BE (let this time be *z*) will be to the said time through BI as the arc FH is to the straight line FG. Now, if another point N is taken on the Cycloid above point B, the time through BE after passing through NB <note>This refers to the interval of time the object takes to reach E after it has already traveled from N to B.</note> will be shorter than the time through BE. It is manifest, however, that point N can be taken so close to B that the difference between these times is as small as you like, and therefore smaller than the amount by which time *z* is exceeded by the time through BE. Let it be thus...

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof of Proposition XXIV from the previous page. Huygens is employing a "reductio ad absurdum" (proof by contradiction) to demonstrate that the time it takes for a body to descend along a cycloidal arc is precisely related to the time it takes to travel along a tangent line at a specific speed.</meta>

<header>THE PENDULUM CLOCK, PAGE 53</header>
<sig>G iii</sig>

<margin>
ON MOTION IN A CYCLOID.
</margin>

the point N being chosen in such a way <note>original: "punctum N ita sumptum"</note>. From this, the time through arc BE after falling from N <note>The text refers to the time it takes to travel the distance BE, assuming the object began its fall at point N.</note> will be greater than the time *z*, and will therefore have a greater ratio to the stated time through BI at half the speed acquired from BΘ, than the arc FH has to the straight line FG. Let it, therefore, have the same ratio as the arc FHO has to the straight line FG.

Let FG be divided into equal parts FP, PQ, etc., each of which is smaller than the height of the line NB, and also smaller than the height of the arc HO; it is clear that this can be done. From these points of division, let straight lines be drawn parallel to the base DC and ending at the tangent BΘ, namely PA, QΞ, and so on. At the points where these lines intersect the circumference FH, and also from point H, let tangents be drawn upward as far as the next parallel line, such as Δx, ΓΣ, and so on. Similarly, from the points where the said parallels meet the Cycloid, let tangents be drawn upward such as *sv*, *τm*, etc. By adding one part GR to the straight line FG, equal to those created by the division, and drawing Rφ parallel to DC in the same way, it is clear that it meets the circumference FHA between H and O, because GR is less than the height of point H above O. Now, we shall proceed with the argument as follows.

The time through the tangent *vs* at a uniform speed <note>original: "celeritate æquabili"</note> which would be acquired from the height Bs, is greater than the time of the continuously accelerated motion through the arc *bs* after falling from N. For the speed from Bs is less than the speed from NB, because the height Bs is less than the height NB. But the speed from Bs is assumed to be continued uniformly through the tangent *vs*, while the speed acquired from NB is further accelerated continuously through the arc *bs*; moreover, this arc is shorter than the tangent *vs*, and is more steeply inclined in all its parts than any part of the tangent *vs*. Thus, the time through the tangent *vs* at the speed from Bs will be altogether greater than the time through the arc *bs* after NB. Similarly, the time through the tangent *mτ* at the speed from Bτ will be greater than the time through the arc *sτ* after Ns; and the time through the tangent *πy* at the speed from By will be greater than the time through the arc *τy* after Nτ. And so, the times of the uniform motions through all the tangents down to the lowest one which touches the cycloid at E—with the speeds for each being as great as those acquired by falling from B to their respective points of contact—will together be greater than the time through the arc BE after falling from N. However, these same times would also be less, as we shall now show.

For let the same times of the uniform motions through the tangents of the cycloid be considered again. The time through the tangent *vs* at the speed from Bs, compared to the time through the straight line BΔ at half the speed from FA, is as the tangent of the circumference Δx is to the part...

<summary>Huygens uses a geometric limiting process, dividing the path into infinitesimal segments, to compare the time of descent on a cycloid with the time on a tangent. He argues by contradiction that the ratio of these times must correspond to the ratio between a circular arc and its corresponding vertical height.</summary>

And every quantity among the former is related to the next in the same proportion as every one of the latter is related to its successor; for they are equal to each other in both cases. Moreover, in whatever proportions the former quantities are related to certain others—namely to the tangents of the circle Δ Χ, Γ Σ, etc.—the latter are also related in the same proportions and the same order to certain others—namely to the times of motion, as we have described, along the tangents of the cycloid VS, MT, etc. Therefore, just as all the first quantities together are to all those to which they are compared—that is, as the whole line FG is to all the tangents Χ Δ, Γ Σ, etc.—so is the time in which the whole line BI is traversed at half the speed from B Θ to all the times of the motions, as we have said, along the tangents of the

Yet we have posited that the time through BE after falling from N, compared to the time through BI at half the speed from B Θ, is as the arc FΘ to the straight line FG. Thus, the time through the cycloidal arc BE, compared to the time through the tangent BI at half the speed from BΘ or from FA, does not have a greater ratio than the arc of the circumference FH has to the straight line FG.

Now let it have, if possible, a smaller ratio. Therefore, some time greater than the time through arc BE (let this time be Z) will be to the stated time through BI as the arc FH is to the straight line FG.

...we concluded <note>The Latin text begins with the end of a word, likely "colligimus" or "posuimus," from the previous page.</note> from the beginning that the same time through BI relates [to the other] as arc LH to the straight line FG. It is therefore clear that the time through the cycloidal arc BE compared to the time through the tangent BI with a uniform speed <term>uniform speed|A constant speed, here specifically half of the maximum speed acquired falling from height B.</term> half of that from B ☉, does not have a smaller ratio than arc FH to the straight line FG. But it was also shown not to have a greater ratio. Therefore, it is necessary that it has the same ratio. Which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION XXV.

*In a cycloid whose axis is erected perpendicularly, with the vertex looking downward, the times of descent by which a moving body, released from any point on it, reaches the lowest point of the vertex, are equal to each other; and they have that ratio to the time of a perpendicular fall through the whole axis of the cycloid, which the semi-circumference of a circle has to its diameter.*

Let ABC be a cycloid <note>A cycloid is the curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line. Huygens discovered it is the "isochronous" curve for pendulums.</note> whose vertex A looks downward, and let its axis AD be erected perpendicularly. From any chosen point on the cycloid, such as B, let a moving body descend by its natural weight through the arc BA, or through a surface so curved. I say that the time of this descent is to the time of a fall through the axis DA, as the semi-circumference of a circle is to its diameter. Once this is demonstrated, it will also be established that the times of descent through any arcs of the cycloid ending at A are equal to each other.

Let a semicircle be described upon the axis DA, whose circumference is cut by the straight line BF (parallel to the base DC) at point E; and having joined EA, let BG be drawn parallel to it, which will indeed touch the cycloid at B. Let this same line meet the horizontal line drawn through A at G; and let there also be a semicircle FHA described upon FA.

<margin>
* Proposition 6 of Galileo’s *On Accelerated Motion*.
</margin>
Therefore, by the preceding proposition, the time of descent through the cycloidal arc BA is to the time of uniform motion through the straight line BG with half the speed acquired from height BG, as the arc of the semicircle FHA is to the straight line FA. But the time of the said uniform motion through BG is equal to the time of naturally accelerated descent through the same line BG—or through EA, which is parallel and equal to it—that is, the time of accelerated descent through the axis DA*. Therefore, the time through arc BA will also be to the time of descent through the axis DA as the semi-circumference FHA is to the diameter FA. Which was to be demonstrated.

<sig>H</sig>

<summary>Huygens proves the "isochronism" of the cycloid: the time it takes for an object to slide to the bottom of a cycloidal path is the same regardless of the starting height. He calculates this time as being the time of a vertical fall through the diameter multiplied by the ratio of a circle's half-circumference to its diameter (π/2).</summary>

### PROPOSITION XXVI.

**W**ith the same conditions established, if an additional horizontal line HI is drawn which intersects the arc BA at I and the circumference FHA at H: I say that the time through arc BI relates to the time through arc IA (after BI has been traversed) in the same ratio as arc of the circumference FH relates to arc HA.

For let the straight line HI meet the tangent <term>tangent|A straight line that touches a curve at a single point without crossing it</term> BG at K, and the axis DA at L. Thus, the time through arc BA is to the time of uniform motion through BG (with half the speed acquired from BG) as the arc FHA is to the straight line FA \*. However, the time of said uniform motion through BG is to the time of uniform motion through BK (with the same half-speed from BG) as the length of BG is to BK—that is, as FA is to FL. Furthermore, the time of uniform motion with the stated speed through BK is to the time through arc BI as FL is to arc FH \*. Therefore, by equality, the time through arc BA will be to the time through BI as arc FHA is to FH. And by dividing and converting the ratios, the time through BI is to the time through IA (following BI) as arc FH is to HA. Which was to be demonstrated <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum," often abbreviated as Q.E.D.</note>.

<meta>This page marks a major transition in the treatise. While the previous page concluded the analysis of motion along a cycloid (Proposition XXVI), Page 59 begins Part Three of the work. Here, Huygens moves from pure physics and the properties of the cycloid into the underlying geometry of "evolutes"—the mathematical theory of how one curve can be "unrolled" from another. This theory is what allowed him to design the cheeks of his pendulum clock to ensure a perfectly cycloidal path.</meta>

<header>ON THE EVOLUTION OF CURVED LINES.</header>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
<note>original: "Horologii Oscillatorii"</note>
## PART THREE.
->On the evolution and measurement of curved lines.<-
### DEFINITIONS.

->I.<-

A line "bent in one direction" <note>Huygens is defining what we would now call a convex or concave curve that lacks points of inflection—it doesn't "S-curve" back and forth.</note> shall be the name for a line which all its straight tangent lines touch on the same side. If, however, the line contains certain straight portions, these portions themselves, when extended, are considered to be the tangents.

->II.<-

When two such lines originate from the same point, and the convexity <note>The outer "bulge" of the curve.</note> of one faces the concavity <note>original: "cavitatem," the inner "hollow" of the curve.</note> of the other—as seen in the accompanying figure with curves ABC and ADE—both are said to be concave in the same direction.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing two curves, ABC and ADE, originating from a common point A. Curve ABC is the lower one, passing through point B and terminating at C. Curve ADE is the upper one, passing through D and terminating at E. A vertical line segment connects C and E. A diagonal line segment connects D and B. This visualizes the spatial relationship between two curves where the convexity of one faces the concavity of the other.</image-desc>

->III.<-

If a thread or a flexible line <term>flexible line|Huygens uses the term "filum seu linea flexilis." This is the core of his "evolution" (unrolling) theory: imagining a string being unwrapped from a curved shape to trace a new path.</term> is understood to be wrapped around a line that is concave in one direction, and while one end of the thread remains fixed to it...

<sig>H ij</sig>

<summary>This page introduces Part Three of Huygens' work, shifting from the physics of oscillation to the geometry of "evolutes." It establishes the formal definitions of curvature and the conceptual framework of unwinding a thread from a curve to generate another line.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the definitions begun on page 59. Huygens is establishing the mathematical vocabulary for his theory of "evolution"—the process of unwinding a thread from a fixed curve to trace a new one. This geometric foundation is essential for his later proof that "cycloidal cheeks" will make a pendulum swing in a perfect cycloid, ensuring the clock's accuracy regardless of the swing's width.</meta>

<header>CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS</header>

<margin>ON THE EVOLUTION OF CURVED LINES.</margin>
...attached, and let the other end be pulled away so that the part which is loosened always remains taut; it is clear that another curve is described by this end of the thread. Let this be called the *Curve Described by Evolution.* <note>In modern geometry, this is called the "involute."</note>

## IV.

But that curve around which the thread was wrapped shall be called the *Evolute*. In the figure above <note>Huygens refers to the diagram on the previous page where ABC and ADE were first introduced.</note>, *ABC* is the evolute, and *ADE* is the curve described by the evolution of *ABC*; namely, when the end of the thread moves from *A* to *D*, the part of the thread extended is the straight line *DB*, while the remaining part *BC* is still applied to the curve *ABC*. It is evident, moreover, that *DB* touches <note>original: "tangere"; meaning it is tangent to</note> the evolute at *B*.

## PROPOSITION I.

Every straight line which touches the evolute will meet the line described by evolution at right angles. <note>original: "angulos rectos"; this is a fundamental property where the unwinding thread is always perpendicular to the path of its tip.</note>

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the theory of evolutes and involutes. It features two curves: a smaller arc (the evolute AB) and a larger, outer arc (the involute AH) generated from it. A straight line segment DC acts as a tangent to a portion of the evolute (AD) at point D and meets the involute at point C. Another line CE is drawn perpendicular to DC at point C. Points labeled include A, B, C, D, E, F, G, and H, demonstrating the perpendicular relationship between the tangent to the evolute and the curve described by evolution.</image-desc>

Let *AB* be the evolute, and *AH* be the curve described by its evolution. Let the straight line *FDC*, which is tangent to the curve *AD* at *D*, meet the curve *ACH* at *C*. I say that it meets it at right angles: that is, if the straight line *CE* is drawn perpendicular to *CD*, I say that it touches the curve *ACH* at *C*. For since *DC* touches the evolute at *D*, it is apparent that it represents the position of the thread at the moment its end reaches *C*. If, therefore, we show that the thread, in the entire remaining description of the curve *ACH*, nowhere reaches the straight line *CE* except at point *C*, it will be clear—

ON THE

OF CURVES.

...it will be manifest that the straight line *CE* touches the curve *ACH* at that same point. <note>original: "contingere"; in a geometric context, this means the line is tangent to the curve.</note>

Let any point besides *C* be taken on the curve *AC*, and let it be *H*. First, let this point be further from the beginning of the evolution *A* than point *C* is. Understand the "free" part of the thread <note>The portion of the thread that has been unwound from the source curve.</note> to be *HG* when its extremity reaches *H*. Therefore, *HG* touches the line *AB* at point *G*. And while the part of the curve *CH* is being described, since the arc *DG* has been unwound, it follows that *CD* (extended from the direction of *D*) will meet *HG* at point *F*. Furthermore, let it be supposed that *GH* meets the straight line *CE* at *E*. Now, because any two sides of a triangle—*DF* and *FG*—together are greater than the third side *DG* (whether that line *DG* be a curve or a straight line), it follows by adding the straight line *DC* to both sides that the lines *CF* and *FG* together are greater than the line *CD* and the arc *DG* combined. 

But because of the nature of "evolution," <note>Huygens refers to his earlier definition: the length of the unwound thread equals the length of the arc it was wrapped around plus any initial straight segment.</note> it is evident that the straight line *HG* is equal to the sum of the straight line *CD* and the line *DG*. Therefore, the two lines *CF* and *FG* together will also be greater than the straight line *HG*; and if the common segment *FG* is taken away from both, *CF* will be greater than *HF*. However, *FE* is greater than *FC*, because the angle *C* of triangle *FCE* is a right angle. <note>In a right-angled triangle, the hypotenuse (FE) is always longer than the legs (FC).</note> Therefore, *FE* is altogether greater than *FH*. From this, it appears that on this side of point *C*, the end of the thread does not reach the straight line *CE*.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the proof. A curve labeled A-C-H represents the path of the thread's end. A second curve A-D-G-B represents the "evolute" from which the thread is unwound. Various straight lines (CE, HG, CD) represent the thread at different stages of being unwrapped, forming triangles (FCE, FGH) to demonstrate the spatial relationship between the curve and its tangent.</image-desc>

Now let the point *H* be nearer to the beginning of the evolution *A* than point *C* is. Let the position of the thread be *HG* at the moment its extremity is at *H*, and let the straight lines *DG* and *DH* be drawn, of the latter of which meets the straight line *CE* at *E*. It is clear, however, that the straight line *DG* cannot be in a direct line with *HG*, and thus *HGD* will form a triangle. Now, because the straight line *DG* is either less than the arc *DKG*, or equal to it (if, for instance, the part of the evolute *DG* were a straight line); if *GH* is added to both, the straight lines *DG* and *GH* together will be less than, or...

<summary>Huygens provides a geometric proof to demonstrate that the curve generated by a thread unwinding from another curve (an involute) is always tangent to the thread itself and remains on one side of its tangent line. This establishes the mathematical rigor for how "pendulum cheeks" must be shaped to control the swing of the clock.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, evolution, evolute, involute, tangent, Christiaan Huygens, right angle, triangle inequality, mathematical proof</keywords>

...are equal to those two, namely *DKG* and *GH*, or equal to the straight line *DC*. <note>Huygens is comparing the length of the unwound thread to the original straight segment.</note> However, the straight line *DH* is shorter than the two straight lines *DG* and *GH* combined. Therefore, this line *DH* will certainly be shorter than the straight line *DC*. But *DE* is longer than *DC*, because in the triangle *DCE*, the angle *C* is a right angle. <note>In a right-angled triangle, the hypotenuse is always the longest side.</note> Therefore, *DH* is much shorter than *DE*. The point *H*, which is the end of the thread *GH*, is thus situated within the angle *DCE*. From this, it appears that nowhere between *A* and *C* does the curve reach the straight line *CE*. Therefore, *CE* touches the curve *AHC* at *C*; and accordingly *DC*, to which *CE* was drawn as a perpendicular, meets the curve at right angles. <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum"—which was to be demonstrated.</note>

From this, it is also manifest that the curve *AHC* is bent in one direction, and is concave in the same direction as the curve *AGB* itself, by whose evolution it was described. For all the tangent lines of the curve *AHC* fall outside the space *DGAHC*, while all the tangents of the line *AGD* fall inside the said space. From this, it is clear that the concavity of *AHC* faces the convexity of *AGD*. <note>This explains the relationship between what we now call the "evolute" and the "involute"—the new curve wraps around the old one.</note>

### PROPOSITION II.

*Every finite curved line, concave in one direction, such as ABD, can be divided into so many parts that if straight lines subtending each part are drawn (such as AB, BC, CD), and if likewise from each point of division and from the extremity of the curve itself, straight lines are drawn tangent to the curve (such as AN, BO, CP) which meet those lines that stand at right angles to the curve at the next following points of division (such as the lines BN, CO, DP); I say, that each subtended line has to the curve’s perpendicular adjacent to it (such as AB to BN, BC to CO, CD to DP) a ratio greater than any proposed ratio.* <note>Huygens is preparing to use a limit argument, showing that as the segments of the curve become smaller, the relationship between the chord and the perpendicular follows a specific rule.</note>

For let there be a given <term>ratio (a relationship between two magnitudes)</term> of the line *EF* to *FG*, which are joined at a right angle at *F*, and let the straight line *GEH* be drawn.

Imagine first that the curve *ABD* is cut into such tiny parts by points *B* and *C* that the tangents which touch the curve at every two points closest to each other meet one another at angles that are each greater than the angle *FEH*; such as the angles *AKB*, *BLC*, *CMD*. <note>Huygens notes that the possibility of this "is more evident than needs a demonstration," assuming the curve is smooth.</note> Having now drawn the <term>subtended lines (chords connecting two points on a curve)</term> *AB*, *BC*, *CD*, and having erected the perpendiculars to the curve *BN*, *CO*, *DP*, which meet the extended tangents *AK*, *BL*, *CM* at *N, O, P*: I say that the individual ratios of the straight lines *AB* to *BN*, *BC* to *CO*, *CD* to *DP*, are greater than the ratio of *EF* to *FG*.

<summary>Huygens completes the proof that the unwinding thread is always perpendicular to the involute curve and introduces a second proposition regarding the ratios between chords and perpendiculars in subdivided curves.</summary>

For because angle *AKB* is greater than angle *HEF*, its supplementary angle <note>original: "residuus illius ad duos rectos" — the angle needed to complete a straight line of 180 degrees.</note>, namely angle *NKB*, will be less than angle *GEF*.

Moreover, angle *B* of triangle *KBN* is a right angle, just as is angle *F* in triangle *EFG*. Therefore, the ratio of *KB* to *BN* will be greater than the ratio of *EF* to *FG*. <note>Huygens is using the geometric properties of triangles to compare the steepness or "slope" of different segments.</note> But *AB* is greater than *KB*, because angle *K* in triangle *AKB* is obtuse; for it is greater than angle *HEF*, which is obtuse by construction. Therefore, the ratio of *AB* to *BN* will be greater than the ratio of *KB* to *BN*, and consequently entirely greater than the ratio of *EF* to *FG*. In the same way, it will be shown that the ratios of *BC* to *CO*, and *CD* to *DP*, are also greater than the ratio of *EF* to *FG*. Thus, the proposition stands as established.

### PROPOSITION III.

*Two curves bent in one direction and concave toward the same sides cannot start from the same point if they are so related to each other that every straight line which meets one of them at right angles also meets the other at right angles.*

For let there be, if it is possible, curved lines of this kind, *ACE* and *AGK*, sharing a common endpoint *A*. And having taken any point *K* on the outer curve, 

let the straight line *KE* be drawn from there, meeting the curve *AGK* at right angles, and therefore also meeting the curve *ACE* at right angles. <note>Huygens is setting up a "proof by contradiction." He assumes two such curves could exist to show that it leads to a logical impossibility.</note>

<summary>Huygens completes the proof of Proposition II using geometric ratios and begins Proposition III, which asserts that a curve is uniquely defined by its set of normal (perpendicular) lines.</summary>

Now, a certain straight line <term>Q</term> <note>Huygens uses "Q" as a reference length for his ratios.</note> may be taken which is greater than the curve <term>K G A</term>. Let it be understood that the curve <term>K G A</term> itself is divided, as was said in the preceding proposition, into so many parts by the points <term>H</term>, <term>G</term>, and <term>F</term>, that the individual <term>chords</term> <note>original: "subtensæ" — straight lines connecting two points on a curve.</note> <term>K H</term>, <term>H G</term>, <term>G F</term>, and <term>F A</term> have a greater ratio to the <term>perpendiculars</term> <note>original: "perpendiculares" — lines meeting the curve at a 90-degree angle.</note> of the curve adjacent to them—namely <term>H M</term>, <term>G N</term>, <term>F O</term>, and <term>A P</term>—than the line <term>Q</term> has to the straight line <term>K E</term>. Therefore, all the said chords taken together will also have a greater ratio to all the said perpendiculars than <term>Q</term> has to <term>K E</term>.

Now, let these same perpendiculars be extended so that they meet the curve <term>A C E</term> at points <term>D</term>, <term>C</term>, and <term>B</term>, which they must do at right angles according to our hypothesis. The line <term>K E</term> will now be less than <term>M D</term>. For, if the line <term>E L</term> is drawn perpendicular to <term>K E</term>, then because <term>K E</term> meets the curve <term>E C A</term> at right angles, <term>E L</term> will be a <term>tangent</term> <note>A line that touches a curve at a single point without crossing it there.</note> to the curve <term>A C E</term>, and it will necessarily meet the straight line <term>M D</term> at some point between <term>D</term> and <term>M</term>. From this, since <term>K E</term> is the shortest of all lines falling between the parallel lines <term>E L</term> and <term>K M</term>, it will be less than <term>M L</term>, and consequently entirely less than <term>M D</term>.

In the same way, <term>H D</term> will be shown to be less than <term>N C</term>, <term>G C</term> less than <term>O B</term>, and <term>F B</term> less than <term>P A</term>. Since, therefore, <term>P A</term> is greater than <term>F B</term>, the two lines <term>P A</term> and <term>O F</term> together will be greater than <term>O B</term>. Likewise, since <term>O B</term> is greater than <term>G C</term>, the two together <term>O B</term> and <term>N G</term> will be greater than <term>N C</term>. But the two lines <term>P A</term> and <term>O F</term> were already greater than <term>O B</term>. Thus, the three together <term>P A</term>, <term>O F</term>, and <term>N G</term> will be entirely greater than <term>N C</term>. Again, because <term>N C</term> is greater than <term>H D</term>, the two together <term>N C</term> and <term>M H</term> will be greater than <term>M D</term>. From which it follows that if in place of <term>N C</term> we take these three greater lines <term>P A</term>, <term>O F</term>, and <term>N G</term>, then these four—<term>P A</term>, <term>O F</term>, <term>N G</term>, and <term>M H</term>—will be entirely greater than <term>M D</term>. Consequently, they are also entirely greater than the straight line <term>K E</term>, because <term>M D</term> was itself greater than <term>K E</term>.

Now, we stated that all the chords <term>A F</term>, <term>F G</term>, <term>G H</term>, and <term>H K</term> have a greater ratio to all the perpendiculars <term>P A</term>, <term>O F</term>, <term>N G</term>, and <term>M H</term> than the line <term>Q</term> has to <term>K E</term>. Therefore, since <term>K E</term> is even smaller than the sum of those perpendiculars, the said chords will have an entirely greater ratio to <term>K E</term> than <term>Q</term> has to <term>K E</term>. It follows that the chords added together will be greater than the straight line <term>Q</term>. However, this line <term>Q</term> was originally chosen to be greater than the curve <term>A G K</term> itself. Therefore, the chords <term>A F</term>, <term>F G</term>, <term>G K</term>, and <term>H K</term> together would be greater than the curve <term>A G K</term> whose parts they connect; <note>In geometry, a straight chord is always shorter than the circular or curved arc it spans.</note> which is absurd, since each individual chord must be shorter than its own arc. Therefore, there cannot be two curved lines that relate to each other in the manner described. <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum" — Which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### PROPOSITION IV.

*If two lines go out from the same point, both bent in one direction and concave toward the same side, and are compared to each other in such a way...*

<summary>Huygens concludes a complex geometric proof showing that it is impossible for two distinct curves to share the same set of perpendiculars, effectively proving that an evolute uniquely determines its involute. He then introduces a new proposition concerning curves originating from a single point.</summary>

<summary>This page presents a formal geometric proof stating that if a series of tangent lines from one curve always intersect a second curve at right angles, then the second curve is the unique product of "unrolling" a string from the first. This principle of "evolution" is foundational to Huygens' work on pendulums and clock accuracy.</summary>
<keywords>Geometry, evolution of curves, involute, evolute, tangents, perpendicularity, Christiaan Huygens, mathematical proof, Horologium Oscillatorium</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof regarding the properties of "evolution." Having established the general principle that an involute curve meets the tangents of its evolute at right angles, Huygens now applies this specifically to the cycloid—the curve of fastest descent and the key to his pendulum clock design.</meta>

## CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS' PROPOSITION V. ON THE EVOLUTION OF CURVED LINES.

*If a straight line touches a <term>cycloid</term> <note>A curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line.</note> at its vertex, and upon this line, acting as a base, another cycloid is constructed that is similar and equal to the first, starting from the point of that same vertex; then any straight line tangent to the lower cycloid will meet the upper portion placed above it at right angles.*

Let the straight line **A G** touch the cycloid **A B C** at its vertex **A**. Upon this line, as a base, let another similar cycloid **A E F** be constructed, whose vertex is **F**. Furthermore, let the straight line **B K** touch the cycloid **A B C** at **B**. I say that this line, when extended, meets the cycloid **A E F** at right angles.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing two cycloid curves. One cycloid (ABC) is oriented downwards with its vertex at A. A second, identical but inverted cycloid (AEF) starts from point A and extends upwards, with its vertex at F. The diagram includes horizontal lines AG and DN, vertical axis AD, and circles AHD and KM used in the construction of the cycloids. Various lines connect points A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, K, M, and N, illustrating tangents and normal lines to the curves.</image-desc>

<margin>
* Proposition 15 of Part 2.
</margin>
For let the <term>generating circle</term> <note>The circle used to "trace" the cycloid curve.</note> **A H D** be described around **A D**, the axis of the cycloid **A B C**. Let **B H**, parallel to the base, meet this circle at **H**, and let **H A** be joined. Since **B K** touches the cycloid at **B**, it is established that it is parallel to the straight line **H A** <note>Huygens refers to a previous proof where he showed the relationship between the tangent and the generating circle's chords.</note>. 

<margin>
* Proposition 14 of Part 2.
</margin>
Thus, **A H B K** is a <term>parallelogram</term> <note>A four-sided shape where opposite sides are parallel and equal.</note>, and consequently **A K** is equal to **H B**, which is to say, equal to the arc **A H**. Furthermore, let a circle **K M** now be described, equal to the generating circle (that is, to **A H D** itself), which touches the base **A G** at **K**, and let it intersect the extended straight line **B K** at point **E**. Since **B K E** is parallel to **A H**, and therefore angle **E K A** is equal to angle **K A H**, it is clear that the extended line **B K** cuts off an arc from circle **K M** equal to that which the straight line **A H** cuts off from circle **A H D**. Thus, the arc **K E** is equal to the arc **A H**, which is equal to the straight line **H B**, which is equal to the straight line **K A**. Hence...

<summary>Huygens begins a formal geometric proof to demonstrate that a cycloid is its own "evolute"—specifically, that the tangent of one cycloid serves as the normal (perpendicular) to an identical, inverted cycloid.</summary>
<keywords>cycloid, evolution, tangent, normal, generating circle, geometry, Christiaan Huygens, parallelogram</keywords>

<margin>
ON THE <term>EVOLUTION</term> <note>In geometry, "evolution" is the process of unwinding a string from a curved shape to create a new curve, called an involute.</note> OF CURVED LINES. * Proposition 15 of Part 2.
</margin>
Indeed, it follows from the property of the <term>cycloid</term> <note>The curve traced by a point on the rim of a wheel as it rolls along a straight line.</note> that when the <term>generating circle</term> **M K** touched the ruler at **κ**, the point describing the curve was at **E**. Therefore, the straight line **K E** meets the cycloid at **E** at right angles <note>original: "ad angulos rectos"</note>. Furthermore, **K E** is the extension of the line **B K** itself. Thus, it is clear that the extended line **B K** meets the cycloid at right angles; which was to be demonstrated.

### PROPOSITION VI.

*Through the evolution of a semi-cycloid, starting from its vertex, another semi-cycloid is described that is equal and similar to the one being unwound, and whose base lies on the straight line that touches the vertex of the original cycloid.*

<margin>
* Proposition 4 of this work.
</margin>
Let **A B C** be a semi-cycloid upon which another similar one, **A E F**, is superimposed, just as in the preceding proposition. I say that if a flexible line <note>Such as a string or thread.</note>, applied around the semi-cycloid **A B C**, is unwound—beginning the unwinding from point **A**—its extremity will describe the semi-cycloid **A E F**. For since the semi-cycloids **A B C** and **A E F** emerge from point **A**, are bent in the same direction, are both concave on the same side, and are moreover arranged so that all tangents of the semi-cycloid **A B C** meet the semi-cycloid **A E F** at right angles, it follows that the latter is described by the evolution of the former, starting from the endpoint **A**. Which was to be demonstrated.

And it is apparent that if we place a half-cycloid, a "twin" to **A B C**, in the opposite position on the other side of the line **C G** (labeled here as **C N**), then by its evolution—or even while the thread, already extended at **C F**, is wrapped back around it—another semi-cycloid will be described by the end of the thread, which together with the previous **A E F** forms a complete, whole cycloid.

And from these facts, along with Proposition 25 on the <term>descent of heavy bodies</term> <note>Huygens refers to his discovery that an object takes the same amount of time to slide down a cycloidal path regardless of where it starts.</note>, what was said earlier in the "Construction of the Clock" regarding the uniform motion of the pendulum is now made manifest. For it is clear that a pendulum, suspended and moved between two <term>plates</term> <note>These are the "cycloidal cheeks" Huygens added to the top of his clocks to guide the pendulum string.</note> bent according to a semi-cycloid, describes an arc of a cycloid with its motion; and consequently, any of its swings—no matter the width—are completed in equal times. For it does not matter whether a moving object is carried upon a physical surface curved into a cycloid, or whether, tied to a thread, it traverses that same line in the air, since in both cases it has the same freedom and the same inclination toward motion at all points of the curve.

### PROPOSITION VII.

*A cycloid line is four times as long as its axis, or the diameter of its generating circle.*

I ij <note>Printer's signature mark.</note>

<summary>Huygens proves that the "involute" of a cycloid is another identical cycloid. He uses this geometric property to explain why a pendulum swinging between cycloidal-shaped "cheeks" will maintain perfectly equal timing regardless of the swing's amplitude.</summary>

<keywords>cycloid, evolution, involute, pendulum clock, tautochrone, equal time, generating circle, geometry of motion, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

<margin>
ON THE <term>EVOLUTION</term> <note>The process of "unrolling" or unwinding a string from a curve to trace a new one.</note> OF CURVED LINES.
</margin>
For, the previous figure being repeated: since after the whole semi-cycloid **A B C** has been unwound, the string occupies the straight line **C F**, which is double [the length of] **A D**, because the axes <note>The "axis" of a cycloid is the diameter of the circle used to generate it; here, it represents the vertical height of the curve.</note> of the cycloids **A B C** and **A E F** are equal; it appears that the semi-cycloid **A B C** itself, being equal to the string applied around it, is double its own axis **A D**, and therefore the entire cycloid is four times its axis.

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram showing the properties and evolution of a cycloid. It features a rectangle D-C-N-G. It also appears that the tangent **B E**, which represents the extended part of the string (previously applied to the curved part **B A**), is equal in length to that part. Moreover, **B E** is double the length of **B K** (or **A H**), because it was shown in the fifth proposition that **K E** is equal to **A H**. Therefore, the part of the cycloid **A B** will be double the straight line **A H** (or **B K**); provided, of course, that **B H** is parallel to the base of the cycloid: and this holds true wherever point **B** is taken upon it.

The distinguished English geometer <note>Christopher Wren (1632–1723), though famous today as the architect of St. Paul's Cathedral, was one of the leading mathematicians and astronomers of his age.</note> Christopher Wren was the first to find this measurement of the cycloid, though by a very different method; he then confirmed it with an elegant demonstration, which was published in the book on the cycloid by the most famous <note>John Wallis (1616–1703) was an English mathematician partially responsible for the development of infinitesimal calculus.</note> John Wallis. Concerning this same line, many other most beautiful discoveries of the mathematicians of our time also exist, for which the primary occasion was provided by certain problems proposed by <note>Blaise Pascal (1623–1662), the French mathematician and philosopher, who famously challenged the mathematical world with a set of problems regarding the cycloid under the pseudonym "Amos Dettonville."</note> Blaise Pascal, a Frenchman, who excelled in these studies. He, reviewing both his own discoveries and those of others, says that <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a monk who acted as the central hub for scientific correspondence in Europe during the 17th century.</note> Mersenne was the first of all to notice this line in the nature of things. [He also says] that <note>Gilles de Roberval (1602–1675), a French mathematician who worked extensively on the properties of curves and the concept of the "infinitely small."</note> Roberval first defined its tangents—

...measured, and that its planes and solids had been dimensioned. Likewise, he <note>original: "viffe... invenisse," referring to Blaise Pascal.</note> found the centers of gravity of both the plane and its parts. Christopher Wren was the first to provide a straight line equal in length to the cycloid curve. I also first discovered the absolute measurement of that portion of the cycloid which is cut off by a line parallel to the base, passing through a point on the axis located one-fourth of the way from the vertex; that portion, indeed, is equal to half of an equilateral hexagon inscribed within the generating circle. Finally, he <note>Pascal</note> determined the centers of gravity of the solids and semi-solids formed by it, both around the base and around the axis, as well as their parts. He also found the center of gravity of the line itself (though this was after receiving the measurement from Wren), and the measurement of the convex surfaces by which those solids and their parts are bounded, along with the centers of gravity of those surfaces. And lastly, he found the dimensions of the curves of any cycloid, whether <term>prolate</term> <note>A "stretched" cycloid formed by a point outside the circle.</note> or <term>curtate</term> <note>A "compressed" cycloid formed by a point inside the circle.</note>: that is, those described by a point taken inside or outside the circumference of the generating circle. Indeed, the demonstrations of these things were published by Pascal. Alongside these, the famous Wallis also set forth his own very subtle reflections on the same line, contending that he had found all those same things by his own effort and had solved the problems proposed by Pascal. The very learned Lalouvère <note>original: "Lovera," referring to Simon de La Loubère.</note> claims the same for himself. How much is truly owed to each, let the learned judge from their writings. For my part, I have related the preceding points only because such extraordinary discoveries did not seem right to pass over in silence; it is thanks to them that, of all lines, none is now better or more thoroughly understood than the cycloid. However, I have chosen to test my own method, which I used in measuring this curve, on other curves as well, which I shall now proceed to discuss.

### PROPOSITION VIII.

*To show by the evolution of what line a parabola is described.*

Let there be a <term>paraboloid</term> **A B** <note>In modern terms, this is a semi-cubical parabola, defined here by the property $x^3 = ay^2$.</note>, whose axis is **A D** and vertex is **A**; let its property be such that for an <term>ordinate</term> <note>A vertical line segment from the curve to the axis.</note> **B D** applied to the axis, the cube of the <term>abscissa</term> <note>The distance along the axis from the vertex.</note> **A D** to the vertex is equal to a solid having a square base **D B** and a height equal to a certain given line **M**; this curve was already known to earlier geometers. Let the line **A E** be joined in a straight line with the axis **D E**, such that **A E** is 8/27 of the length of **M**. Now, if a continuous string is applied around **E A B**, and it begins to be unwound from **E**, I say that the curve described...

<summary>Huygens acknowledges the contributions of Pascal, Wren, and Wallis to the study of the cycloid before introducing Proposition VIII, where he begins demonstrating how a parabola can be generated by unwinding a string from a specific higher-order curve (a semi-cubical parabola).</summary>
<keywords>history of mathematics, Blaise Pascal, Christopher Wren, John Wallis, Lalouvère, cycloid properties, evolution of curves, semi-cubical parabola, paraboloid, geometric discovery</keywords>

...[that the curve obtained] from the evolution is the parabola <term>EF</term>, whose axis is <term>EAG</term>, its vertex is <term>E</term>, and its <term>latus rectum</term> <note>original: "latus rectum"; the parameter that defines the width of a conic section.</note> is equal to double the length of <term>EA</term>.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing lines and curves on a plane. A vertical axis contains points E, A, K, G, and D. A curve AB extends from the vertical axis at D to a point B. A second curve, a parabola labeled EF, originates at E. Line segments BG, GF, FH, and FK connect various points on the curves and axis. A separate vertical line segment is labeled M.</image-desc>

For if any point <term>B</term> is taken on the curve <term>AB</term>, let a straight line <term>BG</term> be drawn which is tangent to the curve at that point, meeting the axis <term>EA</term> at <term>G</term>. And from <term>G</term> let <term>GF</term> be further extended so that it meets the parabola <term>EF</term> at right angles at <term>F</term>; and let <term>FH</term> be perpendicular to <term>GF</term>, which will touch the parabola at <term>F</term>; and finally, let <term>FK</term> be applied as an <term>ordinate</term> <note>original: "ordinatim"; a line segment drawn from a point on a curve perpendicular to the axis.</note> to the axis <term>EG</term>.

Therefore, <term>KG</term> is equal to half of the <term>latus rectum</term>, which is the segment <term>EA</term> itself; and thus, by adding or subtracting <term>AK</term> from both sides, <term>EK</term> will be equal to <term>AG</term>. Moreover, <term>AG</term> is one-third of <term>AD</term>, because <term>BG</term> is tangent to the <term>paraboloid</term> <note>Huygens uses "paraboloid" here to refer to a semi-cubical parabola (a curve where $y^3 \propto x^2$), not the three-dimensional surface.</note> at point <term>B</term>: for this can easily be demonstrated from the nature of this curve. Therefore, <term>EK</term> is also equal to one-third of <term>AD</term>: and <term>KH</term>, which by the nature of a parabola is double <term>KE</term>, will be equal to two-thirds of <term>AD</term>. Thus, the cube of <term>KH</term> is equal to $\frac{8}{27}$ of the cube of <term>AD</term>, which is to say, a solid having the square of <term>DB</term> as its base and a height equal to $\frac{8}{27}$ of <term>M</term>, which is <term>AE</term> itself. For this reason, as the square of <term>DB</term> is to the square of <term>KH</term>, so will the length of <term>KH</term> be to <term>AE</term> (that is, to <term>KG</term>). However, <term>KH</term> was equal to $\frac{2}{3}$ of <term>AD</term>, which is the segment <term>GD</term> itself. Therefore, as the square of <term>BD</term> is to the square of <term>DG</term>, so is <term>HK</term> to <term>KG</term>. Furthermore, as <term>HK</term> is to <term>KG</term>, so is the square of <term>FK</term> to the square of <term>KG</term>. Therefore, just as the square of <term>BD</term> is to the square of <term>DG</term>, so is the square of <term>FK</term> to the square of <term>KG</term>. And consequently, as the length of <term>BD</term> is to <term>DG</term>, so is <term>FK</term> to <term>KG</term>. From which it follows that <term>BGF</term> is a straight line. But <term>GF</term> meets the parabola <term>EF</term> at right angles. Therefore, it appears that <term>BG</term>, the tangent of the <term>paraboloid</term>, when extended, meets that same parabola at

### PROPOSITION IX.

**To find a straight line equal to a given portion of a paraboloid curve; specifically, that in which the squares of the ordinates applied to the axis are to each other as the cubes of the abscissas from the vertex.** <note>This describes a "semi-cubical parabola," defined by the equation $y^2 = kx^3$.</note>

How this may be done is evident from the preceding proposition. Indeed, the parabola <term>EF</term> is not required for the construction, which is performed as follows. Given any part of this paraboloid <term>AB</term>, for which it is necessary to find an equal straight line, let the tangent <term>BG</term> be drawn at point <term>B</term>, meeting the axis <term>AG</term> at <term>G</term>. It will be tangent if <term>AG</term> is one-third of <term>AD</term>, the segment intercepted between

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a vertical axis labeled with points G, A, E, and D. A curve AB (the semi-cubical parabola) originates at point A. A tangent line is drawn from point B to point G on the axis. A second curve (a standard parabola) is shown as EF. Horizontal lines represent ordinates, and various line segments (M, NF) are used to demonstrate the construction of a straight line equivalent to the length of the curve AB.</image-desc>

the vertex and the <term>ordinate</term> <note>original: "ordinatim applicatam"; a vertical line segment from the curve to the axis.</note> <term>BD</term>. Further, taking <term>AE</term> equal to 8/27 of the line <term>M</term> (which is the <term>latus rectum</term> <note>original: "latus rectum"; the parameter that defines the specific shape/width of the curve.</note> of the paraboloid <term>AB</term>), let <term>EF</term> be drawn parallel to <term>BG</term>, and let it meet the line <term>AF</term> (which is parallel to <term>BD</term>) at <term>F</term>. Now, if the segment <term>NF</term>—the excess of the straight line <term>EF</term> over <term>EA</term>—is added to the straight line <term>BG</term>, a straight line will be obtained equal to the curve <term>AB</term>. The proof of this is easily perceived from what was said before.

Therefore, the curve <term>AB</term> always exceeds the tangent <term>BG</term> by as much as the straight line <term>EF</term> exceeds the straight line <term>EA</term>.

But here we have again encountered a line whose length others have already measured before. Specifically, that which in the year 1659 Johannes Heuraet of Haarlem <note>Hendrick van Heuraet (1633–1660), a Dutch mathematician who was the first to publish a method for rectifying a curve.</note> showed to be equal to a straight line; his proof was added following the commentaries of Johannes Schooten <note>Frans van Schooten, who edited and promoted Descartes' work.</note> on Descartes' *Geometry*, published in that same year. And he indeed was the very first of all to [rectify] a curved line, from among those whose points are geometric <note>original: "geometricarum"; referring to algebraic curves as defined by Descartes.</note>...

He concludes by crediting Hendrick van Heuraet with being the first to solve this problem of "rectification" for algebraic curves in 1659.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical discussion of the "rectification" of curves—the mathematical process of finding a straight line exactly equal in length to a curved line. Having introduced Proposition IX, Huygens now addresses the competitive intellectual landscape of the 1650s, discussing the priority of discoveries made by Hendrick van Heuraet, William Neile, Christopher Wren, and himself.</meta>

ON THE

OF CURVED LINES.
</margin>
metrically, and reduced them to this measure, at the same time that [Christopher] Wren had given the length of the Cycloid <note>The curve traced by a point on the rim of a moving wheel.</note> by a no less ingenious argument.

I am aware that, after Heuraet’s discovery was published, the most learned [John] Wallis wished to attribute the same to William Neile—a noble youth among his own people—in his book on the Cissoid. But to me, weighing what he brings forward there, it seems that Neile was not far from that discovery, yet did not fully achieve it. For it does not appear from his demonstration, which Wallis provides, that he sufficiently understood what that curve would be whose measure he saw would be given if it were constructed. And it is believable that if he had known it was among those curves already known to Geometers for a long time, either he or others in his name would have shared such a noble discovery with Geometers sooner; a discovery which, if any other did, deserved for them to cry out that Archimedean *I have found it!* <note>original: *εύρηκα* (eureka). The famous exclamation attributed to Archimedes upon discovering the principle of buoyancy.</note>. To be sure, [Pierre de] Fermat, a senator of Toulouse and a most skilled Geometer, also wrote demonstrations of the same discovery as if proceeding from himself, which were printed in the year 1660; but those were certainly late.

Since we are on these matters, let it also be permitted for me to say what I contributed to advancing such an excellent discovery: for I both provided Heuraet with the occasion to arrive at it, and I was the first of all, even before him, to find the dimension of the parabolic curve from the given quadrature <term>quadrature</term><note>The process of determining the area of a geometric figure, often by finding a square of equal area.</note> of the hyperbola, which is a part of Heuraet's discovery. For toward the end of the year 1657, I happened upon these two things at once: the dimension of the parabolic curve I mentioned, and the reduction of the surface of a parabolic conoid <note>A three-dimensional shape formed by rotating a parabola around its axis.</note> to a circle. And when I had indicated by letter to [Frans van] Schooten and others of my friends that two unusual discoveries regarding the parabola had presented themselves to me—and that one of them was the extension of a conoidal surface into a circle—he shared those letters with Heuraet, with whom he was then on familiar terms. For this man of sharpest intellect, it was not difficult to understand that the dimension of that parabolic curve was related to the surface of the conoid. Having discovered both, and investigating further from there, he hit upon those other paraboloid curves for which equal straight lines are found absolutely.

And regarding the surface of the conoid reduced to a plane, lest anyone should perhaps lack testimony, I have seen fit to add these few words from the letters of the most celebrated man, René-François de Sluse—who must be counted among the foremost Geometers today—in which he congratulated me on that discovery in that very year, perhaps more extensively than it deserved. In which letters
