I gladly prefer this most beautiful reduction <note>original: "άπαγωγήν" (apagogēn). In geometry, this refers to the process of reducing a complex problem to a simpler one, such as relating a curved area to a circle.</note> for the squaring of the circle to all those many results which I once deduced from the linear locus, and which I will share with you, if you so command, when the opportunity arises.*

In the following year, I also discovered how the surfaces of hyperbolic conoids and spheroids <note>A spheroid is a sphere-like object formed by rotating an ellipse.</note> could be reduced to circles. I shared the constructions of these problems—though without adding the demonstrations—with the Geometers with whom I was then in correspondence: [Blaise] Pascal and others in France, and [John] Wallis in England. Not long after, Wallis published his own work on these matters along with many other subtle inventions, which led me to set aside the completion of my own demonstrations. However, since our constructions seemed quite elegant and are not yet publicly available, I am pleased to record them here.

### *To find a circle equal to the curved surface of a parabolic conoid.*

Let a conoid be given whose section through the axis is the parabola *ABC*; its axis is *BD*, its vertex is *B*, and the diameter of the base is *AC*, which is at right angles to the axis *BD*. And let it be required to find a circle equal to the curved portion of the surface.

From the left point A, a straight line segment extends upwards to point E, which is positioned above the vertex B. To the right of the parabola, there are three separate vertical line segments labeled H, K, and L, indicating specific proportional lengths or variables used in the mathematical construction.

<meta>This page continues Huygens’ presentation of his geometric discoveries from the late 1650s. Having mentioned the praise he received for his work on the parabolic conoid, he now provides the specific geometric constructions for determining the surface areas of these three-dimensional shapes, transitioning from the conoid to the oblong spheroid.</meta>

->CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS<-

ON THE

</margin>

By extending the axis from the vertex, let B E be taken equal to B D, and let E A be joined, which will touch the parabola A B C at point A. Furthermore, let A D be divided at point G, so that the ratio of A G to G D is the same as the ratio of E A to A D. And let a straight line H be established as equal to both lines A E and D G combined. Likewise, let the straight line L be equal to one-third of the base A C, and let the straight line K be found as the mean proportional <note>A geometric value (x) between two others (a and b) such that a:x = x:b; in modern terms, the square root of their product.</note> between H and L. If a circle is described using K as its radius, that circle will be equal to the curved surface of the parabolic conoid <note>A solid shape formed by rotating a parabola around its central axis.</note> A B C. From this it follows that if A E is double A D, the curved surface of the conoid will be to the circle of the base as 14 is to 9. If A E is triple A D, the ratio is 13 to 6. If A E is quadruple A D, the ratio is 14 to 5. And it will always be the case that the ratio is one whole number to another, provided that the ratio of A E to A D is of that same kind <note>Meaning a rational ratio.</note>.

### *To find a circle equal to the surface of an oblong spheroid.*

Let there be an oblong spheroid <note>A sphere-like shape elongated like a melon or a rugby ball, created by rotating an ellipse around its longest axis.</note> whose axis is A B, center is C, and whose section through the axis is the ellipse A D B E, with its minor diameter being D E.
Let D F be set equal to C B, or rather, let F be placed at one of the foci <note>One of the two fixed points that define the shape of an ellipse.</note> of the ellipse A D B E, and let the line B G be drawn parallel to the line F D, meeting the extended line E D at point G. 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing an ellipse with its major axis A-B oriented vertically and its minor axis D-E oriented horizontally. The center is C. Point F is a focus on the lower part of the axis. A construction line B-G extends horizontally to the left, and a line G-D extends diagonally. An arc B-H-A is drawn to the right of the axis. A separate vertical line K is shown to the side, representing the calculated radius.</image-desc>

With center G and radius G B, let an arc of a circumference B H A be described over the axis A B. Let the straight line K be the mean proportional between the semi-diameter C D and a line equal to both the arc A H B and the diameter D E. This K will be the radius of the circle which is equal to the surface of the spheroid A D B E.

->### *To find a circle equal to the surface of a wide or compressed spheroid.*<-

Let there be a wide spheroid <note>Now commonly called an **oblate spheroid**, like the shape of the Earth or a lentil.</note> whose axis is A B, center C, and whose section through the axis is the ellipse A D B E. Again, let either of the two foci be F, and with F C divided exactly in half at G, imagine a parabola A G B which has the axis A B as its base and the point G as its vertex.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing an ellipse with its vertical axis labeled A B and horizontal axis D E. The center is C. To the right of the center on the horizontal axis are points G and F. Below the main figure is a standalone horizontal line segment labeled H.</image-desc>

And let the line H be the mean proportional <note>A value $x$ such that $a:x = x:b$. In modern terms, $H = \sqrt{DE \times \text{length of curve } AGB}$.</note> between the diameter D E and a straight line equal to the parabolic curve A G B. This line H will be the radius of the circle which is equal to the surface of the proposed spheroid.

->### *To find a circle equal to the curved surface of a hyperbolic conoid.*<-

Let there be a hyperbolic conoid <note>A solid shape formed by rotating a hyperbola around its axis.</note> whose axis is A B, and whose section through the axis is the hyperbola C A D, whose transverse side <gloss>the distance between the vertices of the two branches of a hyperbola</gloss> is E A, center is F, and latus rectum <gloss>the "right side" or parameter that defines the curvature of a conic section</gloss> is A G. 

Let the straight line A H be taken on the axis, equal to half of the latus rectum A G; and as H F is to A F in length, so let A F be to F K in power <note>Meaning the square of A F is equal to the product of H F and F K ($AF^2 = HF \cdot FK$).</note>. And let another hyperbola K L M be imagined, described with vertex K, using the same axis and center F as the first, and which has its latus rectum and transverse side reciprocally proportional to the former. Moreover, let the extended line B C meet this hyperbola at M, and let A L be parallel to B C. Now, just as the space A L M B—bounded by three straight lines and the hyperbolic curve—is to half the square of B C, so the curved surface of the conoid is to the circle of its base, whose diameter is C D. From this, the rest of the construction will be easily completed, provided that the quadrature <note>The process of finding the area of a curved shape.</note> of the hyperbola is established.

Since, therefore, the surface of a parabolic conoid is reduced to a circle, just as the surface of a sphere is, according to the known rules of geometry; in order for the same to be done for the surface of an oblong spheroid, the arc must be placed...

# CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>ON THE EVOLUTION OF CURVED LINES.</margin>
...that the length of the circumference can be set equal to a straight line. However, to flatten <note>original: "complanandam"; in this context, it refers to "rectifying" or finding a flat area equal to a curved surface.</note> the surface of a wide spheroid, and likewise that of a hyperbolic conoid, by the same method, the **quadrature** <note>The process of determining the area of a plane figure or surface by constructing a square (or circle) of equal area.</note> of a hyperbola is required. For the length of the parabolic line which we employed in the case of this spheroid depends upon the quadrature of the hyperbola, as we shall soon demonstrate.

But, in a point that seems not unworthy of notice, we have found that—without any assumption regarding the quadrature of the hyperbola—a circle can be constructed equal to the surfaces of both the wide spheroid <note>Now usually called an **oblate spheroid**, like a flattened sphere.</note> and the hyperbolic conoid taken together.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a central vertical axis with points labeled E, F, K, A, H, B from top to bottom. A curve (likely a conic section) peaks at point A and meets a horizontal base line at points C and D. Another line or curve passes through point K. A horizontal line passes through points L, A, and G. Points M, C, B, and D are marked along the base line.</image-desc>

For, given any wide spheroid, a hyperbolic conoid can be found—or conversely, given a hyperbolic conoid, a wide spheroid can be found—of such a kind that a circle equal to both surfaces together may be shown. It will suffice to have provided an example of this in one case, which is simpler than the others.

Let there be a wide spheroid whose axis is $SI$, and whose section through the axis is the ellipse $STIK$; let the center of this ellipse be $O$, and the major axis $TK$. Furthermore, let this ellipse be such that the **transverse side** <gloss>the distance between the vertices</gloss> $TK$ has the same ratio to the **latus rectum** <gloss>the parameter governing the "openness" of the curve</gloss> as a line divided in **extreme and mean ratio** <note>This refers to the "Golden Ratio," where the ratio of the whole line to the larger segment is the same as the ratio of the larger segment to the smaller.</note> has to its larger part.

Let $BC$ be double in power <note>meaning the square of the segment $BC$ is twice the square of the segment $SO$ ($BC^2 = 2SO^2$)</note> to $SO$, and likewise let $BA$ be double in power to $OK$. And let these four be in continuous proportion: $BC$, $BA$, $BF$...

<summary>Huygens explains that while measuring the surface of oblate spheroids and hyperbolic conoids individually requires the quadrature of a hyperbola, their combined surfaces can be related to a circle using a specific geometric construction involving the Golden Ratio.</summary>

...B E, and let E P be set equal to E A. Let there now be understood a hyperbolic conoid <note>A solid shape formed by rotating a hyperbola around its axis.</note> Q F N, whose axis is F P; let the adjacent axis, or half the **transverse side** <note>original: "latus transversum"; the distance between the vertices of a hyperbola</note>, be F B; and let half the **latus rectum** <term>latus rectum: a technical term in geometry referring to the chord through the focus of a conic section perpendicular to its major axis</term> be equal to B C.

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram featuring an ellipse and a hyperbolic curve. The ellipse is centered at point O with vertical vertices T and K, and lateral points S and I. To the right of the ellipse is a vertical axis containing points B, C, A, F, E, and P. A hyperbolic curve (conoid section) has its vertex at F and opens downwards to points Q and N on a horizontal base line passing through P. Further to the right are two points labeled M and L. Construction lines connect various points including the vertices of the ellipse and the hyperbolic curve to the vertical axis.
</image-desc>

The curved surface of this conoid, together with the surface of the spheroid S I, will be equal to a circle whose given radius is M L—specifically, a radius whose square is equal to <note>original: "possit"; in classical geometry, "to be able to" often means the line whose square equals a given area.</note> the square of T K plus double the square of S I.

### To find a straight line equal to a parabolic curve.

**L**et there be a segment of a parabola A B C, whose axis is B K, and whose base A C is at right angles to the axis; the task is to find a straight line equal to the curve A B C.

Let a straight line I E be taken equal to half the base A K, and let it be extended to H so that I H is equal to A G—the line which, touching the parabola at point A <note>the tangent line at the base of the curve</note>, meets the extended axis at G. Let there now be a segment of a hyperbola D E F, described with vertex E and center I, whose diameter is E H; and let its base D H F be applied as an **ordinate** <note>original: "ordinatim... applicata"; a line drawn from a point on a curve to its axis, usually parallel to the tangent at the vertex.</note> to the diameter. The **latus rectum** may be chosen at will. If a parallelogram D P Q F is now understood to be constructed upon the base D F, such that it is equal in area to the hyperbolic segment D E F; its side P Q will then cut the diameter of the hyperbola at R in such a way that R I is equal to the parabolic curve A B, the double of which is the full curve A B C.

Thus it appears from this how the measurement of a parabolic curve depends upon the **quadrature** <note>The mathematical process of finding the area of a curved figure, effectively "squaring" it.</note> of the hyperbola, and the latter upon the former in turn.

The number for this logarithm will have 11 digits, since the characteristic <note>In historical table-based logarithms, the "characteristic" is the integer part of the logarithm, which determines the position of the decimal point in the resulting number.</note> is 10. Therefore, first let the nearest smaller number corresponding to the found logarithm be sought, which is 19740. Then, from the difference of that logarithm and the one following it in the table, the remaining digits 81026 are elicited; these are written after the first ones to make 197408.10260, adding a zero at the end so that the number of digits becomes 11. Thus, the area of the space **DEVBAD** is approximately 197,408.10260 parts, where the parallelogram **DC** is 100,000.00000 parts.

## PROPOSITION X.

**To display curved lines whose evolution <note>original: "evolutione"; this refers to the process of "unwinding" a taut string from a source curve (the evolute) to trace a new curve (the involute).</note> describes ellipses and hyperbolas, and to find straight lines equal to those same curves.**

Let there be any ellipse or hyperbola **AB**, whose transverse axis is **AC**; the center of the figure is **D**; and the <term>latus rectum</term> <note>A technical term in conic sections referring to the chord through a focus parallel to the directrix; it determines the "width" of the curve.</note> is double the length of **AE**. Having taken any point in the section, such as **B**, let the straight line **BK** be applied as an <term>ordinate</term> <note>A vertical line segment from a point on the curve to the horizontal axis.</note> to the axis; and let a tangent be drawn to the said point **B** which meets the axis at **F**. Let **BG** be perpendicular to this tangent **FB**, meeting the axis at **G**; and let **BG** be extended all the way to **H**, so that the ratio of **BH** to **HG** is composed of the ratios of **GF** to **FK** and **AD** to **DE**.

I say that the curve **EHM**, all points of which are found in the same manner as point **H**, is the curve by whose evolution—together with the straight line **EA**—the section **AB** will be described. Moreover, **BH** itself touches the curve at...

## PROPOSITION XI.

**Given a curved line, to find another by whose evolution it is described; and to show that for every geometric curve, another geometric curve likewise exists, to which a straight line of equal length can be given.**

Let there be any curve, or part of one, bent in one direction **ABF**, and a straight line **KL**, to which all points may be referred; and it is required to find another curve, such as **DE**, by whose evolution <note>original: "evolutione"; the process of "unwinding" a taut string from a curve to trace a new path.</note> the curve **ABF** itself is described.

<image-desc>
A geometric diagram illustrating the mathematical relationship between a curve (involute) and its evolute. The diagram is split into two symmetrical halves. On both sides, a curve labeled ABF is depicted with normal lines drawn from points B and F. These lines are shown to be tangent to another curve, labeled DE (the evolute). Points are labeled with various letters: Y, H, A, K, L, M, N, X, T, V, B, P, O, F, C, D, G, E, arranged around horizontal and vertical reference lines.
</image-desc>

Suppose the curve has already been found; and since it is necessary that all tangents of the curve **DE** meet the curve **ABF** (which is described by the evolution) at right angles; it is also clear, conversely, that those lines which stand at right angles to **ABF** itself, such as **BD** and **FE**, will touch the evolute <term>evolutam: the "unwound" curve, from which the secondary curve (the involute) is generated</term> **CDE**.

Let points *B* and *F* be understood as very close to each other; and if the evolution is assumed to begin from part *a*, and *F* is further from there than *B*, then the point of contact *E* will also be further from *A* than *D*. The intersection of the straight lines *BD* and *FE*, which is *G*, will fall beyond point *D* on the line *BD*. For it is necessary that *BD* and *FE* concur, since they stand at right angles to the concave side of the curve *BF*.

However, it is apparent that the closer point *F* is to *B*, the closer the points *D, G,* and *E* come to meeting; therefore, if the interval *BF* is understood to be infinitely small, the three said points must be considered as one and the same. Furthermore, if a straight line *BH* is drawn to touch the curve at *B*, it will also be regarded as the tangent at *F*. Let *BO* be parallel to *KL*, and let the perpendiculars *BK* and *FL* fall upon it. Let *FL* cut the line *BO* at *P*, and let the marked points be *M* and *N*, where the lines *BD* and *FE* meet the line *KL*. Therefore, because the ratio of *BG* to *GM* is the same as *BO* to *MN*, if this ratio is given, the other will also be given. And because the line *BM* is given in size and position, point *G* on the extension of *BM*—or *D* on the curve *CDE*—will also be given, since we have said that *G* and *D* converge into one.

Now, the ratio of *BO* to *MN* is given; it is quite simple in the Cycloid <note>A curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line; Huygens's work on this was revolutionary for clockmaking.</note>, where we first investigated it and found it to be double; but in other curves which we have examined thus far, it is found through the composition of two given ratios. For because the ratio of *BO* to *MN* is composed of the ratios of *BO* to *BP* (or *NH* to *LH*) and of *BP* (or *KL*) to *MN*, it is clear that if both these ratios are given, the ratio of *BO* to *MN* composed from them will also be given. It will be shown in what follows that these ratios are given in all geometric curves; and consequently, one can always assign the curves from whose evolution they are described, which are therefore reducible to straight lines <note>original: "ad rectas lineas sint reducibiles"; this refers to "rectification," the process of finding a straight line with the same length as a curve.</note>.

First, let the parabola *ABF* be assumed, whose vertex is *A* and axis is *AQ*. Since the lines *BM* and *FN* are at right angles to the parabola, and the perpendiculars *BK* and *FL* are drawn to the axis *AQ*, then by the property of the parabola, each of the segments *MK* and *NL* will be equal to half the *latus rectum* <term>latus rectum: the "upright side," a constant parameter that defines the width of a conic section relative to its focus.</term>. With the common segment *LM* subtracted, *KL* and *MN* are equal to each other. Hence, since the ratio of *BG* to *GM* is composed of the ratios of *NH* to *HL* and *KL* to *MN* (as was said), and the latter is a ratio of equality, it is clear that the ratio of *BG* to *GM* will be the same as *NH* to *HL*. By dividing, the ratio of *BM* to *MG* will be the same as *NL* to *LH*, or *MK* to *KH*; for *LH* and *KH* are considered the same because of the proximity of points *B* and *F*. Furthermore, the ratio of *MK* to *KH* is given, since point *B* is given; for both *MK* and *KH* are given in magnitude; for *MK* is equal to half the *latus rectum*, and *KH* is double *KA*. It is also given in position and magnitude—

Therefore $MG$ will also be given, and thus point $G$, or $D$, on the curve $RDE$; which is found by extending $BM$ to $G$, so that $BM$ is to $MG$ as $1/2$ of the *latus rectum* <term>latus rectum: literally the "straight side"; it is a constant parameter that determines the width of a conic section (like a parabola) through its focus</term> is to twice the length $KA$.

And in this way, by taking any number of other points on the parabola $ABF$ besides $B$, just as many points on the line $RDE$ will be found by a similar method; and by this very fact it is established that the line $RDE$ is a geometric curve <term>geometric curve: a curve that can be precisely described by an algebraic equation</term>, and at the same time its specific property is made known, from which all its other characteristics can be deduced.

Suppose we then wish to investigate by what equation the relationship of all points of the curve $CDE$ to the straight line $AQ$ is expressed: let a perpendicular line $DQ$ be drawn to it, and let the *latus rectum* of the parabola $ABF$ be called $a$; let $AK$ be $b$; $AQ$ be $x$; and $QD$ be $y$. Since the ratio of $BM$ to $MD$ (that is, $KM$ to $MQ$) is the same as $1/2 a$ to $2b$, and since $KM$ itself is equal to $1/2 a$, then $MQ$ will also be equal to $2b$. Moreover, $MA = 1/2 a + b$. Therefore $AQ$ (or $x$) is equal to $3b + 1/2 a$. From which it follows that $b = 1/3 x - 1/6 a$.

Furthermore, since just as the square of $MK$ (that is, $1/4 a^2$) is to the square of $KB$ (which is $ab$), so is the square of $MQ$ (that is, $4b^2$) to the square of $QD$; thus the square of $QD$ (or $y^2$) will be $\frac{16 b^3}{a}$. Here, if $1/3 x - 1/6 a$ (which was found to be equal to $b$) is substituted in its place, $y^2$ will equal $16$ times the cube of $(1/3 x - 1/6 a)$ divided by $a$. And consequently: 

$\frac{27}{16} a y^2 = (x - 1/2 a)^3$

Let $AR$ be taken on the axis of the parabola equal to $1/2 a$; then $RQ$ will be $x - 1/2 a$. It is clear, therefore, that the curve $CD$ is of such a nature that the cube of the line $RQ$ is always equal to the parallelepiped <term>parallelepiped: a solid body with six faces; in this geometric context, Huygens uses it to represent the product of a square area and a height ($y^2 \cdot \text{height}$)</term> whose base is the square of $QD$ and whose height is $27/16 a$.

Consequently, the curve is that very paraboloid <note>Original: "paraboloidem". In this period, this referred to a "higher order" parabola, specifically what we now call a semi-cubical parabola ($y^2 = kx^3$).</note> whose evolution describes the parabola $AB$, as we have shown above. The *latus rectum* of this paraboloid equals $27/16$ of the *latus rectum* of the parabola $AB$. For then the *latus rectum* of the latter becomes $16/27$ of the *latus rectum* of the paraboloid, just as was defined in that section.

Furthermore, it is clear how the ratio of $OB$ to $BP$, or $NH$ to $HL$, can always be found—not only when $ABF$ is a parabola, but also for any other geometric curve. For one only needs to draw the straight line $FH$, which touches the curve at the chosen point $F$ <note>original: "tangat"; this refers to the tangent line</note>, and let $FN$ be perpendicular to $FH$ <note>this perpendicular line is known as the "normal" to the curve</note>; from this, the lengths $NH$ and $HL$ will be given, and consequently their ratio will also be known.

<image-desc>Two geometric diagrams illustrating the properties of curves, tangents, and normals. Both diagrams feature a curve with labeled points (A, B, F, G, E) and a system of vertical and horizontal lines with various lettered points (H, K, L, M, N, T, X, V) used for geometric proofs involving ratios and line segments.</image-desc>

But it is not equally obvious by what means the ratio of $KL$ to $MN$ may become known; nevertheless, we shall show that this can also always be found in the following way.

Let the straight lines $KT$ and $LV$ be perpendicular to $KL$, and let $KT$ be equal to $KM$, and $LV$ be equal to $LN$. Let $VX$ be drawn parallel to $LN$, meeting $KT$ at $X$. Since, therefore, the difference between the two segments $LK$ and $NM$ is always the same as the difference between the two $LN$ and $KM$—that is, the same as the difference between the two $LV$ and $KT$—and since the difference between $LV$ and $KT$ is equal to $XT$, and $XV$ is equal to $LK$; it follows that $NM$ will be equal to $VX$ and $XT$ added together, or equal to the amount by which $VX$ exceeds $XT$. And thus, if the ratio of $VX$ to $XT$ is given, the ratio of $VX$ to the sum of $VX$ and $XT$ (or to the difference between them) will also be given—that is, the ratio of $VX$ (or $LK$) to $MN$ will be given.

It should be understood, however, that because $KT$ was taken equal to $KM$ and $LV$ equal to $LN$, the locus <term>locus: the path or shape formed by all points that satisfy a particular mathematical rule</term> of points $T$ and $V$ will be a certain line, either straight or curved, as will soon be shown. And if indeed it is a straight line—as occurs if $ABF$ is a conic section <term>conic section: any curve (circle, ellipse, parabola, or hyperbola) produced by the intersection of a plane and a cone</term> and $KL$ is its axis—it is established that the ratio of $VX$ to $XT$ will be given, since the position of the line $VT$ (which is the locus of points $V$ and $T$) is given; and always the same...

But if the locus <note>the path or collection of points</note> is another curved line, the ratio of $vx$ to $x\tau$ will vary according to whether the interval $KL$ is larger or smaller. We must investigate, however, what this ratio will be when we imagine $KL$ to be infinitely small, since we have placed points $B$ and $F$ very close to each other. Similarly, the points $v$ and $\tau$ must be understood to intercept a very tiny portion of the curved line; hence the straight line $v\tau$ will coincide with the line that touches the curve at $\tau$ <note>original: "contingit"; this refers to the tangent line</note>. Let that tangent, therefore, be $\tau\gamma$; for it can be drawn since the curve on which points $\tau$ and $v$ lie is geometric <note>a "geometric" curve in the 17th century meant one that could be expressed by an algebraic equation</note>. Therefore, the ratio of $\gamma K$ to $K\tau$ will be given, and thus also $vx$ to $x\tau$, from which we have shown that the ratio of $LK$ to $NM$ is also given.

To find which line the points $\tau$ and $v$ lie upon, we place a fixed point $S$ on the straight line $KL$, calling $SK$, $x$; and $K\tau$, $y$. For since the curve $ABF$ is given, and $BM$ is drawn to it at right angles <note>this is the "normal" line</note>, the length of the line $KM$ will be found by the method of tangents handed down by Descartes <note>René Descartes, whose *La Géométrie* (1637) revolutionized how curves were analyzed using algebra</note>. This $KM$ will be equal to $K\tau$ (or $y$), and from that equation, the nature of the curve $\tau v$ will become known, upon which the tangent is then to be drawn. But all will become clearer with the following example.

Let $ABF$ be that paraboloid <note>Huygens uses "paraboloid" here to refer to a higher-order parabola, specifically a cubic parabola where the cubes of the ordinates are proportional to the squares of the abscissae</note> for which we previously found an equal straight line; namely, one in which the cubes of the perpendiculars to the line $SK$ are to each other as the squares of the segments cut off from $SK$ itself. And it is required to find the curve $CDE$, by the evolution of which the paraboloid $SBF$ is described.

Here, first, the ratio of $BO$ to $BP$ is easily found, because we know how to draw the tangent of the paraboloid at point $B$ by taking $SH$ equal to $1/2 SK$. Since $BM$ stands at right angles to this tangent, the lines $MH$ and $HK$ are now given, and therefore their ratio to each other, which is the same as $OB$ to $BP$.

In order that the ratio of $BP$ (or $KL$) to $MN$ may become known, let the straight lines $K\tau$ and $Lv$ be placed perpendicular to $KL$, equal to the individual segments $KM$ and $LN$, and let $vx$ be parallel to $LK$. Now, because by taking $KM$ away from the two segments $KL$ and $LN$ together, $MN$ is left; that is, by taking $K\tau$ away from the two $xv$ and $vL$ (or $xv$ and $xK$), it appears that $vx$ and $x\tau$ are left. Therefore, these two, $vx$ and $x\tau$, will be equal to $MN$ itself, and consequently the ratio of $KL$ to $MN$ is the same as $vx$ to the two $vx$ and $x\tau$ together. But so that this ratio may be known when the interval $KL$ is at its minimum, one must (following what was said before) inquire what the locus or line is upon which points $\tau$ and $v$ lie. To do this, let the <term>latus rectum</term> <note>the "side parameter" that defines the scale of the curve</note> of the paraboloid $ABF$ be $a$; $SK = x$; $K\tau = y$.

Since, therefore, $KH, KB, KM$ are proportional, and $HK = $

The ratio $\frac{3}{2} x$ to $KB$ <note>a segment on the diagram</note>, according to the nature of the paraboloid, is equal to the cubic root of $a^2 x$ (which equals $y$). It follows that $KM$ (that is, $KT$) equals $\frac{2}{3}$ of the cubic root of $a^2 x$, which equals $y$, and therefore $\frac{8}{27} a^2 x$ is equal to <note>original: "$\infty$"; Huygens uses this symbol for equality</note> $y^3$. From this it is evident that the locus <note>the path or collection of points</note> of points $T$ and $v$ is that paraboloid which geometers call "cubic" <note>today known as a semi-cubic parabola, defined by the equation $y^2 = cx^3$</note>. Accordingly, the tangent at $T$ is drawn by taking $SY$ as double the length of $SK$ and joining $Y$ to $T$. And now indeed the ratio of $vx$ to the two segments $vx$ and $xT$ combined (which we said is the same as $KL$ to $MN$) will be that which $YK$ has to both $YK$ and $KT$ combined. Since this ratio is given <note>meaning it is a known or determined value</note>, the ratio of $KL$ to $MN$ is also given. Furthermore, it has been shown that the ratio of $OB$ to $PB$ is given. Therefore, since the ratio of $BD$ to $DM$ is composed of these two <note>in 17th-century geometry, "composing" ratios is equivalent to multiplying them</note>, as was shown above, this ratio will also be given; and by division <note>a geometric operation on proportions</note>, the ratio of $BM$ to $MD$ is given, and thus the location of point $D$ on the curve $DE$.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the properties of a cubic paraboloid curve and its evolution. A curve labeled with points E, D, C, A, Z is shown. A vertical axis contains points Y, H, S, K, L, M, N. Several horizontal and diagonal lines intersect this axis and the curve, with points of intersection and construction labeled as T, B, V, X, P, F, O, G. The diagram depicts the relationship between tangents, ratios of line segments, and the construction of the curve.</image-desc>

We arrive at the shortest construction in this manner. $KT$ (or $KM$) was called $y$. Thus $MH$ will be $y + \frac{3}{2} x$. And the ratio of $MH$ to $HK$ (or $OB$ to $BP$) is as $y + \frac{3}{2} x$ to $\frac{3}{2} x$, or, by doubling all terms, as $2y + 3x$ to $3x$. Next, because $YK$ is equal to $3x$, the ratio of $YK$ to $YK + KT$ (or, as previously stated, $KL$ to $MN$) will be as $3x$ to $3x + y$. But we said that the ratio of $BD$ to $DM$ is composed of the ratios $OB$ to $BP$ and $KL$ to $MN$. Therefore, the ratio of $BD$ to $DM$ will be composed of the ratios $2y + 3x$ to $3x$ and $3x$ to $3x + y$; consequently, it will be as $2y + 3x$ to $3x + y$. And by division, the ratio of $BM$ to $MD$ is the same as $y$ to $3x + y$.

Let $SZ$ be perpendicular to $SK$, and let the extension of $MB$ meet it at $z$.

Therefore, because the ratio of $BM$ to $MD$ was found to be that of $y$ to $y + 3x$, which is the same as the ratio of $mk$ to $mk + 3ks$. Moreover, just as $mk$ is to $mk + 3ks$, so $MB$ is to $MB + 3BZ$: it follows, then, that $MB$ is to $MD$ as $MB$ is to $MB + 3BZ$. From this it is clear that $MD$ must be taken as equal to $MB + 3BZ$. In this way, it will be possible to find any points on the curve $CDE$. Any portion of this curve, such as $DS$, will be equal to the straight line $DB$, which meets the paraboloid $SAB$ at right angles <note>In modern terms, $DB$ is the "normal" to the curve at point $B$</note>. It is established that this curve is <term>geometric</term> <note>meaning it can be described by an algebraic equation rather than just a mechanical motion</note>, and if we wish, we can express the relationship of all its points to the points of the axis $SK$ by means of an equation.

In a similar way, however, if we investigate that <term>paraboloid</term> <note>Huygens uses "paraboloid" here to mean a higher-order parabola, specifically the cubic parabola</note> or cubic parabola, in which the cubes of the ordinates to the axis are to each other as the intercepted portions of the axis <note>the equation $y^3 = ax$</note>, we will find that the curve by whose evolution it is described—and which can therefore be rectified <note>made equal to a straight line</note>—can be determined through points by a construction no more difficult. let there be drawn through any point $B$ taken on the paraboloid a straight line $BD$, which cuts the curve at right angles and meets its axis at $M$, and the line $SZ$ at $Z$. Furthermore, there is a point here worthy of note, which also happens in several other of these paraboloids: there are two evolutions in opposite directions, each of which begins from a certain point $A$. Thus, by the evolution of $ARD$, continued further into infinity, the infinite part of the paraboloid $ABF$ is described; however, by the evolution of the whole $FHK$, similarly extended into infinity, only the small portion $AS$ is described. ...on the sought curve $CD$. For example, if $sB$ is a parabola formed from a conic section, we know it corresponds to the first equation in the table, $ax = y^2$; to which corresponds, on the other side of the table, $BM + 2BZ = BD$ <note>Huygens uses the symbol $\infty$ for equality; I have substituted the modern "=" for clarity.</note>. From this, the length of the line $BD$ is determined, and thus the discovery of any number of points on the curve $CD$ is possible. Indeed, in this case, it was demonstrated above that this curve is a <term>paraboloid</term> <note>Specifically, a semi-cubic parabola</note>—namely, the one whose equation is the third in this table.

The table is constructed according to this rule: $BM$ is taken as a multiple according to the number that is the exponent of the power of $x$ in the equation; $BZ$, however, is taken as a multiple according to the exponent of the power of $y$. From the sum of these two, one takes the part defined by the exponent of the power of $a$.

Besides these <term>paraboloid</term> <note>Higher-order curves in the parabola family</note> lines, we have also found others from which, by a similar construction, curves comparable to straight lines are derived. These resemble hyperbolas because they possess <term>asymptotes</term> <note>Lines that a curve approaches indefinitely but never touches</note>, though only those that form a right angle. Of these, we first establish the hyperbola itself, which is produced from a conic section.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a coordinate system with two perpendicular lines (asymptotes) meeting at point S. A curve A B is drawn between these lines. Various points are marked: P on the horizontal asymptote, K on the vertical asymptote. Lines are drawn from a point B on the curve to M and K. Below curve A B, another curve C D is depicted with associated line segments.</image-desc>

To explain the nature of the remaining curves: let $PS$ and $SK$ be the asymptotes of the curve $AB$, forming a right angle, and from any point $B$ on the curve, let $BK$ be drawn parallel to $PS$. Let $SK = x$ and $KB = y$. If, therefore, $AB$ is a hyperbola, we know that the <term>rectangle</term> <note>The product of the coordinates</note> of the lines $SK$ and $KB$—that is, the product $xy$—is always equal to the same square, which may be called $a^2$.

The line $s \kappa$, when multiplied by the height $\kappa b$ <note>original: "in altitudinem $\kappa b$ ductum"</note>—that is, the solid $x^2 y$—will be equal to a certain cube, which we shall call $a^3$. And so there exist countless others of this kind of <term>hyperboloid</term> <note>A family of curves defined by the equation $x^m y^n = a^{m+n}$</note>, the property of which the following table displays for each individual equation, while simultaneously showing the method for constructing the curve $DC$, from whose <term>evolution</term> <note>The process of "unrolling" a thread from a curve to describe a new one</note> each is generated.

| If | Then |
| :--- | :--- |
| $xy = a^2$ | $\frac{1}{2} BM + \frac{1}{2} BZ = BD$ |
| $x^2 y = a^3$ | $\frac{2}{3} BM + \frac{1}{3} BZ = BD$ |
| $x y^2 = a^3$ | $\frac{1}{3} BM + \frac{2}{3} BZ = BD$ |
| $x^3 y = a^4$ | $\frac{3}{4} BM + \frac{1}{4} BZ = BD$ |
| $x y^3 = a^4$ | $\frac{1}{4} BM + \frac{3}{4} BZ = BD$ |

The straight line $DBMZ$ cuts the curve $AB$, as before, at right angles, and it meets the <term>asymptotes</term> <note>Lines that a curve approaches indefinitely but never touches</note> $s \kappa$ and $sp$ at the points $m$ and $z$. If, therefore, for the sake of example, the hyperbola should be $AB$, whose equation is $xy = a^2$, then $BD$ will be taken as equal to $\frac{1}{2} BM + \frac{1}{2} BZ$, just as the table prescribes. Point $D$ will then be on the sought curve $DC$, any other points of which can be found in this way, and any portion of it can be equated to a straight line <note>This refers to "rectification," or finding the exact length of a curve</note>. And this is indeed that same curve whose relationship to the axis of the hyperbola we expressed earlier by an equation. Moreover, the construction of this table is clearly the same as the previous one.

Furthermore, since lines $DBZ$ must be drawn for the construction of both these curves and those that arise from <term>paraboloids</term> <note>Higher-order parabolas</note>—lines which cut the curves $AB$ at a given point $B$, or rather their <term>tangents</term> $BH$, at right angles—we shall state in general how these tangents may be found. Thus, in the equation that explains the nature of each curve (such as the equations set forth in the two preceding tables), one must consider what the exponents of the powers of $x$ and $y$ are, and ensure that, just as the exponent of the power of $x$ is to the exponent of the power of $y$, so is $s \kappa$ to $\kappa H$. For the joined line $HB$ will touch the curve at $B$. For instance, in the third hyperboloid, whose equation is $x y^2 = a^3$: because the exponent of the power of $x$ is 1, and the exponent of the power of $y$ is 2, it must be that 1 is to 2 as $s \kappa$ is to $\kappa H$. Those skilled in the <term>analytical art</term> <note>Early symbolic algebra and the foundations of calculus</note> are familiar with the proof of these things, as they have long since begun to contemplate all these lines; they have measured the planes and solids not only of those paraboloids, but also of certain infinite spaces lying between hyperboloids and their asymptotes. This we also could have accomplished by a simple and universal method, taking the proof solely from the property of tangents. But those matters do not belong in this place.

<meta>This page begins Part Four of the treatise. While the previous section concluded the geometric study of evolutes and curved lines, Huygens now pivots to the physical application of these theories: determining the "center of oscillation." He introduces this by recounting his personal history with the problem, moving from his early failures to his eventual success triggered by the practical need to regulate his mechanical clocks.</meta>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
## PART FOUR.
### On the Center of Oscillation.

The investigation of the Centers of Oscillation, or Agitation, was proposed to me long ago—when I was still almost a boy—and to many others by the most learned <term>Mersenne</term> <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath who acted as a central hub for scientific correspondence in Europe</note>. It was a very famous problem among the mathematicians <note>original: "Geometras"</note> of that time, as I gather from the letters he sent to me, as well as from those of <term>Descartes</term> <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the famous philosopher and mathematician</note> published not long ago, which contain his responses to Mersenne’s inquiries on these matters. He requested that I find those centers in sectors of a circle, suspended both from the angle and from the middle of the arc and swung sideways; likewise in segments of a circle, and in triangles, hanging now from the vertex, now from the middle of the base. This task amounts to finding a <term>simple pendulum</term> <note>A theoretical pendulum consisting of a single point mass on a weightless string</note>—that is, a weight hanging from a thread—of such a length that it performs its oscillations in the same time as those suspended shapes already mentioned. At the same time, he promised a reward for the labor—great and even enviable, should I happen to satisfy the inquiries. But at that time, he obtained what he desired from no one.

As for me, since I found nothing by which even the first path to that contemplation might open up, I felt as if I were repulsed from the very threshold and abstained from further investigation at that time. However, those distinguished men who hoped they had completed the task—Descartes, <term>Honoratus Fabrius</term> <note>Honoré Fabri (1607–1688), a Jesuit mathematician and physicist</note>, and others—by no means hit the mark, except in a few easier cases, for which they nonetheless provided no suitable demonstration, as it seems to me. I hope this will become manifest by comparing what we present here, if anyone should happen to compare their findings with ours; for I believe these are demonstrated from more certain principles and have found them to be entirely consistent with experiments.

The occasion for attempting these things anew was offered by the method of regulating the pendulums of our <term>machine</term> <note>original: "automati nostri," referring to his mechanical clock</note>, when I applied a movable weight to them in addition to the one at the bottom, as was explained in the description of the clock. From this point, beginning the matter with better omens and from its first origin, I finally overcame all difficulties; and I found not only the solution to Mersenne’s problems but also others more difficult than those,

<summary>Huygens introduces the problem of the "center of oscillation," recounting how Marin Mersenne challenged the era's greatest minds to solve it. He notes that while Descartes and Fabri failed to provide adequate proof, his own work on clock regulation eventually led him to a definitive solution based on experimental evidence and solid principles.</summary>

<keywords>center of oscillation, Marin Mersenne, René Descartes, Honoratus Fabrius, simple pendulum, clock regulation, mechanical horology</keywords>

->CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS<-

<margin>ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.</margin>
...finally, a way by which that center might be investigated by a definite method in lines, surfaces, and solid bodies. From this, indeed, besides the pleasure of discovery—which had been much sought after by others—and of knowing the laws and decrees of nature in these matters, I also derived that utility for the sake of which I had first applied my mind to these things: the discovery of that easy and efficient method for regulating the clock <note>original: "horologii temperandi." Huygens refers to the practical application of his mathematical theories to ensure his pendulum clocks kept perfect time.</note>. Moreover, this also occurred, which I think should be valued more highly: that through these things I could hand down a most absolute definition of a certain measure, which would endure for all ages; such as that which will be found added to the end of these things <note>Huygens proposed using the length of a pendulum that swings once per second as a "universal measure" or standard unit of length that would not change over time or vary by country.</note>.

## DEFINITIONS.

### I.

Let a **pendulum** be defined as any figure endowed with weight—whether it be a line, a surface, or a solid—suspended in such a way that it can continue a reciprocal motion <note>A back-and-forth movement</note> by the force of its own gravity around some point, or rather an axis, which is understood to be parallel to the plane of the horizon.

### II.

*That axis parallel to the plane of the horizon, around which the motion of the pendulum is understood to occur, shall be called the **axis of Oscillation**.*

### III.

*A **simple pendulum** <note>original: "Pendulum simplex"</note> shall be defined as that which is understood to consist of a thread or an inflexible line, devoid of weight, carrying a weight attached to its lowest part; the gravity of this weight is to be considered as if collected into a single point.*

### IV.

*A **composite pendulum**, however, is one that consists of several weights maintaining unchanging distances, both from one another and from the axis of Oscillation. Hence any suspended figure endowed with weight can be called a composite pendulum, insofar as it is thought of as divisible into any number of parts.*

### V.

*Isochronous pendulums <note>From the Greek "isos" (equal) and "chronos" (time); pendulums that take the same amount of time to complete a swing.</note> shall be those whose oscillations, through similar arcs, are performed in equal times.*

<summary>Huygens concludes his preface by linking his theoretical work to the practical regulation of clocks and the proposal of a universal unit of length. He then provides five foundational definitions for pendulums, distinguishing between theoretical "simple" pendulums and real-world "composite" objects.</summary>
<keywords>oscillation, simple pendulum, composite pendulum, isochronous, universal measure, clock regulation, axis of oscillation</keywords>

HOROLOG. OSCILLATOR. 93
M iij

->VI.<-

<margin>ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.</margin>
*The **Plane of Oscillation** is defined as that which is understood to be drawn through the center of gravity of the suspended figure, perpendicular to the axis of oscillation.*

->VII.<-

*The **Line of the Center** is the straight line drawn through the center of gravity of the figure, perpendicular to the axis of oscillation.*

->VIII.<-

*The **Line of the Perpendicular** is the straight line in the plane of oscillation, drawn from the axis of oscillation, perpendicular to the plane of the horizon.*

->IX.<-

*The **Center of Oscillation or Agitation** of any figure is defined as a point on the line of the center, at such a distance from the axis of oscillation as is equal to the length of a simple pendulum that is isochronous <note>Moving in exactly the same amount of time.</note> with the figure.*

->X.<-

*The **Axis of Gravity** is any straight line passing through the center of gravity of the figure.*

->XI.<-

*A **Plane Figure**, or a line situated in a plane, is said to be agitated **in its plane** when the axis of oscillation is in the same plane as the figure or line.*

->XII.<-

*The same is said to be agitated **sideways** when the axis of oscillation is perpendicular to the plane of the figure or line.*

->XIII.<-

*When weights are said to be **multiplied into straight lines** <note>original: "duci." In 17th-century mathematical Latin, "to lead" or "to draw" a quantity into another meant to multiply them, often visualized as forming an area from two lines.</note>, it is to be understood as if numbers or lines, expressing the quantities of the weights and their mutual ratio to each other, are so multiplied.*

# HYPOTHESES
->I.<-

**I**f any number of weights, by the force of their own gravity, begin to move, it is impossible for the center of gravity of the composite formed from them to rise higher than where it was found at the start of the motion. <note>This is Huygens' fundamental axiom. It states that a system of weights cannot, on its own, move its collective center of mass upward—a concept essential to the later law of conservation of energy.</note>

<summary>Huygens defines the geometric and physical terminology for pendulum motion, including the "center of oscillation," and introduces his primary hypothesis that a system's center of gravity cannot rise spontaneously.</summary>
<keywords>center of oscillation, center of gravity, plane of oscillation, isochronous, axis of gravity, pendulum physics, Christiaan Huygens</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the list of definitions on page 93. Having defined the terms of his study, Huygens now introduces his fundamental Hypothesis—a principle that serves as the bedrock for his proofs regarding the center of oscillation. This principle is an early formulation of what we now call the conservation of mechanical energy.</meta>

### CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS ###

<margin>ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.</margin>
In these matters, altitude is considered according to the distance from a horizontal plane, and heavy bodies are assumed to strive to descend toward this plane along straight lines perpendicular to it. This same point is either explicitly stated by all who have treated the center of gravity, or must be supplied by readers, since without it the consideration of the center of gravity has no place.

So that our hypothesis itself might move no doubt <note>original: "scrupulum," literally a small sharp stone; figuratively a doubt or difficulty.</note>, we will show that it intends nothing other than what no one has ever denied: namely, that heavy bodies are not carried upward. For first, if we propose any one heavy body, it is beyond doubt that it cannot ascend higher by the force of its own weight. Now, it is understood to "ascend" specifically when its center of gravity ascends. But it is also necessary to grant the same concerning any number of weights joined together by inflexible lines <note>These are idealized "rods" that have no weight of their own and do not bend.</note>, since nothing forbids them from being considered as a single unit. Therefore, their common center of gravity cannot spontaneously ascend either.

But if any number of weights are positioned without being connected to one another, we know that they also have some common center of gravity. Whatever the altitude of this center is, I say that the altitude of the gravity composed of all of them ought to be reckoned as the same; since indeed all can be brought to that same altitude of the center of gravity, with no other power summoned than that which is inherent in the weights themselves, but only by joining them with inflexible lines at will and moving them about the center of gravity—which requires no determined force or power. Wherefore, just as it is impossible for certain weights placed in the same horizontal plane to all be lifted equally above that plane by the force of their own weight, so too the center of gravity of any weights whatsoever, however they are arranged, cannot reach an altitude greater than that which it already has. Furthermore, the fact that we said any weights can be brought to a horizontal plane passing through their common center of gravity, without using any force, will be shown as follows.

Let there be weights A, B, and C in given positions, whose common center of gravity is D. Let a horizontal plane be understood to be drawn through D, of which the straight section <note>A cross-section or line representing the plane.</note> is EF. Now let there be inflexible lines DA, DB, and DC, which connect the weights to one another rigidly; let these further be moved until A is at *e* in the plane EF. Indeed, with all the rods shifted through equal angles, B will now be at G and C at H.

Now again, let B and C be understood to be connected by a rod HG, which cuts the plane EF at F; where necessarily the center of gravity of the pair...

<summary>Huygens introduces his primary hypothesis: the center of gravity of a system of weights cannot rise spontaneously by its own force. He begins a geometric proof to show that any system of weights can be rearranged around its center of gravity without external work.</summary>
<keywords>center of gravity, altitude, horizontal plane, inflexible lines, hypothesis, spontaneous ascent, mechanical equilibrium</keywords>

He then transitions to applying this principle to pendulums and the impossibility of perpetual motion.</meta>

Thus, the weights H and G move again around point F as an axis, without any external force, and both are brought simultaneously to the plane E F, so that now the three which were previously at A, B, and C appear transferred to the same altitude as their center of gravity D, by their own equilibrium. This was what was to be shown. The same demonstration applies to any number of other weights.

<note>The diagram illustrates a series of weights (circles) connected by lines, used to prove that a system of weights will always seek its center of gravity and cannot spontaneously move that center to a higher elevation.</note>

Furthermore, this hypothesis of ours applies even to liquid bodies, and through it, not only can all those principles which Archimedes <note>Archimedes (c. 287–212 BCE) was the first to mathematically describe the buoyancy of floating bodies.</note> held regarding floating bodies be demonstrated, but also many other theorems of Mechanics. And truly, if the designers of new machines, who attempt perpetual motion <note>original: "motum perpetuum." Huygens was famously skeptical of "perpetual motion" machines, using the impossibility of such a device as a logical foundation for his physics.</note> with useless effort, knew how to use this same principle, they would easily detect their own errors and understand that such a thing is by no means possible by mechanical reasoning.

->II.<-

> *Once the air and every other obvious hindrance are removed—as we wish to be understood in the following demonstrations—the center of gravity of a swinging pendulum traverses equal arcs in its descent and its ascent.*

This has been demonstrated for a simple pendulum <note>A "simple pendulum" consists of a single point-mass on a weightless string, whereas a "composite pendulum" is a solid object (like a wooden rod or a brass disc) where the mass is distributed.</note> in Proposition 9 of *On the Descent of Heavy Bodies* <note>original: "de Descensu gravium," referring to Huygens' earlier mathematical treatments of falling objects.</note>. Experience declares that the same must also be held for a composite pendulum; for indeed, whatever the shape of the pendulum...

### PROPOSITION I.

*If any number of weights are located on the same side of a plane, and perpendicular lines are drawn from the center of gravity of each to that plane; each of these perpendiculars multiplied by its respective weight will, when summed together, equal the same amount as the perpendicular falling from the center of gravity of all the weights onto the same plane, multiplied by the total weight of all of them.*

Let there be weights A, B, and C, situated on the same side of a plane whose straight section is DF <note>In this geometric diagram, the plane is represented by the line DF as if we are looking at it from the edge.</note>, and let perpendiculars AD, BE, and CF be drawn from each weight to that plane. Let point G be the center of gravity of all the weights A, B, and C combined, from which a perpendicular GH is drawn to the same plane. I say that the sum of the products <term>product: the result of multiplying two numbers together</term> made by each individual weight multiplied by its own perpendicular is equal to the product of the line GH multiplied by the total sum of weights A, B, and C.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating a horizontal line segment with points labeled D, E, H, and F from left to right. Above the line, three small circles representing weights A, B, and C are connected by vertical line segments to points D, E, and F respectively. A point G is positioned to the right of B, with a vertical line segment descending to point H on the horizontal line. Below the horizontal line, three larger circles representing weights K, L, and M are connected by vertical line segments extending downwards from points D, E, and F respectively.</image-desc>

For let the perpendiculars drawn from each weight be understood to continue to the other side of the plane DF, and let each of these extensions—DK, EL, and FM—be equal in length to GH itself; and let all these lines represent inflexible rods, parallel to the horizon. Now, let there be placed at points K, L, and M weights of such a magnitude that each creates an equilibrium <note>original: "æquilibrium." In physics, this refers to a state where the weights on either side of a pivot point balance each other out.</note> with its opposite weight (A, B, or C) at the intersection of the plane (at points D, E, and F). Therefore, the sum of weights K, L, and M will counterpoise the sum of weights A, B, and C. Furthermore, based on the law of the lever, just as the length AD is to DK, so will weight K be to weight A; and consequently, the distance DA multiplied by the magnitude of weight A will equal DK (which is the same as GH) multiplied by weight K. Simili-

<meta>catchword: -larly (similiter)</meta>

<summary>Huygens begins his derivation of the center of oscillation by proving a foundational geometric lemma: the sum of the moments of individual weights relative to a plane is equal to the moment of their collective center of gravity.</summary>

...the product of E and B will equal EL (or GH) times L; and FC times C will equal FM (or GH) times M. Therefore, the sum of the products of AD by A, BE by B, and CF by C, will equal the sum of the products of GH by all the values K, L, and M. However, since K, L, and M balance the weights A, B, and C, they will also balance the same weights A, B, and C if they were suspended from their common center of gravity G. Hence, since the distance GH is equal to each of the individual lines DK, EL, and FM, it is necessary that the magnitudes A, B, and C, when taken together, equal the sum of K, L, and M. Thus, the sum of the products of GH times all the weights A, B, and C will equal the sum of the products of DA times A, EB times B, and FC times C. <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum" — which was to be demonstrated (Q.E.D.).</note>

Even though the lines AD, GH, and CF were placed parallel to the horizon and the plane was set upright in this demonstration, it is clear that if all these elements were moved together into any other position, the equality of the products would remain the same, since all the lines are the same as before. Therefore, the proposition stands.

### PROPOSITION II.

*Assuming the previous conditions, if all the weights A, B, and C are equal; I say that the sum of all the perpendicular lines AD, BE, and CF is equal to the perpendicular GH, drawn from the center of gravity, multiplied by the number of weights.*

Since the sum of the products made by each weight multiplied by its own perpendicular is equal to the product of GH and the total sum of the weights; and since here, because the weights are equal, that sum of products is equal to the product of a single weight multiplied by the sum of all the perpendiculars; and likewise, the product of GH and all the weights is the same as the product of one weight and GH multiplied by the number of weights: it is clear that the sum of the perpendiculars must necessarily equal GH multiplied by the number of weights. <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum"</note>

### PROPOSITION III.

*If several magnitudes <note>Huygens uses "magnitudes" to refer to physical objects or weights.</note> all descend or ascend, even if through unequal intervals; the heights of the descent or ascent of each, multiplied by the magnitude itself, produce a sum of products equal to the product of the height of the descent or ascent of the center of gravity of all the magnitudes, multiplied by the sum of all the magnitudes.*

<summary>This page concludes the proof that the weighted sum of distances from a plane equals the total weight times the center of gravity's distance, then applies this to cases where weights are equal or are in motion (ascending/descending).</summary>
<keywords>center of gravity, center of oscillation, weights, mathematical proof, perpendicular lines, displacement, magnitudes, mechanics</keywords>

In the previous pages, Huygens established how the collective center of gravity relates to the positions of individual weights. <margin>On the Center of Oscillation.</margin>

Let there be <term>magnitudes</term><note>Huygens uses "magnitudes" to refer to the weights or masses in the system.</note> A, B, and C, which descend from positions A, B, and C into D, E, and F; or ascend from D, E, and F into A, B, and C. And let the center of gravity of them all, while they are in A, B, and C, be at the same altitude as point G; but when they are in D, E, and F, let it be at the same altitude as point H. I say that the sum of the <term>products</term><note>In 17th-century mechanics, the "product" of a weight and its altitude is a precursor to the modern concept of potential energy.</note> of the altitude AD times A, BE times B, and CF times C, is equal to the product of GH times all the weights A, B, and C.

![A geometric diagram showing several circles labeled A, B, C, D, E, F. A, B, C are aligned vertically above points on a horizontal line labeled M, N, O, P. D, E, F are also aligned vertically above M, N, O, P. Additionally, a point G is vertically aligned with A, B, C, and a point H is vertically aligned with D, E, F, illustrating changes in vertical position or altitude.](diagram.png)

For let a horizontal plane be understood, the cross-section of which is the straight line MP, and let the extended lines AD, BE, CF, and GH fall upon it at points M, N, O, and P.

<margin>* Proposition 1 of this work.</margin>

Therefore, because the sum of the products of AM times A, BN times B, and CO times C is equal to the product of GP times all the weights A, B, and C *. And similarly, because the sum of the products of DM times A, EN times B, and FO times C is equal to the product of HP times all the weights A, B, and C; it follows that the difference by which the former products exceed the latter is equal to the product of GH times all the magnitudes A, B, and C. Moreover, it is manifest that this said difference is equal to the products of AD times A, BE times B, and CF times C. Therefore, these together will also be equal to the product of GH times all the weights A, B, and C. <note>original: "quod erat demonstrandum" — which was to be demonstrated.</note>

### PROPOSITION IV.

If a pendulum composed of several weights, and released from rest, has completed any part of a full oscillation, and if from that point onwards the individual weights are understood—having been released from their common bond—to convert their acquired velocities upward and to ascend as far as they can; this having been done, the center of gravity of the whole composed of them will have returned to the same altitude which it occupied before the oscillation began. <note>This is Huygens' famous "Principle of the Equality of Descent and Ascent," a crucial step toward the law of conservation of energy.</note>

<summary>Huygens completes the geometric proof relating the displacement of a collective center of gravity to the displacement of individual weights and introduces Proposition IV, a fundamental principle of mechanics stating that weights cannot lift their collective center of gravity higher than its starting point.</summary>

->THE OSCILLATORY CLOCK<-

<margin>ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.</margin>

Let there be a compound <term>pendulum</term><note>A "compound" pendulum consists of multiple masses or an extended body, unlike a "simple" pendulum which is a single point mass.</note> composed of any number of weights A, B, and C, attached to a rod or surface that lacks weight. Let it be suspended from an axis passing through point D, which should be understood as being perpendicular to the plane shown here. In this same plane, let the <term>center of gravity</term><note>The point at which the entire weight of the body may be considered to be concentrated.</note> E of the weights A, B, and C also be placed; and let the line of the center DE be inclined to the perpendicular line DF at the angle EDF, the pendulum having been pulled back to that point. Now, let it be supposed that it is released from there and completes any part of its oscillation, so that the weights A, B, and C arrive at G, H, and K. From there, once the common bond of the rod is released, let each weight be understood to turn its acquired speed upward (which could be done by striking certain inclined planes, such as QQ) and ascend as far as they are able, namely to L, M, and N. When they have arrived there, let the center of gravity of them all be point P. I say that this point P is at the same altitude as point E.

![Geometric diagram showing a pivot point D from which weights A, B, and C are suspended. Various arcs and lines track their movement through positions G, H, K and their ultimate heights L, M, N. A vertical axis D-F is shown, and points E and P represent the centers of gravity for different configurations of the weights. Inclined planes labeled Q are positioned to redirect the motion of the weights as they decouple from the pendulum rod.](diagram.png)

<margin>* Proposition 4, part 2.</margin>

For first, it is indeed established that P is no higher than E, according to the first of our assumed <term>hypotheses</term><note>Huygens refers to his fundamental axioms of motion established at the beginning of this section, specifically that weights cannot move their own center of gravity upward.</note>. But we will show that it cannot be lower in the following way. For let P be lower than E, if it were possible; and let the weights be understood to fall back down from the same heights to which they ascended, which are LG, MH, and NK. From this, it is certain that they acquire the same speeds for descending as they had for ascending to those heights <note>original: "vnde quidem easdem celeritates ipsis acquiri constat, quas habebant ad ascendendum ad istas altitudines"</note>, that is, those very speeds which they had acquired by the motion of the pendulum from C, B, and A to K, H, and G. Therefore, if they are now returned with the said speeds to the rod or surface to which they were attached, and they cling to it simultaneously and continue their motion according to the arcs already begun; which will happen if, before they reach the rod, they are understood to be reflected by the inclined planes QQ—

Thus, the center of gravity of the pendulum, E, traverses equal arcs EF and FR in its descent and ascent, and is therefore found at the same height at R as it was at E. Therefore, the center of gravity P is not lower than E. But neither was it higher. Therefore, it must be equally high, which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION V.

*Given a pendulum composed of any number of weights, if each weight is multiplied by the square of its distance from the axis of oscillation, and the sum of these products is divided by the product of the total weight and the distance of the common center of gravity of them all from that same axis; the result will be the length of a **simple pendulum** <term>isochronous</term><note>meaning it swings in the same amount of time (from the Greek "isos" meaning equal and "chronos" meaning time)</note> to the compound one, or the distance between the axis and the center of oscillation of the compound pendulum itself.*

Let the weights composing the pendulum be A, B, and C (considering neither their shape nor size, but only their weight <note>Huygens treats these as point-masses for the purpose of the geometric proof</note>), suspended

The center diagram shows a single arc GHMO and a vertical line FH used for comparison. * Prop. 1 of this book <note>original: "Prop. 1. huj." refers to the first proposition of this specific part of the treatise.</note>
</margin>
...[the squares] of their distances, the sum of the products will be $a \cdot e \cdot e + b \cdot f \cdot f + c \cdot g \cdot g$ <note>The notation "ee" represents the square of distance "e".</note>. And again, by multiplying the sum of the weights by the distance of the center of gravity of them all, the product will be equal to $a \cdot d + b \cdot d + c \cdot d$*. From this, by dividing the first product by the second, we obtain the ratio:
-> $a \cdot e \cdot e + b \cdot f \cdot f + c \cdot g \cdot g$ / $a \cdot d + b \cdot d + c \cdot d$ <-
If the length of a **simple pendulum** FG—which we shall also call $x$—is made equal to this length, I assert that this simple pendulum is **isochronous** <term>isochronous</term><note>From the Greek for "equal time"; meaning the two pendulums will complete their swings in exactly the same duration.</note> to that compound one.

For let both the pendulum FG and the line of the center DE be displaced from the vertical line by equal angles—the former from FH and the latter from DK—and then let them be released to swing. On the line DE, let the length DL be taken equal to the length FG. Thus, the weight G of the simple pendulum FG will traverse the arc GM in a full oscillation, which the vertical line FH will bisect. However, the point L <note>a point on the compound pendulum at the same distance from the pivot as the simple pendulum's weight</note> will traverse an arc LN, which is similar and equal to the first arc, and which the vertical line DK will divide in the middle. Likewise, the center of gravity E will traverse a similar arc EI. But if it can be shown that at any points chosen on the arcs GM and NL that divide them in the same way, such as $o$ and $p$, the **speed** of the weight G at $o$ is the same as the speed of point L at $p$, it will then follow that both arcs are traversed in equal times; and therefore, the simple pendulum FG is isochronous to the compound pendulum composed of weights A, B, and C. This is shown in the following way:

Suppose first, if it were possible, that the speed of point L when it reaches $p$ is greater than the speed of weight G at $o$. Now, it is clear that while point L traverses the arc LP, the center of gravity E simultaneously traverses a similar arc EQ. Let perpendiculars be drawn upward from points Q, P, and O, meeting the chords of the arcs EI, LN, and GM at R, S, and Y; and let the line SP be called $y$. From this, since the distance LD ($x$) is to the distance ED ($d$) as the height SP ($y$) is to the height RQ, it follows that RQ will be equal to $d \cdot y / x$. Now, because the weight G has that speed at $o$ by which it is able to ascend to the same height from which it descended—namely through the arc $om$, or the perpendicular $oy$ which is equal to $ps$—it follows that point L, when it reaches $p$, will have a speed greater than that required to ascend to the height $ps$. But while L passes through $p$, the weights A, B, and C simultaneously traverse arcs similar to Lp, namely AT, BV, and CX. And the speed of point L at $p$ is to the speed of weight A at T (since they are held by the same connection) as the distance DL is to the distance DA. But as the square of the speed of point L at $p$ is to the square of the speed of weight A at T, so is the height to which it can ascend with that speed to the height to which it can ascend with this [other] speed*. 
<margin>
* Prop. 3 & 4, part 2.
</margin>
Therefore also, as the square of the distance DL (which is $x \cdot x$) is to the square of the distance DA (which is $a \cdot a$), so is the height to which one ascends with the speed of point L when it is at $p$ (which height was said to be greater than $ps$ or $y$) to the height to which one ascends with the speed of weight A at T—assuming that after it reached T, it were released from the pendulum...

<sig>N iij</sig>

<summary>Huygens provides the mathematical formula for the length of an equivalent simple pendulum and begins a geometric proof to show that the speeds of the compound and simple systems must be identical for them to be isochronous.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof of Proposition V from page 101. Huygens is using a proof by contradiction (reductio ad absurdum) to demonstrate that the speed of a point on a compound pendulum must match the speed of a simple pendulum of a specific length, otherwise, the center of gravity would defy the laws of physics by rising higher than its starting point.</meta>

<header>CHRISTIAN HUYGENS</header>

<margin>
ON THE CENTER 
OF OSCILLA-
TION.
</margin>...were to turn its motion upward separately. This height would therefore be greater than <gloss>e²y</gloss>/<gloss>x²</gloss> <note>original: "e e y / x x". In this notation, doubling a letter like "ee" denotes the square of that value.</note>.

By the same reasoning, the height to which weight B would ascend, with the speed acquired through arc BV, would be greater than <gloss>f²y</gloss>/<gloss>x²</gloss>. And the height to which weight C would ascend, with the speed acquired through arc CX, would be greater than <gloss>g²y</gloss>/<gloss>x²</gloss>. Whence, by multiplying each of these heights by their respective weights, the sum of the products will be greater than <gloss>(ae²y + bf²y + cg²y)</gloss>/<gloss>x²</gloss>, which is thereby proved to also be greater than <gloss>(ady + bdy + cdy)</gloss>/<gloss>x</gloss>. For because the length *x* is set equal to <gloss>(ae² + bf² + cg²)</gloss>/<gloss>(ad + bd + cd)</gloss>, then *x* multiplied by <gloss>(ad + bd + cd)</gloss> is equal to <gloss>ae² + bf² + cg²</gloss>. And by multiplying all terms by *y*, and dividing by *x²*, <gloss>(ady + bdy + cdy)</gloss>/<gloss>x</gloss> will be equal to <gloss>(ae²y + bf²y + cg²y)</gloss>/<gloss>x²</gloss>. From this, that which was stated follows.

Moreover, that sum of products is equal to that which is made by multiplying the height to which the common center of gravity of weights A, B, and C ascends, by the sum of those weights, *a + b + c*; assuming, as said, that each weight moves upward separately as far as it can. However, the quantity <gloss>(ady + bdy + cdy)</gloss>/<gloss>x</gloss> is produced from the descent of the center of gravity of these same weights (which descent is RQ, or <gloss>dy</gloss>/<gloss>x</gloss>, as was found above), also multiplied by the same sum of weights *a + b + c*. Therefore, since the first product was shown to be greater than the second, it follows that the ascent of the center of gravity of weights A, B, and C—if, having left the pendulum where it arrived at T, V, and X, each weight converted its acquired speed upward—would be greater than the descent of the same center of gravity while they moved from A, B, and C to T, V, and X. This is **absurd** <note>This "absurdity" refers to a violation of what we now call the Conservation of Energy; Huygens' principle states that a system of weights cannot, by its own gravity, raise its center of gravity higher than its original position.</note>, since the said ascent must be equal to the descent, by the preceding proposition.

In the same way, if it were said that the speed of point L, when it reaches P, is less than the speed of weight G when it reaches O; we would show that the possible ascent of the center of gravity of weights A, B, and C is less than the descent, which contradicts the same preceding proposition. Therefore, it remains that the speed of point L, moved to P, is the same as that of weight G at O. Whence, as stated above, it follows that the **simple pendulum** FG is **isochronous** <note>original: "isochronum", meaning they swing with the same period of time.</note> to the compound one composed of A, B, and C.

*...is applied to the distance of the common center of gravity from the same axis of oscillation, multiplied according to the number of the weights themselves, the length of a simple pendulum <term>isochronous</term> <note>Moving in equal time; a simple pendulum that swings at the same rate as the complex one.</note> with the composite one will result.*

*Suppose the same conditions as before, but let all the weights be understood to be equal to one another, and let each be called **a**. Furthermore, let no physical size be considered for them, but let them be treated as points of minimum extension.*

*Therefore, the length of the isochronous simple pendulum, according to the preceding proposition, will be <gloss> (ae² + af² + ag²) / (ad + ad + ad) </gloss> <note>Huygens uses "ee" to represent e squared (e²).
