Or, because the quantity to be divided and the divisor are both divided by **a**, the same length will now become <gloss> (e² + f² + g²) / 3d </gloss>. By this is signified the sum of the squares of the distances of the weights from the axis of oscillation, divided by the distance of the center of gravity of all the weights from the same axis of oscillation, multiplied by the number of the weights themselves, which here is 3. For it is easily perceived that this number, by which the distance **d** is multiplied, necessarily corresponds to the number of weights. Wherefore the proposal is established.*

*But if equal weights are joined in a single straight line and suspended from its upper end, it is established that the distance of the center of gravity of the whole composition from the axis of oscillation, multiplied by the number of weights, is equal to the sum of the distances of all the weights from the same axis of oscillation; <margin>* See Proposition 2 of this work.</margin> and therefore, in this case, the length of the isochronous simple pendulum will also be obtained if the sum of the squares of the distances of the individual weights from the axis of oscillation is divided by the sum of those same distances.*

### DEFINITION XIV.

*IF there should be in the same plane a certain figure and a straight line which touches it from the outside; and if another straight line, perpendicular to the plane, is carried around the perimeter of the figure, it describes a certain surface; if this surface is then cut by a plane drawn through the aforementioned tangent line and inclined to the plane of the figure: the solid comprised by these two planes and the part of the described surface intercepted between both planes shall be called a **Wedge** <note>original Latin: "Cuneus"</note> cut off above that figure as its base.*

->DEFINITIO XV.<-

# ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.

...of the figure ACB is equal to the rectangle formed by FA and LA, multiplied by the number of particles.

<margin>* Prop. 1 of this work.</margin>
<margin>* The preceding Proposition.</margin>
It is indeed established from the preceding demonstration that the heights of the individual parallelepipeds <note>A three-dimensional solid with six faces that are parallelograms; Huygens uses these to model volumes in his geometric proofs.</note>, such as GK, are equal to the distances of the particles—which form their bases, such as G—from the line AE. Therefore, if we now multiply the parallelepiped GK by the distance GH, it is the same as if the particle G were multiplied by the square of the distance GH. And the same holds true for all the others. Now, all the products of the parallelepipeds multiplied by their distances from the line AE are together equal to the product of the wedge <note>original: "cuneus"; a geometric solid used here to represent a distribution of mass or weight.</note> ABD and the distance LA, because the wedge balances <note>literally "weighs upon"</note> at point L. Therefore, the sum of the products of each individual particle G and the squares of their distances from line AE will also equal the product of the wedge ABD and the line LA; that is, the product of the figure ACB and the rectangle formed by FA and LA. For the wedge ABD is equal to the product of figure ACB and the line FA. Again, because figure ACB is equal to the product of a single particle G and the total number of those particles, it follows that the said product of figure ACB and the rectangle formed by FA and LA is equal to the product of particle G and the rectangle formed by FA and LA, multiplied by the number of particles G. Consequently, the aforementioned sum of the products of the individual particles G and the squares of their distances from line AE—or the product of one particle G and the sum of all these squares—will also be equal to this. Therefore, by omitting the multiplication by particle G on both sides, it follows that the sum of these squares must be equal to the rectangle formed by FA and LA, multiplied by the number of particles into which figure ACB is understood to be divided. This is what was to be demonstrated.

Therefore, all the squares of $FK$ will be equal to the same number of squares of $EA$, with as many double rectangles $EA \cdot AG$, and furthermore the same number of squares of $AG$ with the same number of rectangles $AG \cdot GH$. Yet these three—namely, the square of $EA$ with the double rectangle $EA \cdot AG$ and the square of $AG$—make the square of $EG$. Therefore, it is apparent that all the squares of $FK$ are equal to the same number of squares of $EG$, together with the same number of rectangles $AG \cdot GH$. Which was to be demonstrated.

Furthermore, in all other cases, all the squares of $FK$ are equal to the same number of squares of $KL$, minus twice as many rectangles $KL \cdot LF$, plus the same number of squares of $LF$; that is, the same number of squares of $EA$, minus as many double rectangles $EA \cdot AG$, plus as many squares of $AG$, with...

### ->PROPOSITION XII.<-

Let the points A, B, C, and D be given; let their center of gravity (or the center of equal weights suspended from them) be E. With center E, let any circle $Ff$ be described. If any point, such as F, is taken on its circumference, and straight lines AF, BF, CF, and DF are drawn to it from the given points, I say that the squares of all these lines, taken together, are equal to a certain given plane area, and this sum remains always the same regardless of where point F is chosen on the circumference.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a large circle intersected by vertical and horizontal lines, forming a grid. Several points are labeled with letters: A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, M, N, O, P, R, S, T, V, X, Y, Z. Point F is on the circumference of the circle. Lines radiate from F to points A, B, C, D, and E, forming various triangles. Other lines connect points within the grid and to the axes, indicating perpendiculars and relationships between points.</image-desc>

For let the straight lines GH and GK be drawn, meeting at a right angle, such that all the given points lie on the same side of each line. From each point, let perpendiculars be drawn to both of these lines: $AI$ and $AK$; $BM$ and $BO$; $CN$ and $CP$; $DH$ and $DQ$. From the center of gravity E, and from the point F, let perpendiculars $ER$ and $FS$ be drawn to one of the two lines, either GH or GK. Likewise, from the given points, let perpendiculars $AV, BX, CY,$ and $DZ$ be drawn to the line $FS$ itself. And let $FT$ be the perpendicular drawn to the line $ER$. Furthermore, let:

-> $AL = a$ | $AK = e$ | radius $EF = z$ <-
-> $BM = b$ | $BO = f$ | $GS = x$ <-
-> $CN = c$ | $CP = g$ <-
-> $DH = d$ | $DQ = h$ <-

Now, because E is the center of gravity of the points A, B, C, and D: if the perpendiculars $AL, BM, CN, DH$ are added together, and the sum of all of them is divided into as many parts as there are given points,

...equal parts [of the] sum of the perpendiculars AK, BO, CP, DQ, the perpendicular drawn from E to the line GK, which is RG, will be equal to one of them \*. Therefore, if the sum of all the perpendiculars AL, BM, CN, DH—or $a + b + c + d$—is called $l$: and the sum of all AK, BO, CP, DQ—or $e + f + g + h$—is called $m$: and the number expressing the quantity of given points is called $\theta$ <note>Huygens uses the Greek letter theta ($\theta$) to represent the total number of points in the system.</note>; then ER will be $l/\theta$, and RG will be $m/\theta$. And since GS is $x$, then RS (or FT) will be $x - m/\theta$; or $m/\theta - x$, if GR is greater than GS; and the square of FT will always be $x^2 - 2xm/\theta + m^2/\theta^2$. When this is subtracted from the square of FE (which is $Z^2$ <note>Here $Z$ represents the radius of the circle described around the center of gravity E.</note>), there remains the square of TE, which is $Z^2 - x^2 + 2xm/\theta - m^2/\theta^2$. Consequently, TE is $\sqrt{Z^2 - x^2 + 2xm/\theta - m^2/\theta^2}$. However, ER was $l/\theta$. Therefore, TR is $l/\theta$ plus or minus $\sqrt{Z^2 - x^2 + 2xm/\theta - m^2/\theta^2}$. For the sake of brevity, let this TR be called $y$. 

Now, let us further collect the sum of the squares of all the lines FA, FB, FC, and FD. The square of AF is equal to the squares of AV and VF <note>This is a coordinate-based application of the Pythagorean theorem: the square of the hypotenuse (distance FA) equals the sum of the squares of the horizontal and vertical differences.</note>. Now, AV is equal to the difference between the two lines VK and AK, or the two lines SG and AK; and therefore AV is $x - e$ or $e - x$; and the square of AV is $x^2 - 2ex + e^2$. Furthermore, VF is equal to the difference between the two lines FS and VS, or the two lines FS and AL; and therefore VF is $y - a$ or $a - y$; and the square of VF is $y^2 - 2ay + a^2$. By adding the squares AV and VF, the square of FA becomes $x^2 - 2ex + e^2 + y^2 - 2ay + a^2$. In the same way, the squares of the remaining lines FB, FC, and FD will be found; and all of them, arranged in order, will be these:

> the square of FA = $x^2 - 2ex + e^2 + y^2 - 2ay + a^2$.
> the square of FB = $x^2 - 2fx + f^2 + y^2 - 2by + b^2$.
> the square of FC = $x^2 - 2gx + g^2 + y^2 - 2cy + c^2$.
> the square of FD = $x^2 - 2hx + h^2 + y^2 - 2dy + d^2$.

The sum of these indeed—if we let the squares $e^2 + f^2 + g^2 + h^2 = n^2$; and the squares $a^2 + b^2 + c^2 + d^2 = k^2$—will be $\theta x^2 - 2mx + n^2 + \theta y^2 - 2ly + k^2$. This is because $\theta$ was the number of given points and therefore the number of squares, and it had been established that $e + f + g + h = m$, and $a + b + c + d = l$.

In that sum, if for the terms $\theta y^2$ and $2ly$ we substitute that which $y$ stood for, namely $l/\theta \pm \sqrt{Z^2 - x^2 + 2xm/\theta - m^2/\theta^2}$, then $+\theta y^2$ becomes $l^2/\theta \pm 2l\sqrt{Z^2 - x^2 + 2xm/\theta - m^2/\theta^2} + \theta Z^2 - \theta x^2 + 2xm - m^2/\theta$; and $-2ly$ becomes $-2l^2/\theta \mp 2l\sqrt{Z^2 - x^2 + 2xm/\theta - m^2/\theta^2}$ (or if $+\theta y^2$ is taken with the minus sign in the root, the corresponding $-2ly$ takes the plus sign). And therefore, in either case, for $\theta y^2 - 2ly$ we will have $-l^2/\theta + \theta Z^2 - \theta x^2 + 2xm - m^2/\theta$. When the remaining quantities are added to this, the sum of the previously mentioned...

...combined with those contained [in the previous section], $\theta x^2 - 2xm + n^2 + k^2$, the total sum will be produced—namely of the squares FA, FB, FC, and FD—which is equal to <note>Huygens uses the symbol $\infty$ to mean "is equal to," a common notation in the 17th century.</note> $\theta z^2 + n^2 + k^2 - \frac{m^2 l^2}{\theta}$. This is clearly a given value <note>literally "a given plane," referring to a geometric area representing a constant value</note>, since all these quantities are given; and it is always found to be the same, wherever point F is taken on the circumference. This is what was to be demonstrated.

If the given points are assumed to have different weights <note>original: *gravitates*</note> that are commensurable with one another—for instance, if point A weighs 2, B weighs 3, C weighs 4, and D weighs 7—and after finding their center of gravity, a circle is again described, and lines are drawn from the given points to a point on the circumference, and the squares of each are multiplied according to the number of its weight (so that the square AF is doubled, BF tripled, CF quadrupled, and DF septupled); I say again that the sum of all will be equal to a given space, and always the same, wherever the point on the circumference is taken. For this is evident from the preceding demonstration, if we imagine the points themselves to be multiplied according to the numbers of the gravity attributed to each; as if, for instance, two points were joined at A, three at B, four at C, and seven at D, and all of them were equally heavy.

### PROPOSITION XIII.

*If a plane figure, or a line existing in a plane, is suspended in various different ways from points which, being taken in the same plane, are at an equal distance from its center of gravity; when set into lateral motion, it is isochronous* <term>isochronous: from the Greek for "equal time," meaning the pendulum takes the same amount of time to complete one swing</term> *to itself.*

Let there be a plane figure, or a line existing in a plane, ABC, whose center of gravity is D. With this same center, let a circumference of a circle be described in the same plane, ECF. I say that if the figure is suspended from any point on that circle, such as E, C, or G, and set into motion to the side, it is isochronous to itself, or to the same simple pendulum.

Let the first suspension be from point E; however, when the point is outside the figure, as it is here, we must imagine the line EH (from which the figure hangs) to be rigid and immovably attached to it.

<margin>* Prop. 6 of this [book].</margin>
Let the figure ABC be understood as being divided into the smallest equal particles. From the centers of gravity of all these particles, let lines be drawn to the point E; it is manifest that when the figure is moved to the side, these lines are perpendicular to the axis of oscillation. Therefore, the squares of all these perpendiculars, divided by the line ED multiplied according to the number of particles into which the figure is divided, constitute the length of a simple pendulum that is isochronous to the figure *.

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Huygens completes the proof that the sum of squared distances to a circle's edge is constant and introduces Proposition XIII: a plane object's oscillation period remains the same if its suspension point is moved along a circle centered on its center of gravity.</summary>

which is K L. Moreover, when the figure is suspended from point G, the length of the simple isochronous pendulum <note>A "simple isochronous pendulum" is an idealized mathematical pendulum—a single point mass on a string—that swings in exactly the same time as the complex physical object being studied.</note> is found again by dividing the sum of the squares of all the lines drawn from the particles of the figure to point G by the line G D multiplied by the number of those same particles <margin>* Proposition 6 of this [work].</margin>. Therefore, since points G and E are on the circumference described with center D (which is the center of gravity of figure A B C, or the center of gravity of all the points which are the centers of the equal particles of the figure); it follows that the sum of the squares of the lines drawn from the said particles to point G will be equal to the sum of the squares of the lines drawn from the same particles to point E <margin>* Preceding Proposition.</margin>. 

<image-desc>A circular diagram containing a large right-angled triangle ABC, with its hypotenuse AC. The triangle is filled with a grid of smaller squares representing individual particles. An external vertical line segment KL is shown to the upper right of the circle, representing the length of a simple pendulum.</image-desc>

These sums of squares, in either suspension, are divided by <note>Huygens uses the term "applied to," a geometric way of describing division where a volume or area is distributed along a line.</note> equal magnitudes: namely, in the suspension from G, by the line <note>The original text here reads B D, likely a typographical error for G D, which represents the distance from the pivot to the center of gravity.</note> G D multiplied by the number of all particles; and in the suspension from E, by the line D E multiplied by the number of the same particles. Therefore it is clear, from this latter division—namely when the suspension is from G—that the length of the isochronous pendulum becomes the same as from the former division; that is, the same as the length K L. 

In the same way, if the figure is suspended from C, or any other point on the circumference E C F, it will be proved to be isochronous to the same pendulum K L. Thus the proposition is established.

*Given a figure conceived as being composed of the smallest equal particles, from all of which perpendicular lines are understood to be drawn to a given straight line; to find the sum of all the squares formed by these [lines], or a plane which, when multiplied by the number of particles, is equal to the said sum of the squares.* <note>Huygens is describing a geometric method to calculate the "moment of inertia." In the 17th century, before formal calculus, this was visualized as the sum of squared distances of every tiny part of an object from its axis of rotation.</note>

And it being understood that, from each of the smallest equal particles constituting the solid **A B C D**, such as **F**, straight lines are drawn perpendicular to the given line through **E**, just as here **F E**; it is required to find the sum of all the squares of [these lines] **F E**.

...any of [the lines] drawn perpendicular to the line given through **E**, such as **F E**, is equal to the squares of the two [lines] **F G** and **F H**, which are drawn perpendicularly from that same particle to the aforementioned planes through **E G** and **E C** <note>original: "47. lib. 1. Eucl." This is the Pythagorean theorem ($a^2 + b^2 = c^2$). Huygens is applying it to distances in 3D space.</note>. Therefore, if we can know the sum of the squares formed by all the perpendiculars that fall from all the particles into the said planes through **E G** and through **E C**, we will also have the sum, equal to this, of the squares from the perpendiculars that fall from all those same particles to the straight line given through point **E**.

Indeed, that former sum of squares will be gathered in this way. First, let a plane figure **O Q P** be given at the side of the solid figure **A B C D**, having the same height as the solid itself, and which is of such a kind that, when cut by straight lines **Q Q**, **R R**, which correspond to the planes **M M**, **N N** cutting the solid figure **A B C D** (and those parallel to them), the ratio of the said lines to each other is the same as the ratio of those planes, provided those that correspond to each other in order are taken. For example, if line **R R** is to **Q Q** as plane **N N** is to **M M**. Because if the plane figure **O Q P** is understood to be divided into as many equal smallest particles as are understood to be in the solid **A B C D**, there will also be in each segment of the plane figure, such as **Q Q R R**, the same number of particles as there are in the segment of the solid figure **M M N N** corresponding to that segment. And consequently, the sum of the squares from the perpendiculars of all the particles of figure **O Q P** to the plane **E G** will be equal to the sum of the squares from the perpendiculars of all the particles of the solid figure to the same plane **E G** extended. Furthermore, that sum of squares will be given if there are given in figure **O Q P**, and in its "wedge" <note>A "wedge" (cuneus) in this context refers to a specific geometric construction used to calculate moments, a common technique before the invention of integral calculus.</note>, those things which we said were required in Proposition 9 of this work. Therefore, with these given, the sum of the squares from the perpendiculars which are drawn from all the particles of the solid **A B C D** to the plane **E G** will also be given.

Now let another plane figure **S Y T Z** be placed, of the same width as the solid **A B C D**—that is, bounded by the planes **B Y** and **D Z** which touch the solid and are parallel to the plane **E A C**—and which is of such a kind that, when cut by straight lines **V V**, **X X**, etc., which correspond to the planes **K K**, **L L** cutting the figure **A B C D** (and those parallel to them), it creates the same ratio between these lines as between those planes, if those that correspond to each other are taken. And so, again, all the squares together of the perpendiculars falling from the particles of figure **S Y T Z** to the straight line **S T** will be equal to all the squares of the perpendiculars which are drawn from the particles of the solid **A B C D** to the plane **A C**. Moreover, the sum of those squares will be given if the distance of the center of gravity of figure **S Y T Z** from the line **B Y** or **D Z** is given, as well as the distance likewise of the center of grav[ity]—

### PROPOSITION XV.

*With the same conditions established, if the solid **A B C D** is of such a kind that the plane figure **S Y T Z**, which is proportional to it, does not have a known distance of the center of gravity from the tangents **B Y** or **D Z**, or if the sub-centric point <note>original: "subcentrica", a term Huygens uses to describe the center of gravity of a wedge-shaped section of a solid</note> of the wedge cut off over it by a plane through the same **B Y** or **D Z** is unknown; yet in the proportional figure at the side, **O Q P**, the distance **Φ P** is given, by which the center of gravity of the half-figure **O P V** is distant from the axis **O P**; it will be possible from this to find the sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of solid **A B C D** from the plane **E C**. However, it is necessary that all sections, **N N**, **M M**, be similar planes, and that the plane **E C** passes through the centers of gravity of all of them; as happens in a prism, pyramid, cone, conoids, and many other figures. And it is necessary that the distances of the centers of gravity of those planes, upon tangents parallel to the axis of oscillation, be given; as well as the sub-centric points of the <term>wedges</term> <note>original: "cuneus". Huygens uses the geometry of wedges to calculate how mass is distributed around an axis.</note> which are cut off over them by planes drawn through those same tangents.*

<meta>This page continues the geometric derivation of Proposition XV. Huygens is using the "method of indivisibles"—treating solids as collections of infinitely many thin segments—to calculate the total sum of the squares of the distances of a body's particles from a plane. This value is a precursor to what modern physicists call the "moment of inertia," a necessary component for determining the center of oscillation in a pendulum.</meta>

ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>

...[will have the squared] ratio <note>The Latin 'catam' is the conclusion of 'duplicatam' from the previous page, indicating a "duplicate ratio," or the ratio of the squares.</note> of the square of **B D** to the square of **N N**; that is, the same ratio as the square of **v v** to the square of **R R** in the proportional figure. Therefore, the aforementioned first sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of segment **B N N D** from the plane **E C** will be to the other sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of segment **N M M N** as the square of **v v** is to the square of **R R**. By the same reasoning, it will be shown that the sums of the squares of the distances of the particles in the remaining segments of the solid **A B C D** are to one another in the ratio of the squares formed by the lines in the figure **o v v** which correspond to the base of each segment.

Wherefore, the sum of the squares of the distances of all the particles of all the segments of the solid **A B C D** from the plane **E C** will be to the sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of an equal number of segments (each equal to the largest segment—that is, of a cylinder or prism **B D S S** having the same base and height as the solid **A B C D**) as all the squares of the lines **v v**, **R R**, **Q Q**, etc., are to an equal number of squares equal to the largest, **v v**. This is the same as the ratio of the solid of revolution **o v v** <note>Huygens uses the concept of a "solid of revolution"—a shape formed by rotating a plane figure around an axis—to simplify the calculation of these sums.</note> about the axis **o p** to the cylinder **v v ω ω** which has the same base and height. 

Now, it is established that this ratio of the solid **o v v** to the cylinder **v v ω ω** is composed of the ratio of the planes by whose rotation they are generated—that is, the ratio of the plane **o p v** to the rectangle **p ω**—and the ratio of the distances by which the centers of gravity of these planes are distant from the axis **o p**; that is, the ratio of **p φ** to **p Δ**. And the first of these ratios, namely of the plane **o p v** to the rectangle **p ω**, is the same as that of the solid **A B C D** to the cylinder or prism **B D S S**; that is, the same as the ratio of the number of particles of the solid **A B C D** to the number of particles of the cylinder or prism **B D S S**. 

The other ratio, namely **p φ** to **p Δ**, is the same (by construction) as that of the space **z** to the rectangle **B C K**. Thus, the said sum of the squares of the distances of all the particles of the solid **A B C D** from the plane **E C** will have to the sum of the squares of the distances of all the particles of the cylinder or prism **B D S S** from the same plane, that ratio which is composed of the ratio of the number of particles of the solid **A B C D** to the number of particles of the cylinder or prism **B D S S**, and of the ratio of the space **z** to the rectangle **B C K**. This is the ratio of the rectangle **z** multiplied by the number of particles of the solid **A B C D**, to the rectangle **B C K** multiplied by the number of particles of the cylinder or prism **B D S S**.

But the fourth of these magnitudes is equal to the second; namely, the rectangle **B C K** multiplied by the number of particles of the cylinder or prism **B D S S** is equal to the sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of that same prism or cylinder **B D S S** from the plane **E C**; since that same rectangle **B C K**, multiplied...

<summary>Huygens uses geometric proportions and the properties of solids of revolution to calculate the sum of the squares of the distances of a body's particles from a plane. This mathematical proof allows him to relate the complex internal structure of a solid body to a simpler cylinder or prism, facilitating the calculation of the center of oscillation.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the mathematical proof of Proposition XV, which calculates the sum of the squares of distances from a plane for various geometric solids—a concept known today as the "moment of inertia." It then transitions to Proposition XVI, which establishes a fundamental symmetry in pendulum motion: that an object's swing period remains the same if the distance from the center of gravity to the suspension point remains constant, regardless of the orientation of the object.</meta>

</margin>

...multiplied by the number of particles of the segment **B N N D**, equals the sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of that same segment from the plane **E C***. Therefore, the third part will also equal the first; namely, the plane **z**, multiplied by the number of particles of the solid **A B C D**, will equal the sum of the squares formed from the distances of the particles of that same solid **A B C D** from the plane **E C***. It should be noted, indeed, that when the solid **A B D** is a <term>solid of revolution|a 3D shape created by rotating a 2D area around an axis</term> around the axis **A C**, the rectangle **B C K** always becomes equal to a fourth part of the square of **B C**; because the subcentric line <note>original: "subcentrica cunei." And accordingly, then the rectangle **Δ P Φ** itself, multiplied according to the number of particles of the solid **A B D**, is equal to the sought sum of the squares from all the perpendiculars which fall from those same particles onto the plane **E C**.

### PROPOSITION XVI.

**A**_ny figure, whether it be a line, a surface, or a solid; if it be suspended and swung in different ways upon axes parallel to each other, and which are equally distant from the center of gravity of the figure, it is <term>isochronous|having the same period of time; swinging at the same speed</term> with itself._

<image-desc>A circular geometric diagram containing an irregular quadrilateral shape (A, B, C, D) inside it. The circle's center is labeled E. A point F is at the top of the circle, H at the bottom left, and G at the bottom right. A line connects F to E. A dotted or hatched area represents the mass of the figure within the circle. Lines extend from the center E to several points on the circumference (F, P, H, G) and to the vertices of the inner shape (A, B, C, D).</image-desc>

Let any magnitude be proposed, whose <term>center of gravity|the point at which the weight of an object is evenly balanced in all directions</term> is point **E**, and let it first be suspended from an axis which is understood to pass through **F** at right angles to the plane of this page. Thus, this same plane will also be the plane of oscillation. In which, if a circumference **F H G** is described with center **E** and radius **E F**, and any point on it is taken, such as **H**, the magnitude is understood to be suspended a second time from an axis fixed in this point, and to be swung, while the same plane of oscillation remains. I say that it will be isochronous to itself when swung about the axis at **F**.

<summary>Huygens concludes a complex derivation of the sum of squared distances for solids of revolution and introduces Proposition XVI, which states that any object swung from different points will have the same period as long as those points are equidistant from its center of gravity.</summary>
<keywords>center of oscillation, center of gravity, isochronous, solid of revolution, Christiaan Huygens, geometry of motion, moment of inertia</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the demonstration of Proposition XVI. In the preceding section, Huygens began to prove that an object suspended from different points will have the same period of oscillation as long as those points are at an equal distance from the object's center of gravity. Here, he uses a particle-based model to show that the mathematical "sum of squares" (related to what we now call the moment of inertia) remains consistent in these cases.</meta>

-># CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS<-

<margin>
ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>

Let us imagine the proposed magnitude to be divided into very small equal particles. Thus, because in both of those points of suspension the plane of oscillation remains the same with respect to the parts of the magnitude, it is manifest that if perpendicular lines are conceived to fall from all the particles into which the magnitude is divided onto the said plane of oscillation, those lines would meet the plane at the same points in either suspension. Let these points be those which appear in the space **A B C D**.

Since, therefore, **E** is the center of gravity of the proposed magnitude, and since the magnitude itself therefore maintains equilibrium in any position around an axis erected through point **E** perpendicular to the plane **A B C D**, it is easily perceived that if an equal gravity <note>Huygens is treating the particles as having equal weight or mass to simplify the geometric proof.</note> is assigned to all the aforementioned points marked in the space **A B C D**, their center of gravity will also be point **E**. But if, as can happen, several perpendiculars coincide at some points, those points must be understood as if they were doubled that many times, and their gravity must be taken as multiple. And thus, considering them in this way, it is again clear that the center of gravity is point **E**.

<margin>
* Proposition 12 of this work.
</margin>

Furthermore, it is clear that the sum of the squares of the straight lines drawn from all the said points to point **F** is the same as the sum of the squares of those straight lines which are drawn perpendicular from the individual particles of the proposed magnitude to the axis of oscillation passing through **F**; because the lines themselves, whose squares are understood, have the same length in both cases. Similarly, when the suspension is from the axis through **H**, it is clear that the sum of the squares of the straight lines drawn from all the points marked in the space **A B C D** to point **H** is the same as the sum of the squares of those lines which are drawn perpendicular from all particles of the proposed magnitude to the axis of oscillation passing through **H**. 

Therefore, in either case, if the sum of the squares of the straight lines drawn from all the aforesaid points to points **F** or **H** is divided by the lines **E F** or **E H** (multiplied according to the number of particles into which the proposed magnitude is understood to be divided), there will arise from this <term>application|In 17th-century geometry, 'application' often refers to the process of division or relating one geometric area/volume to a line</term> the length of a <term>simple pendulum|A theoretical pendulum consisting of a single point mass on a weightless string</term> which is <term>isochronous|Swinging in the same amount of time; having an identical period of oscillation</term> to the magnitude suspended from **F** or **H**. Moreover, the sum of the squares in either case is equal*; and the lines **E F** and **E H** are also equal to each other; and the number of particles is the same. Therefore, since both the quantities applied and those to which they are applied are equal in both cases, the lengths arising from the application will also be equal. That is, the lengths of the pendulums isochronous to the proposed magnitude suspended from **F** or from **H** will be equal. Wherefore, what was proposed is established.

<summary>This page concludes the proof of Proposition XVI, demonstrating that an object's oscillation period is identical for any suspension point equidistant from its center of gravity. Huygens achieves this by showing the geometric sum of the squares of distances remains constant, a precursor to the modern concept of the moment of inertia.</summary>

## PROPOSITION XVII.

Given a plane <note>In the geometric language of the time, a "plane" refers to an area. Huygens is performing a calculation where an area represents the sum of the squares of distances.</note> which, when multiplied by the number of particles into which the suspended figure is understood to be divided, is equal to the squares of all the distances from the axis of oscillation; if that area is applied <note>In 17th-century geometry, to "apply" an area to a line meant to divide that area by the length of the line to find a new length, effectively finding the other side of a rectangle.</note> to a straight line equal to the distance between the axis of oscillation and the center of gravity of the suspended magnitude, the result will be the length of a simple pendulum <term>isochronous</term> <note>isochronous: from the Greek "isos" (equal) and "chronos" (time); meaning it swings with the exact same period or timing.</note> to it.

Let there be a figure **A B C**, whose center of gravity is **E**, suspended from an axis which is erected through point **F** perpendicular to the plane being viewed. And by assuming the figure is divided into the smallest equal particles, from all of which perpendicular lines are understood to fall onto the said axis: let there be, by the things shown above, a found plane **H**, whose multiple by the number of said particles is equal to all the squares of the said perpendiculars. And with plane **H** applied to the straight line **F E**, let the length **F G** be created. I say that this is the length of a simple pendulum having isochronous oscillations to the magnitude **A B C**, when moved about the axis through **F**.

### PROPOSITION XVIII.

**I**F *a plane area—which, when multiplied by the number of particles of a suspended body, is equal to the squares of the distances from the axis of gravity <note>original: "axe gravitatis"; refers to the axis passing through the center of gravity parallel to the axis of suspension.</note> (parallel to the axis of oscillation)—is applied to a straight line equal to the distance between both mentioned axes, it will produce a straight line equal to the distance by which the center of oscillation lies below the center of gravity of that same body.*

For example, if, upon suspension from **I**, the said distance were **EY**, it will be equal to **EG**; and the whole **YL** will be equal to **GF**; and thus, in either suspension, the same simple pendulum is isochronous to the magnitude **ABCD**.

...suspension, the center of oscillation is **D**, while in the latter [suspension] the center of oscillation is **E**. I say that as **BA** is to **CA**, so **EA** is to **DA**.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram consisting of a vertical line with five points labeled from top to bottom: C, B, A, E, and D. Likewise, in the suspension from **C**, since the distance **AE** is produced, by applying the same said space to the distance **CA**, the rectangle **CAE** will also be equal to that same space. Therefore, the rectangles **BAD** and **CAE** are equal to each other; and consequently, the ratio of **BA** to **CA** is the same as **AE** to **DA**. Which was to be demonstrated.

From this it is clear that, given a simple pendulum <note>A "simple" pendulum is an idealized model where all the mass is concentrated at a single point (the bob) at the end of a weightless string.</note> which is isochronous <term>isochronous: swinging in equal time, regardless of how wide the swing is</term> to a suspended magnitude in one suspension, and given its center of gravity; then for every other suspension, whether longer or shorter, the length of the isochronous pendulum is given, provided that the plane of oscillation remains the same.

...suspension is from **D**, the center of oscillation will then be **B**. This is indeed manifest from the preceding proposition itself.

## PROPOSITION XXI.
### *How the centers of oscillation are to be found in plane figures.*

Having understood what has been demonstrated thus far, it will now be easy to define the centers of oscillation in most of the figures commonly used in Geometry. And to speak first of plane figures: we defined above two types of oscillatory motion in them—namely, either about an axis lying in the same plane as the figure, or about an axis which is erected perpendicular to the plane of the figure. The first of these we called <term>agitation in the plane</term><note>original: "agitationem in planum". This refers to the object swinging like a pendulum where the flat face of the shape stays in the same vertical plane as it moves.</note>, and the second <term>agitation to the side</term><note>original: "agitationem in latus". This refers to a shape swinging like a door on a hinge, where the flat face of the shape moves through the third dimension.</note>.

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram showing two circles. Each circle has a vertical line passing through its center and extending above, labeled E and F at the top intersection point (labeled E on both sides of F for the first diagram, and E only on the right for the second). A horizontal line passes through each circle, intersecting the vertical line inside. Points are labeled D along the horizontal line in both diagrams. Within the first circle, points A, K, H are marked on the vertical line. Within the second circle, points A, H, K are marked on the vertical line. Points B and C are marked on the circumference or near the horizontal line in both circles. The labels suggest geometrical relationships between these points and lines.</image-desc>

If it is moved in the first manner, namely about an axis lying in the same plane, such as the figure **BCD** about the axis **EF**; here, if a <term>wedge</term><note>original: "cuneus". Huygens uses a "wedge" as a conceptual tool to visualize how the mass of a flat shape is distributed relative to its axis of rotation.</note> is understood to be cut off over the figure by a plane that intersects the plane of the figure in such a way that the intersection (which is here **DD**) is parallel to the axis of oscillation; and the distance of the <term>center of gravity</term> of the figure from this intersection is given (here **AD**); and likewise the <term>subcentric weight</term><note>original: "subcentrica unciæ". This is a technical term used by Huygens to describe a value related to the moment of inertia—essentially a mathematical placeholder for the squared distances of all the shape's parts from the center of gravity.</note> of the said figure over the same intersection (which here is **DH**); then the center of oscillation **K** of the figure **BDC** will be obtained by "applying" the rectangle **DAH** to the distance **FA** <note>In 17th-century geometry, "applying a rectangle to a distance" is a way of describing division: (Area of Rectangle DAH) divided by (Length FA).</note>. For from this application will arise the distance **AK**, by which the center of oscillation is lower than the center of gravity. For the rectangle **DAH**, multiplied according to the number of particles of the figure **BCD**, is equal to the squares of the distances from the line **BAC** which passes through the center of gravity...

...of gravity **A** is drawn parallel to the axis of oscillation **E F***. Therefore, by applying the same rectangle to the distance **F A**, the distance **A K** is obtained, by which the center of oscillation is lower than the center of gravity **A***.

From this it is clear that if the axis of oscillation is **D D**, the center of oscillation becomes point **H**; and thus the length **D H** of a simple pendulum that is isochronous <note>original: "isochroni". Having the same period of swing, regardless of the width of the arc.</note> with the figure **B C D** is then the <term>subcentric length</term><note>original: "subcentricam". This refers to the distance from the axis to the center of gravity of a geometric "wedge" construction Huygens uses to calculate moments of inertia.</note> of the wedge cut off by a plane through **D D**, above the same **D D**. This alone was noticed by others before, yet not demonstrated.

However, it is not our purpose here to pursue how the centers of gravity of wedges on plane figures are found, as they are already well known in many cases. For example, if figure **B C D** is a circle, **D H** will be equal to 5/8 of the diameter. If it is a rectangle, **D H** will be equal to 2/3 of the diameter. From this also appears the reason why a rod, or a line endowed with weight and suspended from one end, becomes isochronous with a pendulum of a length two-thirds its own <note>original: "subsesquialteræ". A ratio where the shorter length is 2/3 of the longer.</note>. This is found by considering such a line as if it were a rectangle of the smallest width.

But if the figure were a triangle with the vertex turned upward, **D H** becomes 3/4 of the diameter <note>Huygens uses "diameter" here generally to mean the height or principal dimension of the geometric figure.</note>. If the vertex is turned downward, it is 1/2 of the diameter.

Furthermore, it should be known that what was demonstrated in Proposition 16 pertains to the motion of this kind of plane figure in this way: namely, if we give the figure **B C D** various positions by turning it about the axis **B A C**, so that it either lies parallel to the horizon or is tilted obliquely, as long as the axis of agitation **F E** remains the same, the length of the isochronous pendulum **F K** will also remain the same. This is clear from that proposition.

Furthermore, when a plane figure is agitated about an axis erected perpendicular to the plane of the figure—which we called **agitation to the side**—as if figure **B C D** were moved about an axis understood to pass through point **F** and be perpendicular to the plane **D B C**; here, besides the <term>wedge</term><note>original: "cuneum". A three-dimensional geometric construction used by Huygens to represent the distribution of mass relative to an axis.</note> upon the figure cut off by a plane through **D D** (which touches the figure at the top point), another wedge must also be considered, which is cut off by a plane through **B D**, touching the figure on the side, and which is at right angles to the tangent **D D**. It is necessary that, besides the center of gravity **A** of the figure and the subcentric length **H D** of the first wedge, the subcentric length **L B** of the second wedge also be given. For in this way the rectangles **D A H** and **B A L** will be known, which when taken together create the area to be applied here, which hereafter will also be called the **Rectangle of Oscillation**. When applied to the distance **F A**, this will give the distance **A K**, by which the center of oscillation **K** is lower than the center of gravity **A**.

And indeed the *demonstration of these things is evident* from what precedes, since the rectangles **D A H**, **B A L**, or **D A H**, **O A N**, being *multiples* according to the number of particles of the figure, are equal to the *squares of the distances from the center of gravity* **A**; or, what is the same thing here, from the *axis of gravity* parallel to the axis of oscillation; and accordingly, these said rectangles, applied to the distance **F A**, produce the *length of the interval* **A K**.

### The Center of Oscillation of a Circle.

And in a circle, it is clear that the rectangles **D A H** and **B A L** are equal to each other, and together they make up *half of the square* of the *radius* <note>original: "semidiametro". Half-diameter.</note>. Whence, if it is made so that **F A** is to the *radius* **A B** as the latter is to another length, half of that length will be the distance **A K**, from the center of gravity to the center of oscillation. If, therefore, a circle is swung from an axis **D** taken on its circumference, **D K** will be equal to three-quarters of the diameter **D M**.

In this manner, we have also sought the centers of oscillation in the *following plane figures*, which it will be sufficient to *simply record*. Namely,

### *The Center of Oscillation of a Rectangle.*

In every rectangle, such as **cb**, the <term>area to be applied</term> <note>original: "spatium applicandum". In Huygens' method, this refers to a calculated value (akin to the moment of inertia) that, when divided by the distance to the center of gravity, reveals the length of the equivalent simple pendulum.</note>, or the rectangle of oscillation, is found to be equal to one-third of the square of the <term>semi-diagonal</term> <note>Half of the diagonal line connecting opposite corners.</note> **ac**. From this it follows that if a rectangle is suspended from any of its corners and set into motion with this lateral swing, the pendulum <term>isochronous</term> <note>original: "isochronum". From the Greek for "equal time"; referring to a pendulum that swings with the exact same period as the object described.</note> to it is **2/3** of the length of the entire diagonal.

### *The Center of Oscillation of an Isosceles Triangle.*

In an isosceles triangle, such as **cbd**, the area to be applied is equal to one-eighteenth of the square of the <term>diameter</term> <note>original: "diametro". Huygens uses this term to refer to the altitude or central axis of the triangle, **be**.</note> **be**, and one-twenty-fourth of the square of the base **cd**. From this, if a line **dg** is drawn from a corner of the base perpendicular to the side **db**, meeting the extension of the altitude **be** at point **g**; and let **a** be the center of gravity of the triangle; and the interval **ga** being divided into four equal parts, if one of these parts **ak** is added to the segment **ba**; then **bk** will be the length of the isochronous pendulum if the triangle is suspended from the vertex **b**. However, when it is suspended from the midpoint of the base **e**, the length of the isochronous pendulum **ek** will be equal to half of **bg**.

And from this it is clear that a right isosceles triangle, if suspended from the midpoint of its base, is isochronous to a pendulum having a length equal to its own altitude. Similarly, if it is suspended from its right angle, it is isochronous to that same pendulum.

<image-desc>A series of geometric diagrams. The top diagram is a rectangle with vertices labeled B and C, showing a diagonal with point A at its center. Below this are three diagrams of the same isosceles triangle in different orientations and with different construction lines. The triangles are labeled with vertices B, C, and D, showing altitudes (b e), points for centers of gravity (a), centers of oscillation (k), and construction points (g) formed by extending the altitude to meet perpendiculars from the base vertices.</image-desc>

### *The Center of Oscillation of a Parabola.*

In a segment of a <term>right parabola</term> <note>A standard parabolic shape cut by a line perpendicular to its axis.</note>, the area to be applied is equal to **12/175** of the square of the axis, together with one-fifth of the square of the half-base. Since—

### The Center of Oscillation of a Circular Sector.

In a circular sector **BCD**, if the radius **BC** is called $r$; the half-arc **CF**, $p$; and the <term>half-chord</term> <note>original: "semisubtensa". Half the length of the straight line connecting the ends of the arc.</note> **CE**, $b$: the <term>area to be applied</term> <note>original: "spatium applicandum". A geometric value used in Huygens' calculations that, when divided by the distance to the center of gravity, gives the pendulum's length.</note> is equal to $\frac{1}{2} r^2 -$
<margin>$\frac{4 b^2 r^2}{9 p^2}$,</margin>
that is, half of the square of **BC**, minus the square of **BA**; assuming **A** to be the center of gravity of the sector. For in that case, **BA** is approximately $\frac{2br}{3p}$. If, however, the sector is suspended from **B**, the center of its own circle, the pendulum isochronous to it becomes $\frac{3pr}{4b}$, which is three-quarters of a line that is to the radius **BF** as the arc **CFD** is to the chord **CD**. These values are found by knowing the <term>subcentrics</term> <note>original: "subcentricis". A term used by Huygens to describe specific geometric centers of the "wedges" or solid slices he uses to model the distribution of mass in a swinging shape.</note> of the wedges; first, of that wedge which is cut off above the whole sector by a plane drawn through **BK** parallel to the chord **CD**, whose wedge's subcentric above **BK** we have found to be $\frac{3}{8} r - \frac{3}{8} a + \frac{3pr}{8b}$, calling $a$ the <term>versed sine</term> <note>original: "sinum versum". The distance from the midpoint of the chord to the midpoint of the arc (**EF**).</note> **EF**; and second, of that wedge which is cut off above the half-sector **BFC** by a plane through **BF**, whose wedge's subcentric above **BF** we find to be $\frac{5}{8} b - \frac{3br}{8a} + \frac{3pr}{8a}$.

But there is also another way to find the center of oscillation of the sector more easily, which is as follows. Imagine a very small part of the sector **BCD**, the sector **BCP**, which can be treated as a triangle. Now, the squares of the distances of its particles from point **B** are equal to the squares of the distances from the line **BR**, which bisects the sector, together with the squares of the distances from the line **BQ**, which is at right angles to **BR**. But the ratio of these squares to those is greater than any given ratio, since the angle **CBP** is extremely small; and therefore, those are to be considered as nothing.

From which, having performed the division, the result is $\frac{3pr}{4b}$, the length of the isochronous pendulum, just as was found previously.

### The center of oscillation of a Circle, by a different method than above.

In the same way, and very simply, one may find the center of oscillation in a circle. For let there be a circle **GCF**, whose center is **B**; and let a very small sector **BCP** be understood within it, just as before in sector **BCD**.

Since, therefore, according to what has just been set forth, the squares of the distances of the particles of the sector **BCP** from the center **B** are equal to the rectangle **NBO**—that is, to half the square of the radius, multiplied by the number of particles of that same sector—and since a circle is composed of such sectors; it follows that the squares of the distances of the particles of the entire circle from the center **B** are equal to half the square of the radius, multiplied by the number of those same particles of the circle.

### *Center of Oscillation of Regular Polygons.*

In a very similar way, the isochronous pendulum is found for any regular polygon, such as **ABC**. For the area to be applied is equal to half the square of the perpendicular line drawn from the center to a side of the polygon, together

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of a pentagon with a vertical line through its center. The top vertex is labeled A. The center is labeled E. The base line has endpoints B and C, with a midpoint F where the vertical line from A meets it. Lines connect E to B and E to C.</image-desc>

with one twenty-fourth part of the square of that side. But, if the isochronous pendulum is sought for the *perimeter* of the polygon, the area to be applied becomes equal to the square of the perpendicular from the center to the side, plus one twelfth part of the square of the side.

### *The Use of Plane and Solid Loci in this Theory.*

Furthermore, the study of Loci <note>Locus (plural Loci): in geometry, a collection of points which share a common property or satisfy a specific equation.</note> in these matters is quite rewarding. Suppose that, given a point of suspension **A** and a length **AB**, we wish to find the locus of two equal weights **C** and **D**, which are at equal distances from **A** and from the perpendicular line **AB**. When these weights are swung around an axis at **A** (perpendicular to the plane through **ACD**), they are to be isochronous to a simple pendulum of length **AB**.

Let **AB** = **a**. Having drawn the line **CD** so that it cuts **AB** at right angles at **E**, let the indeterminate <note>original: "indeterminata"; here Huygens uses it to mean a variable coordinate.</note> distance **AE** = **x**, and **EC** or **ED** = **y**. Therefore, the square of **AC** = **x² + y²**. This value, multiplied by the number of particles of weights **C** and **D** (which are understood here as infinitesimals), is equal to the squares of the distances of those same particles from the axis...

of suspension **A**. Therefore, the square of **AC**, or *xx + yy*, applied to the distance **AE**—which specifically lies between the axis of suspension and the center of gravity of the weights **C** and **D**—will result in `(xx + yy) / x`, the length of the isochronous <term>isochronous: from the Greek for "equal time," describing a pendulum that completes its swings in the same amount of time regardless of their width.</term> pendulum; which therefore must be equal to **AB** or *a*.</p>

<image-desc>A circular diagram with points A at the top and B at the bottom. Points C and D are on the left and right sides of the circumference. A horizontal line connects C and D, passing through E.</image-desc>

Thus `(xx + yy) / x` = *a*. And *yy = ax - xx*. From which it is clear that the locus <note>original: "locum"; in geometry, a locus is the set of all points whose coordinates satisfy a given equation or condition.</note> of points **C** and **D** is the circumference of a circle, whose center is **F** (where **AB** is bisected) and whose radius is equal to `1/2` *a*, or **FA**. Therefore, wherever on the circumference **ACBD** two equal weights are placed at equal distances from **A**, these, when swung from **A**, will be isochronous to a pendulum having a length equal to the diameter **AB**.

And from this it is also manifest that both the circumference **ACBD**, if weight is attributed to it, and any portion of it that is equally divided at **A** or **B** and suspended from an axis through **A**, is isochronous to the same pendulum **AB**.

Let this be an example of a "solid locus" <note>A "solid locus" in 17th-century geometry usually referred to a locus that forms a conic section (like a circle, ellipse, or parabola).</note>. Let **AN** be an inflexible line without weight. And let it be proposed to attach to a point chosen on it, such as **M**, a line or rod endowed with weight, **OML**, at right angles; this rod is bisected at **M**, and its oscillations when swung in this plane from the suspension **A** are isochronous to a simple pendulum of length **AN**.

Let **OH** be drawn parallel to **AN**, and **AH** parallel to **OM**, and let **OR** be equal to **OL**. Thus, the subcentric <term>subcentrica: a technical term used by Huygens to describe a geometric value or line segment used in calculating the center of oscillation relative to the center of mass.</term> of the wedge <note>original: "cunei"; Huygens uses the "method of wedges" to calculate moments of inertia and centers of oscillation by imagining the mass distributed as a geometric solid.</note> on the line **OL**, cut off by a plane drawn through **OH**, will be **OR**. But for the other wedge on the same **OL**, cut off by a plane through line **AH** (this wedge, however, is nothing other than a rectangle), its subcentric will be **AM** itself. Wherefore that rectangle, which we called the "Rectangle of Oscillation" above, will be only the rectangle **OMR**; which, when applied to the length **AM**, will give the distance of the center of oscillation of line **OL** (suspended from **A**) below the point **M**.

<summary>Huygens proves that any pair of weights or any circular arc suspended from a point on its circumference will swing with the same period as a simple pendulum equal to the circle's diameter. He then begins a new derivation for the center of oscillation of a weighted rod attached perpendicularly to a swinging arm.</summary>

On the previous page, he demonstrated that weights placed on the circumference of a circle would swing in time with a pendulum equal to the circle's diameter. Here, he extends this logic to show how an elliptical shape also possesses specific isochronous properties.</meta>

ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.

Let **AN** now be equal to *a*; **AM** equal to *x*; and **MO** or **ML** equal to *y*. Therefore, the rectangle <note>original: "rectangulum"; in this context, Huygens refers to a geometric product or area value used in his derivation.</note> **OMR** is equal to $^1/_3 y^2$. When this is applied to **AM**, it becomes $\frac{y^2}{3x}$. This length must therefore be equal to **MN**, since we want the center of oscillation of the rod **OL** to be at point **N**. This results in the equation $\frac{y^2}{3x} + x = a$. From this, it follows that $y = \sqrt{3ax - 3x^2}$. This signifies that the points **O** and **L** lie on an **Ellipse**, whose minor axis is **AN**; while its parameter <note>original: "latus rectum"; the chord through a focus of a conic section, perpendicular to the major axis, which defines the "breadth" of the curve.</note>—according to which the lines applied to this axis are measured—is triple the length of **AN**.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing an ellipse with its longer axis horizontal and shorter axis vertical. The vertical axis is labeled A at the top, M at the center, and N at the bottom. A horizontal line segment passes through M, intersecting the ellipse at points O on the left and L on the right. A point R is marked on the segment ML. A vertical dashed line extends upwards from point O to a point H.</image-desc>

From this, it clearly becomes manifest that since every rod parallel to **OL** and terminating at this ellipse has swings of equal time <term>isochronous: from the Greek for "equal time," describing a motion that takes the same amount of time regardless of how wide the swing is.</term> to a simple pendulum **AN**, the entire area of the ellipse—if suspended from **A** and swung sideways—will also be isochronous to the pendulum **AN**. This also holds true for any part of the ellipse that is cut off by one or two lines perpendicular to **AN**.

Furthermore, we shall add another example of a plane locus <note>original: "loci plani"; the set of all points satisfying a specific geometric condition.</note> in which several noteworthy things occur.

Let there be a rod **AB**, which we consider to be without weight, suspended from point **A**; and let it be necessary, for a given...

...then at point **B** on that [axis], attach two pairs of triangles, which recede from the axis **AB** at equal angles. These angles at **B** are to be considered minimal, or infinitely small <note>original: "infinite parvi"; Huygens is using the concept of infinitesimals—shapes so thin they are essentially lines—to build up more complex areas like circles.</note>. These triangles, when suspended from point **A**, perform isochronous <term>isochronous: swinging in the same amount of time regardless of the width of the swing</term> oscillations with a simple pendulum of a given length **AL**.

In this case, by drawing line **CG** perpendicular to **BG**, and setting **AB** = *a*; **AL** = *b*; **BG** = *x*; and **CG** = *y*: we find the following equation: 
*y* = $\sqrt{2ab - 2a^2 - \frac{8}{3}ax + \frac{4}{3}bx - x^2}$ <note>original: the symbol "$\infty$" is used here by Huygens to represent equality (=).</note>.
From this equation, it is evident that the bases of the triangles **C** and **D** (which are treated here as mere points) lie on the circumference of a circle, specifically because we find the simple term $-x^2$ in the equation.

However, we may observe here that if *a* is equal to zero—that is, if the point **B** where the triangles **BC** and **BD** are attached is the same as the point of suspension **A**—then the equation will be *y* = $\sqrt{\frac{4}{3}bx - x^2}$. Accordingly, in this case, if we take **AO** = $\frac{2}{3}b$ (which is $\frac{2}{3}$ of the pendulum length **AL**), and a circle **ADN** is described with **O** as its center passing through **A**, then the bases of the triangles **AC** and

<image-desc>A circular diagram with a vertical diameter labeled with points A (at the top), O (the center), G, L, and N (at the bottom). A horizontal chord CD passes through point G on the diameter. Two straight lines connect point A to points C and D, forming a triangle ACD inscribed within the circle.</image-desc>

**AD** will lie upon that circumference. Therefore, since any two extremely thin triangles formed from **A** to the circumference **ACND**, which correspond to each other in size and position, have their center of oscillation at point **L** (given that **AL** is assumed to be $\frac{3}{4}$ of the diameter **AN**); and since the entire circle is composed of such pairs of triangles, as is any portion of it such as **ACND** which has equal sides **AC** and **AD**; it is manifest that the center of oscillation for both the whole circle and the specified portion is located at **L**.

Furthermore, if in the discovered equation we set $\frac{8}{3}a = \frac{4}{3}b$, or $2a = b$ (meaning the triangles are attached at point **B**, which bisects the length **AL**), then *y* = $\sqrt{2a^2 - x^2}$. # L ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.

## THE OSCILLATING CLOCK.

...for these indeed, when suspended from **A**, the center of oscillation will be **L**. And since both the whole circle and any sector of it, having its axis on the straight line **AL**, is composed of pairs of such triangles, it is manifest that for these also, when suspended from **A**, the center of oscillation is the point **L**.

<image-desc>A circular diagram with various lines and points. Point A is at the top, B, C, D, G, L are inside the circle, and N is at the bottom, extending from the bottom of the circle. And so, in this one case, without needing to establish the dimension of the arc, a pendulum isochronous to the sector is found.

Furthermore, for the universal construction <term>construction: the geometric method of drawing a figure that represents a mathematical equation</term> of the first equation, *y* = $\sqrt{2ab - 2a^2 - \frac{8}{3}ax + \frac{4}{3}bx - x^2}$ <note>original: "y ∞ V...". Huygens uses the symbol "∞" for equality (=) and "V" as a radical sign for the square root.</note>, let **AL** be divided in half at **E**, and let a third part of **BE**, which we call **EF**, be added to **BE**; then **F** will be the center for drawing the circle. The radius **FO**, however, must be taken as equal to the line which can produce <note>original: "quae potest"; this refers to a line whose square is equal to the following value.</note> double the difference of the squares of **AE** and **EF**.

<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' investigation into the center of oscillation. Having previously discussed circular sectors, he now extends his findings to circular segments and introduces a method for finding the center of oscillation in non-symmetrical shapes, like scalene triangles, by comparing them to symmetrical ones.</meta>

# CHRISTIAN HUYGENS

<margin>ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.</margin>

...of those objects suspended, the center of oscillation will be **L**. Therefore, for any portion of a described circle whose vertex is at **B** and whose axis lies on the straight line **AL**—such as both portions **CBD**—it is established that when suspended from **A**, the center of oscillation is the same point **L**. This also applies to the circular segments **KON** and **KMN**, which are formed by the straight line **KBN** perpendicular to **AB**.

And let it suffice to have observed these things regarding the lateral motion <note>Huygens is referring to the swinging motion of flat shapes (planes) and rods (lines) moving side-to-side.</note> of planes and lines. To these we add only this: once the centers of oscillation for rectilinear figures—that is, those arranged equally on both sides of an axis, such as an isosceles triangle <term>isosceles triangle: a triangle with at least two equal sides</term> or a right parabolic section—have been found, the centers of oscillation for "oblique" figures can also be obtained. These oblique figures are formed as if by a displacement <term>displacement: Huygens uses the word "luxation," suggesting a shifting or stretching of the shape while keeping its base and height the same</term> of the regular ones; examples include a scalene triangle <term>scalene triangle: a triangle where all three sides have different lengths</term> or a non-right parabola. For example, if an isosceles triangle **BAC**, with its axis **AD**, is understood to be suspended from point **E**, and there is another scalene triangle **FAG** having the same axis **AD** and a base **FG** equal to the base **BC**; I say that this triangle, when suspended from **E**, is isochronous <term>isochronous: swinging in exactly the same amount of time</term> with the former triangle **BAC**.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram representing two triangles sharing a common vertical axis and suspension point. A vertical line passes through points E (at the top), A (the shared vertex of the triangles), and D (the center of the bases). Triangle BAC is an isosceles triangle with a horizontal base BC. Triangle FAG is a scalene triangle with a slanted base FG that intersects the axis at the same point D. Both triangles are divided by several internal lines parallel to their respective bases, illustrating their composition from individual rods or lines.</image-desc>

<margin>* Proposition 16 of this work.</margin>
This is because a heavy rod—or "heavy line" <note>original: "linea gravis"; in Huygens' geometry, a "line" can have weight for the purpose of the calculation</note>—**FG**, attached to a weightless rod **ED** at point **D** in an oblique position and suspended from **E**, is isochronous to the rod **BC** attached similarly at **D** \*; the same occurs for all the other rods of both triangles that intersect the axis **AD** at the same points and are equal to one another. It follows that the entire triangles, which can be understood as being composed of these same lines or rods, must be isochronous. In other figures, the demonstration is similar.

<summary>Huygens demonstrates that circular segments share the same center of oscillation as their parent sectors and proves that a scalene triangle swings at the same rate as an isosceles triangle of equal base and height.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the study of two-dimensional planes to three-dimensional solids. THE PENDULUM CLOCK. ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.

## PROPOSITION XXII.

How the centers of oscillation may be found in solid figures.

Furthermore, in solid figures, it will also be easy to find the center of oscillation through what was previously demonstrated. For if there is a solid ***A B C***, suspended from an axis which is understood to be at right angles to the plane of this page through point ***E***; and let the center of gravity be ***F***: with planes ***E F D*** and ***G F H*** now drawn through ***F***, the latter of which is parallel to the horizon, while the other passes through the axis ***E***; and having found, by proposition 14, the sums of the squares of the distances of the particles of the solid ***A B C*** from the plane ***G F H***, and likewise from the plane ***E F D***; that is, having found both rectangles which, being multiples according to the number of said particles, are equal to the said sums of squares; these rectangles, applied to <note>In 17th-century geometry, "applying" an area to a line refers to division (Area / Length = Length).</note> the distance ***E F***—by which, namely, the axis of suspension is distant from the center of gravity—will give the interval ***F K***, by which the center of agitation <note>original: "agitationis." Huygens uses "agitation" and "oscillation" interchangeably to describe the swinging motion.</note> ***K*** is lower than the center of gravity ***F***. This indeed is clear from proposition 18. We shall also give some examples of these.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a triangle labeled A, B, and C representing the cross-section of a pyramid. A vertical axis E-D passes through the vertex A. Points F and K are marked on the vertical line below A. A horizontal line G-H intersects the vertical axis at point F. The labels G and H are placed at the intersections of this line with the sides of the triangle AB and AC.</image-desc>

### *The center of oscillation in a Pyramid.*

Let there first be a pyramid ***A B C***, having vertex ***A***, axis ***A D***, and a square base whose side is ***B C***; and let it be set to oscillate about an axis which, passing through the vertex ***A***, is at right angles to the plane of this page.

Here, the proportional plane figure ***o v v***, to be placed alongside according to proposition 14, will consist of the parabolic remainders ***o P V***, which indeed remain when the semi-parabolas ***o V Ω***, having vertex ***o***, are subtracted from the rectangles ***Ω P***.

For just as the sections of the pyramid ***B C***, ***N N*** are proportional to each other, so too are the straight lines ***V V***, ***R R***, which correspond to them in the plane figure. And just as the center of gravity ***E*** is distant from the vertex of the pyramid by three-fourths of the axis ***A D***, so also the center of gravity ***F*** of the figure ***O V V*** will be distant from the vertex ***O*** by three-fourths of the diameter ***O P***.

A geometric diagram used for calculating the moments of a pyramid. On the left, a rectangular construction labeled with vertices Ω and O contains a parabolic or triangular figure OVV. Furthermore, let a horizontal plane ***N E*** be understood to pass through the center of gravity of the pyramid ***A B C***, which also cuts the figure ***O V V*** along ***R F***; and having found the subcentric <note>The "subcentric" (original: *subcentrica*) is a technical geometric distance used by Huygens, representing a point related to the second moment of area or "moment of inertia" in modern terms.</note> of the wedge <note>original: *cunei*. In geometry, a cuneus is a solid wedge shape.</note> cut above the figure ***O V V*** by a plane through ***O Ω***, which subcentric is ***O G*** (and is $\frac{4}{5}$ of the diameter ***O P***), then the rectangle ***O F G***—when multiplied by the number of particles of the figure ***O V V***—will be equal to the squares of the distances from the line ***R F*** <margin>* Prop. 10 of this book.</margin>, and consequently also to the squares of the distances from the plane ***N E*** of the particles of the solid ***A B C***. Moreover, the rectangle ***O F G*** becomes equal to $\frac{3}{80}$ of the square of ***O P***, or of the square of ***A D***.

Next, to find the sum of the squares of the distances from the plane ***A D***, one must first know the subcentric of the wedge cut above the square base of the pyramid ***B C*** by a plane passing through a line understood to be at ***B*** parallel to the axis ***A***; this subcentric is ***B K***, and it is $\frac{2}{3}$ of ***B C***. Likewise, one must know the distance of the center of gravity of the half-figure ***O P V*** from ***O P***, which is ***φ P***; and it is $\frac{3}{10}$ of ***P V***. Then, with ***P V*** divided in half at ***Δ***, if it is made so that ***Δ P*** is to ***P φ*** (that is, as 5 to 3) as the rectangle ***B D K*** (which is $\frac{1}{12}$ of the square of ***B C***) is to another space ***Z***, then this space ***Z***—multiplied according to the number of particles of the solid ***A B C***—will be equal to the squares of the distances from the plane ***A D*** <margin>* Prop. 15 of this book.</margin>. It appears, then, that space ***Z*** becomes equal to $\frac{1}{20}$ of the square of ***B C***.

Therefore, the entire space to be applied <note>In 17th-century geometry, "applying" an area to a line refers to division (Area / Length = Length). Here, Huygens is dividing the sum of the squares by the distance to the center of gravity to find the center of oscillation.</note> is equal here to $\frac{3}{80}$ of the square of ***A D***, plus $\frac{1}{20}$ of the square of ***B C***. From this, if the suspension is placed at ***A***, the vertex of the pyramid as shown here, and thus the distance to which the application must be made...

### The Center of Oscillation of a Cone.

<margin>* Prop. 15 of this [work].</margin>
But if ***A B C*** were a cone, everything will proceed in the same manner, except that the space ***Z*** here becomes equal to the rectangle $\Delta$ ***P*** $\Phi$ *, that is $\frac{3}{20}$ of the square of ***P V*** or ***B D***, or $\frac{3}{80}$ of the square of ***B C***. Therefore, the whole space to be applied in a cone will be $\frac{3}{80}$ of the square of ***A D***, together with $\frac{3}{80}$ of the square of ***B C***. And consequently, assuming the suspension is from the vertex ***A***, ***E S*** (by which the center of oscillation is lower than the center of gravity) becomes equal to $\frac{1}{20}$ ***A D*** and $\frac{1}{20}$ of the third proportional to the two lines ***A D*** and ***B C***. Or the total length ***A S*** is equal to $\frac{4}{5}$ ***A D***, together with $\frac{1}{5}$ of the third proportional to the two lines ***A D*** and ***D B***. From this it is manifest that if ***A D*** and ***D B*** are equal—that is, if the cone ***A B C*** is a right-angled cone <note>A "right-angled cone" refers to a cone where the semi-vertical angle is 45 degrees, making the height equal to the radius of the base.</note>—the distance ***A S*** becomes equal to the axis ***A D***.

It follows further, from Proposition 20, that this right-angled cone, if suspended from the center of its base ***D***, will be isochronous <note>original: *isochronum*. Having the same period of oscillation; the two pendulums will swing in perfect time with one another.</note> with itself when suspended from the vertex ***A***, just as was shown above concerning the right-angled triangle.

### The Center of Oscillation of a Sphere.

If ***A B C*** is a sphere, the proportional plane figure to be placed at the side will be ***O V H***, composed of parabolas whose common base ***O H*** is equal to the diameter of the sphere ***A D***. Having cut the sphere with planes through the center ***E***, of which ***B C*** is parallel to the horizon and ***A D*** is vertical: to find the sum of the squares of the distances from the plane ***A D***, one must know the distance of the center of gravity of the parabola ***O V H*** from ***O H***, which is $\Phi$ ***P***, and is $\frac{2}{5}$ of ***V P***. Then, having divided ***P V*** into two equal parts at $\Delta$, it is established that the rectangle $\Delta$ ***P*** $\Phi$, multiplied by the number of particles of the sphere ***A B C***, is equal to the squares of the distances from the plane ***A D*** *. Moreover, the rectangu-<margin>* Prop. 15 at the end.</margin>lar space $\Delta$ ***P*** $\Phi$ is equal to $\frac{1}{5}$ of the square of ***P V***, or the square of ***B E***.

Yet it is clear that the squares of the distances from plane ***B C*** are equal to the squares of the distances from plane ***A D***, and therefore to the same rectangle $\Delta$ ***P*** $\Phi$ multiplied by the said number of particles. Therefore the space to be applied in the sphere ***A B C*** will be double the rectangle $\Delta$ ***P*** $\Phi$; and thus equal to $\frac{2}{5}$ of the square of the radius ***E B***.

Therefore, if the sphere is suspended from a point ***A*** on its surface, it will be
