Huygens uses this interchangeably with "center of oscillation."</note> ***S***, is equal to 2/5 of the semidiameter ***A E***. And the total length ***A S*** is equal to 7/10 of the diameter ***A D***. If, however, the sphere is suspended from another point, such as ***L***, then ***E S*** will be equal to 2/5 of the third proportional to the two lines ***L E*** and ***E B*** <note>The "third proportional" to lines *a* and *b* is a line *x* such that *a:b = b:x*, meaning $x = b^2/a$. Here, the formula relates to the distribution of mass.</note>.

<image-desc>Two geometric diagrams. The left diagram shows a circle with an internal rhombus (diamond shape) labeled O, H, V, V. A vertical axis OH intersects a horizontal axis VV at point P. A small cross symbol marks a point on the lower vertical axis. The right diagram depicts a circle representing a sphere with a vertical axis L-A-E-D and a horizontal chord B-C passing through the center E. Point L is the point of suspension above the circle, A is the top of the sphere, E is the center, and D is the bottom.</image-desc>

### The Center of Oscillation of a Cylinder.

In a cylinder, we find that the space to be applied <term>spatium applicandum: a geometric quantity Huygens uses to calculate the moment of inertia</term> is equal to 1/12 of the square of the height, together with 1/4 of the square of the semidiameter of the base. Whence, if the cylinder is suspended from the center of its upper base, the length of the isochronous pendulum <note>A simple pendulum that swings in the same amount of time as the complex body.</note> becomes equal to 2/3 of the height, together with half of that line which is to the semidiameter of the base as the semidiameter is to the height.

### The Center of Oscillation of a Parabolic Conoid.

In a parabolic conoid <note>A solid formed by rotating a parabola around its axis.</note>, the "rectangle of oscillation" is 1/18 of the square of the height, with 1/6 of the square of the semidiameter of the base. Whence, if it is suspended from the point of the vertex, the length of the isochronous pendulum becomes 3/4 of the axis, together with 1/4 of that line which is to the semidiameter of the base as the semidiameter is to the axis; that is, together with 1/4 of the latus rectum <term>latus rectum: the "upright side" or parameter that defines the width of a conic section</term> of the generating parabola.

### The Center of Oscillation of a Hyperbolic Conoid.

The center of oscillation can also be found in a hyperbolic conoid. If, for example, there is a conoid whose section through the axis is the hyperbola ***B A B***, having axis ***A D*** and a transverse side <term>latus transversum: the distance between the two vertices of a hyperbola</term> ***A F***: there will be a plane figure proportional to it, ***B K A K B***, contained by the base ***B B***...

<summary>Huygens provides the specific geometric formulas for finding the center of oscillation (the point at which all mass could be concentrated without changing the swing time) for spheres, cylinders, and conoids.</summary>
<keywords>center of oscillation, sphere, cylinder, parabolic conoid, hyperbolic conoid, isochronous pendulum, latus rectum, Christian Huygens</keywords>

...and similar segments of a parabolic line ***AKB***, which pass through the vertex ***A*** of the parabola and have an axis ***GE*** that bisects the transverse side <term>transverse side: a term from the geometry of conic sections relating to the orientation and width of the curve</term> ***AF*** and is parallel to the base ***BB***. Regarding the center of gravity ***L*** of this figure ***BKAKB***, its distance from the vertex ***A*** is the same as the center of gravity of the conoid ***ABB***; and the ratio of the axis ***AD*** to the distance ***AL*** is as three times ***FA*** plus twice ***AD*** is to twice ***AF*** plus one-and-a-half times <note>original: *sesquialtera*, meaning a ratio of 3:2 or 1.5.</note> ***AD***. Furthermore, the distance of the center of gravity of the half-figure ***ADBK*** from the axis ***AD*** can be found, as can the subcentric <term>subcentric: Huygens uses this term to denote the distance from the axis of rotation to the center of gravity of a specific section or "wedge" of a solid</term> of the wedge cut above the figure ***BKAKB*** by a plane through ***AP*** parallel to ***BB***. I say that the subcentric of this wedge above ***AP*** can also be found; and from these, consequently, the center of agitation <note>original: *centrum agitationis*, used by Huygens synonymously with the center of oscillation.</note> of the conoid in any suspension can be determined, provided that the axis about which it moves is parallel to the base of the conoid. And I find that if the axis ***AD*** is set equal to the transverse side ***AF***, the space to be applied <term>space to be applied: a geometric quantity used to determine the moment of inertia, often expressed as a fraction of the object's dimensions</term> is equal to 1/20 of the square of ***AD*** plus 31/200 of the square of ***DB***. In that case, ***AL*** is 7/10 of ***AD***.

A vertical axis extends downwards from F through E, A, L, and ends at a point S.</image-desc>

From this, if a conoid of this kind is suspended from the vertex ***A***, the length of the isochronous pendulum <note>original: *pendulum isochronum*, a pendulum that maintains the same period regardless of its swing's amplitude.</note> ***AS*** is found to be equal to 27/35 of ***AD***, together with 31/140 of the third proportional <note>A third proportional to lines *a* and *b* is a line *x* such that *a:b = b:x*, or $x = b^2/a$.</note> to the two lines ***AD*** and ***DB***.

### THE PENDULUM CLOCK <note>original: HOROLOG. OSCILLATOR.</note>

<margin>
ON THE
CENTER OF
OSCILLATION.
</margin>

...from the plane through its axis, is equal to 3/80 of the square of the diameter of the base, or 3/20 of the square of the radius, multiplied by the number of the particles of the whole cone. From which, here also in the semi-cone ***ABC***, the squares of the distances from the plane ***AB*** will be equal to 3/20 of the square of ***BC***, multiplied by the number of the particles of the semi-cone itself. But the rectangle ***HGF***, multiplied by the number of particles of the semi-cone ***ABC***, is also equal to the squares of the distances from the plane ***AB***, as is clear from Proposition 9. Therefore, the rectangle ***HGF*** is equal to 3/20 of the square of ***BC***. Now, by setting ***AB*** as ***a***; ***BC*** as ***b***; and the quadrant of the circumference described by radius ***BC*** as ***q***; it follows that ***EB*** is equal to $2b^2/3q$. Since ***ND*** is equal to three-fourths of this, it follows that ***ND*** (or ***GF***) is equal to $1b^2/2q$. By subtracting the square of this from the rectangle ***HGF*** (which was 3/20 of the square of ***BC***), the rectangle ***GFH*** becomes $3/20 b^2 - 1b^4/4q^2$. This rectangle, multiplied by the number of particles of the semi-cone ***ABC***, is equal to the squares of the distances from the plane ***MDO***. But the squares of the distances from the plane ***MD*** are equal (as in a full cone) to 3/80 of the square of ***AB***, or $3/80 a^2$, multiplied by the number of particles of the semi-cone ***ABC***. Thus, the whole space to be applied will here be equal to $3/80 a^2 + 3/20 b^2 - 1b^4/4q^2$.

From this, the center of agitation <term>center of agitation: an older term for the center of oscillation; the point in a compound pendulum where its mass could be concentrated to keep the same period</term> is found for any suspension of a semi-cone, provided it is from an axis parallel to the base of the triangle from section ***AB***. It should be noted that, although the figure ***s z y*** is of a completely unknown nature, the subcentric <term>subcentric: a term Huygens uses for a specific line or segment in his geometric method of calculating the center of oscillation</term> ***GH*** of the wedge cut off above it by the plane through ***s y*** is found from this. For since the rectangle ***HGF*** was equal to $3/20 b^2$ (the square of ***BC***), and ***GF*** is equal to $1b^2/2q$, it follows that ***GH*** is equal to $3/10 q$.

Furthermore, the centers of agitation of the semi-cylinder and the parabolic semi-conoid can also be found, as well as those of other semi-solids; which we leave for others to investigate.

Just as in plane figures, so too here in solid figures, what we said there regarding the centers of agitation of oblique figures holds true—those which are formed as if by a "dislocation" <note>original: *luxatione*; here Huygens refers to a geometric transformation where a figure is shifted or skewed without changing its vertical distribution of mass</note> of straight figures, whose centers of oscillation do not differ from the centers of oscillation of the straight figures. Thus, if there were two cones ***ABC*** and ***AFG***, one right and the other scalene <note>A "right" cone has its vertex directly over the center of its base; a "scalene" cone is tilted to one side</note>, with equal diameters and bases, if these are suspended from the vertex or from any axes at equal distances from their centers of gravity, they will be isochronous <term>isochronous: keeping equal time; having the same period of oscillation</term>; provided that the axis from which the scalene cone is suspended is perpendicular to the plane of the triangle through the diameter, which plane is at right angles to the base.

### PROPOSITION XXIII.

**T***o regulate the motion of clocks by adding a small secondary weight which can be moved up and down along the pendulum rod, divided according to a certain ratio.*

To explain this, we must first find the center of oscillation of the pendulum itself, composed of a rod possessing weight and a weight attached at the bottom.

<margin>
<image-desc>A vertical diagram representing a pendulum rod. At the top, a horizontal line is marked D A D. Along the vertical axis descending from point A are markings for points M and T. At the bottom of the axis is a circled letter C.</image-desc>
</margin>
<margin>
* Proposition 6 of this [work], at the end.
</margin>

Let the rod, with the attached weight, be $AC$, the length of which is called $a$. Now, let both the rod itself and the attached weight $C$ be understood as divided into the smallest equal particles; let the rod have $b$ number of these particles, and the weight $C$ have $c$ number, by setting $b$ to $c$ in the same ratio as the weight <note>original: gravitas; here meaning mass or heaviness</note> of the rod to the weight of the attached mass. Therefore, the length of a simple pendulum, given as isochronous <note>original: isochroni; meaning having the same period of swing as the composite pendulum</note>, will be found if the sum of the squares of the distances of all the particles from the point of suspension $A$ is divided by the sum of those same distances. Let $AC$ be cut in half at $M$; then at $T$, so that $AT$ is twice $TC$. Because $M$ is the center of gravity of the line $AC$, and $AT$ is the sub-central <note>A technical term used by Huygens to describe the distance to the center of gravity of the geometric "wedge" representing the squares of distances</note> of the wedge <term>cuneus|a wedge-shaped geometric figure used to calculate the distribution of weight along the pendulum</term> cut off above it by the plane through $AD$ perpendicular to $AC$ (which wedge is here actually a triangle), the sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of the rod from point $A$ will be...

<header>THE PENDULUM CLOCK</header>

<margin>ON THE CENTER OF OSCILLATION.</margin>
is equal to the rectangle $AMT$, together with the square of $AM$; <note>Huygens is using a geometric method to sum the moments of inertia. In modern terms, he is integrating the mass distribution along the rod.</note> that is, the rectangle $TAM$ multiplied by the number of particles $b$; which is $\frac{1}{3} a^2 b$; because $MA$ is $\frac{1}{2} a$, and $TA$ is $\frac{2}{3} a$, and accordingly the rectangle $TAM$ is equal to <note>The symbol $\infty$ was commonly used by 17th-century mathematicians like Huygens and Wallis to denote equality (=).</note> $\frac{1}{3} a^2$. Moreover, the sum of the squares of the distances of the particles of weight $C$ from the same point $A$ will equal the square of $AC$ multiplied by the number of particles of that weight; that is, $a^2 c$. Thus, the sum of all the squares, both from the distances of the particles of the rod and from the weight $C$, will be $\frac{1}{3} a^2 b + a^2 c$.

Furthermore, all the distances <note>This refers to the "first moment" or the sum of the distances of the particles, used to find the center of gravity.</note> of the particles of the rod $AC$ from point $A$ are equal to $\frac{1}{2} ba$; namely, the length of the rod itself, which is $a$, multiplied by half the number of particles it contains. And all the distances of the particles of weight $C$ from the same point $A$ are $ac$. So the sum of both distances is $\frac{1}{2} ab + ac$. By dividing the previously found sum of squares, $\frac{1}{3} a^2 b + a^2 c$, by this [sum of distances], the result is -> $\frac{\frac{1}{3} a^2 b + a^2 c}{\frac{1}{2} ab + ac}$ or $\frac{\frac{1}{3} b + c}{\frac{1}{2} b + c} a$ <-, the length of the isochronous pendulum. <note>isochronous: from the Greek *isos* (equal) and *khronos* (time); meaning it completes its swing in the same amount of time.</note>

This length will therefore be obtained if it is made so that half the weight of the rod, plus the weight of the attached mass, is to one-third the weight of the rod, plus the weight of the same attached mass, as the length $AC$ is to another [length]. However, one must take the length $AC$ from the point of suspension $A$ to the center of gravity of weight $C$; since the ratio of its size is not considered here, and it is regarded as a minimum <note>Huygens is treating weight C as a "point mass" for the sake of this calculation.</note>.

Now if, in addition to weight $C$, another weight $D$ is understood to be attached to the rod, whose weight, or number of particles,
<margin>
<image-desc>A vertical diagram of a physical pendulum. A top point of suspension is labeled 'A'. Below it on the line are points 'E', 'N', and 'D'. At the bottom of the line is a circular mass labeled 'C'.</image-desc>
</margin>
is $d$, and the distance $AD$ is $f$: to find a simple pendulum that is isochronous to this composite one, the squares of the distances of the particles of weight $D$ from point $A$ must be added to the previous sum of squares, which squares are clearly $d f^2$. So that the sum of all [squares] will now be $\frac{1}{3} a^2 b + a^2 c + f^2 d$. Likewise, to the sum of the distances, one must add the distances of the particles of weight $D$, which make $df$. And the sum of all the distances will therefore be $\frac{1}{2} ba + ca + df$; by dividing the sum of squares by this, it becomes -> $\frac{\frac{1}{3} a^2 b + a^2 c + f^2 d}{\frac{1}{2} ab + ac + fd}$ <-, the length of the isochronous pendulum.

But if this length of the isochronous pendulum is required to be equal to a given length, which is $p$, and all other things are given as before, except...

<margin>

</margin>
the distance $AD$ or $f$, which determines the location of the weight $D$; and if this distance is to be found, it shall be done in this manner. Namely, since it is required that $\frac{\frac{1}{3} a^2b + a^2c + f^2d}{\frac{1}{2} ab + ac + fd}$ be equal to $p$, <note>The length of the simple pendulum that swings in the same time as the compound one.</note> there arises from this equation $f^2 = pf + \frac{\frac{1}{2} abp + cap - \frac{1}{3} a^2b - a^2c}{d}$. 
And $f = \frac{1}{2} p \pm \sqrt{\frac{1}{4} p^2 + \frac{\frac{1}{2} abp + cap - \frac{1}{3} a^2b - a^2c}{d}}$. <note>Huygens uses the symbol $\infty$ for equality and "vel -" for "or minus," which we represent today with the plus-minus sign $\pm$.</note> Where it must be noticed that there are two true roots, if $\frac{1}{2} abp + cap$ is less than $\frac{1}{3} a^2b + a^2c$; that is, if the length $p$ is less than $\frac{\frac{1}{3} ab + ac}{\frac{1}{2} b + c}$, which was found previously to be the length of the isochronous pendulum—or the distance of the center of oscillation from the point of suspension—in a pendulum composed of the rod $AC$ and the weight $C$.

From this it is evident that, if we wish to cause the motion of the pendulum to be accelerated by applying weight $D$, it can be positioned in two places between $A$ and $C$, at either of which the same speed is imparted to the pendulum: for instance, at $D$ or $E$. These locations will be at an equal distance from point $N$, which is distant from $A$ by half the length $p$, that is, half the length of the simple pendulum for which this compound one was required to be isochronous <term>isochronous: swinging in equal time regardless of the width of the arc</term>. It appears, moreover, that when this length $p$ is assumed to be only slightly less than $AC$, then point $N$ is also slightly higher than the midpoint of the rod $AC$.

Furthermore, from the equation above, $f = \frac{1}{2} p \pm \sqrt{\frac{1}{4} p^2 + \frac{\frac{1}{2} abp + acp - \frac{1}{3} a^2b - a^2c}{d}}$, the determination of the length $p$ is held. For it is clear that $\frac{1}{4} p^2 + \frac{\frac{1}{2} abp + acp}{d}$ must not be less than $\frac{\frac{1}{3} a^2b + a^2c}{d}$. Hence $p$ must not be less than $\frac{a}{2d} \sqrt{\frac{4}{3} bd + 4cd + b^2 + 4bc + 4c^2} - \frac{ab + 2ac}{2d}$. But if $p$ is equal to this quantity, that is, if $\frac{1}{4} p^2 + \frac{\frac{1}{2} abp + acp}{d}$ were equal to $\frac{\frac{1}{3} a^2b + a^2c}{d}$, then in that same equation above, $f = \frac{1}{2} p$, which is $\frac{a}{2d} \sqrt{\frac{4}{3} bd + 4cd + b^2 + 4bc + 4c^2} - \frac{ab + 2ac}{2d}$. By this, the distance of the weight $D$ from point $A$ is determined, at which it accelerates the motion of the pendulum the most of all.

And these principles are further applied to the use of clocks in this way. Let there be, for example, a pendulum of a clock which marks individual seconds <note>original: "scrupula secunda"</note> with each oscillation. Let the weight of the rod be $\frac{1}{50}$ of the weight of the mass attached at the bottom of the pendulum; and, besides this, let there be another small movable weight along the length of the rod, the weight of which is...

<summary>Huygens uses quadratic equations to solve for the optimal placement of a secondary weight on a clock pendulum. He mathematically demonstrates that there are two points on a rod where an added weight will result in the same oscillation frequency, and calculates the specific point for maximum acceleration of the swing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from the previous section, specifically calculating the exact placement of a small adjustment weight (weight D) on the pendulum rod to achieve a precise change in the clock's rate, such as gaining one minute per day.</meta>

### THE PENDULUM CLOCK

<margin>
ON THE CENTER
OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>
...let it be assumed to be the same as that of the rod itself. The question now is: at what place on the rod must this weight be placed, so that the motion of the clock is accelerated by one minute <note>original: *scrupulo primo*, literally "first small part," which refers to a minute of time</note> in the space of 24 hours? Likewise, where should it be placed so that there is an acceleration of two minutes; likewise, of three, four, and so on.

Multiplying twenty-four hours by sixty results in 1440, which is the number of minutes contained in one day. Subtract one from these when an acceleration of one minute is sought: 1439 remain. Now, the ratio of 1440 to 1439 squared <note>original: *duplicata*. In pendulum physics, the period of oscillation is proportional to the square root of the length; therefore, the change in length required for a change in time is proportional to the square of the ratio of the times.</note> is very nearly the same as the ratio of 1440 to 1438. Therefore, if the length of a simple pendulum marking seconds is understood to be divided into 1440 equal parts, and 1438 of those parts are assigned to another pendulum, this latter one will lead the former by one minute in 24 hours. Thus, in this case, the length $p$ is equal to 1438 parts.

However, because the clock's pendulum, composed of a metal rod and an attached weight, is assumed to be isochronous <note>Isochronous means "equal time"; an isochronous pendulum takes the same amount of time to complete a swing regardless of how wide the swing is.</note> with a simple pendulum of 1440 parts, the length of that rod must first be found from the equation established above. Specifically, $\frac{\frac{1}{3} ab + ac}{\frac{1}{2} b + c}$ was equal to the length of the simple pendulum that is isochronous with a composite one consisting of a rod of length $a$ and weight <note>original: *gravitatem*, meaning mass or weight in this context</note> $b$, and an attached weight with weight $c$. Therefore, if the length of the isochronous simple pendulum is called $f$, then $\frac{\frac{1}{3} ab + ac}{\frac{1}{2} b + c} = f$. <note>Huygens uses the symbol $\infty$ for equality.</note> Assuming, as we do here, $c = 50$, $b = 1$, and $f = 1440$, the length of the rod $a$ will be $1444 \frac{4}{5}$.

Now, because it was $f = \frac{\frac{1}{2} p \pm \sqrt{\frac{1}{4} p^2 + \frac{1}{2} abp + acp - \frac{1}{3} a^2b - a^2c}}{d}$, <note>The OCR shows some variation in variables ($a$ vs $f$); Huygens is solving for the position of the sliding weight on the rod.</note> it becomes $f = \frac{\frac{1}{2} p \pm \sqrt{\frac{1}{4} p^2 + 72962 p - 105061210}}{d}$. From which further, if $p$ is, as we said, 1438 parts, $f$ will be found to be $1331 \frac{1}{2}$ of the same parts where $f$ (the simple pendulum marking seconds by its oscillations) contains 1440. If its length is established as three feet (which we have called "horary feet"), it will have 33 inches and 3 "units of an inch" which they call lines. <note>An "uncia" (inch) was 1/12th of a foot, and a "linea" (line) was 1/12th of an inch.</note> Or, by subtracting this length $f$ from the total length of three feet, there will remain two inches and 9 lines, to be measured upward from the center of oscillation of the compound pendulum, so that the location of weight D is found, providing an acceleration of one minute in a period of 24 hours. In the same way, we have investigated by calculation the remaining distances by which the rod is to be divided, by substituting various lengths for $p$; these are shown in the table below—

## PROPOSITION XXIV.

**The** *principle of the center of oscillation cannot be maintained in pendulums suspended between cycloids; and how the difficulty arising from this is resolved.*

If anyone, through careful examination, compares those things which we have demonstrated above concerning the pendulum suspended between cycloids <note>A cycloid is the curve traced by a point on the rim of a circular wheel as it rolls along a straight line. Huygens discovered that a pendulum swinging in a cycloidal arc is perfectly isochronous.</note> with these things which pertain to the center of oscillation, it will seem to him that something is missing from that perfect equality of oscillations which we claim. And first, he will doubt whether, to find the circle generating the cycloid, the length of the pendulum should be taken from the point of suspension to

<meta>This page continues Proposition 24, where Huygens addresses the technical discrepancy between the center of gravity and the center of oscillation in a physical pendulum. He then introduces Proposition 25, his famous proposal for a universal unit of length based on the swing of a pendulum.</meta>

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK

<margin>
ON THE CENTER
OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>
...whether [the length of the pendulum] should be measured to the center of gravity of the attached lead weight, or truly to the center of oscillation; for the latter differs from the former by a distance that is often noticeable, and becomes greater as the size of the sphere or lead lens <note>A "lens" here refers to a flattened, disc-shaped weight, which Huygens found more aerodynamic and stable than a sphere.</note> increases. For what if the diameter of the sphere should equal a fourth or a third part of the length of the pendulum? Even if we say that the length must be taken from the center of oscillation, it would not be clear how those principles demonstrated regarding the center of oscillation could apply to a pendulum that is continually changing its effective length, such as one that moves between cycloids. It might even seem that the center of oscillation itself changes at every different length; however, this is not how it should be understood.

The matter is indeed very difficult to explain if we pursue absolute simplicity <note>original: *ἀπλότητα* (haplotēta)</note>. For in the demonstration of equal times <note>isochronism</note> in a cycloid, we considered the moving body as if it were a single point endowed with gravity. But if we look at the actual effect, this difficulty is not of great concern; because the size of the weight used in clocks is not so large (though the larger the better) that the difference between the centers of gravity and oscillation could cause any significant disturbance here. 

However, if we wish to escape these complexities entirely, we can achieve it by making the sphere or lens of the pendulum movable around its own horizontal axis. This is done by inserting the ends of the axis into the bottom of the pendulum rod, which must therefore be bifid <note>split into two parts like a fork</note> at that point. In this way, by the nature of motion, it happens that the pendulum sphere always maintains the same position relative to the horizontal plane. Thus, every one of its points, just like the center itself, traverses the same cycloidal paths. Consequently, the consideration of the center of oscillation ceases to be an issue; and such a pendulum achieves no less perfect equality of time than if all its gravity were contained in a single point.

### PROPOSITION XXV.
*On the method of establishing a universal and perpetual measure.*

A certain and permanent measure of magnitudes, which is subject to no accidents and cannot be abolished or corrupted by the injuries of time or long distance, is a thing both most useful and long sought after by many. If this had been discovered in ancient times, the current disputes regarding the dimensions of the ancient Roman, Greek, and Hebrew foot <note>The "foot" was a standard unit of length, but its exact value varied wildly between different ancient civilizations and even different modern cities.</note> would not be so perplexed. Indeed, such a measure is easily established by the work of our Clock; since without it, such a thing can hardly, or only with great difficulty, be achieved.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 151 regarding the search for a universal measure of length. Huygens is explaining why using a clock is superior to earlier methods of timing pendulum swings against the rotation of the stars.</meta>

### CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>
ON THE CENTER
OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>
...be achieved. For although some have attempted this using the simple oscillation of pendulums—by counting the swings that occur during a full revolution of the heavens <note>A sidereal day, the time it takes for the earth to rotate once relative to the stars.</note>, or during a known part of it measured by the distances between fixed stars according to their right ascension <note>The celestial equivalent of longitude, used to track the movement of stars.</note>—this method does not provide the same certainty as that achieved by employing clocks. Furthermore, the labor is by far the most troublesome and tedious because of the constant anxiety of counting every swing. However, since the knowledge of the centers of oscillation contributes something to the most exact investigation of this measure (in addition to the use of clocks), we finally subjoin this determination here, following our treatment of those centers.

The most suitable instruments for this purpose are clocks whose individual oscillations mark seconds <note>original: *scrupula secunda*</note>, or half-seconds, and which are equipped with hands to display them. For once such a clock has been adjusted to the mean length of days by observations of the fixed stars—using the method we showed in the description of the clock—another simple pendulum must be suspended next to it. This should consist of a lead sphere, or one of another heavy material, bound by a thin thread. It should be set in motion with a small impulse and the length of the thread must be extended or contracted until its swings, over the course of a quarter or half an hour, proceed in perfect unison with the oscillations of the pendulum fitted to the clock. I have said it should be moved with a small impulse because small oscillations (of, say, 5 or 6 degrees) have periods that are sufficiently equal, whereas large ones do not. 

Then, once the measurement of the distance from the point of suspension to the center of oscillation of the simple pendulum has been taken, if the individual swings are equal to single seconds, that length is divided into three parts. Each of these parts will constitute the length of the foot which we previously called the <term>Hour Foot</term> <note>original: *HORARIUM*; Huygens' proposed universal unit of length, based on the constant of gravity rather than a physical prototype.</note>. In this way, a standard can be established not only among all nations but also restored for future ages. Thus, the models of all other feet, once their proportions are expressed in relation to this one, can be known with certainty in the future. As we have already stated above, the <term>Paris Foot</term> <note>A standard unit of length in France before the metric system; roughly 32.5 cm.</note> is to this Hour Foot as 864 is to 881. This is the same as saying that, if we start with the Paris Foot, a simple pendulum whose oscillations correspond to seconds of time is made of three such feet plus eight and a half lines <note>A "line" was a traditional unit equal to 1/12th of an inch.</note>. Furthermore, the Paris Foot is to the <term>Rhenish Foot</term> <note>The standard foot used in Huygens' homeland, the Netherlands; roughly 31.4 cm.</note> (which they use in our country) as 144 is to 139; that is, the Paris foot, diminished by five of its own lines, leaves the other. In this way, both this foot and any others receive measures that will last forever.

# THE PENDULUM CLOCK
### CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>
ON THE CENTER
OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>

...it has been demonstrated in the previous sections how the center of oscillation is found for a weight suspended by a length of thread. Specifically, if a proportion is made such that the distance between the point of suspension and the center of the sphere is to its radius, as the radius is to another value; then two-fifths of that value, measured downward from the center of the sphere, ends at the sought-after center of oscillation. It is easily apparent why the consideration of this center is necessary for the accurate establishment of the <term>horary foot</term> <note>The "horary foot" (*pes horarius*) was a proposed universal unit of length based on the length of a pendulum that swings exactly once per second.</note>. For if the distance is measured simply from the point of suspension to the center of the sphere, but the size of the sphere is not defined in proportion to the length of the thread, the measure of the pendulum—whose swings are meant to mark <term>seconds</term> <note>original: *scrupula secunda*</note>—will not be reliable. The larger the sphere used, the smaller the required distance between the sphere's center and the point of suspension will be found to be. This is because in <term>isochronous</term> <note>From Greek: "equal time," referring to pendulums that take the same amount of time to complete a swing regardless of the width of the arc.</note> pendulums, the centers of oscillation must be at equal distances from the points of suspension; however, the center of oscillation sits further below the center of a larger sphere than it does in a smaller one.

For this reason, it was necessary for those who attempted to establish a method for a universal measure before this determination of the oscillatory center to find a workaround. Since the very first invention of our Clock, that noble <term>Royal Society of England</term> <note>The Royal Society, founded in 1660, was a key center for the Scientific Revolution.</note> took this task upon themselves, as did more recently the most learned astronomer of Lyon, Gabriel Mouton. For these men, I say, it was necessary to specify the diameter of the suspended globe, either by a fixed proportion to the length of the thread (such as making it one-thirtieth part or some other fraction) or by some known measure, like a finger-breadth or an inch. But in this latter method, one assumes a fixed measure which is the very thing we are trying to define. Though I know the error would be hardly perceptible, provided the spheres do not exceed the size I have mentioned, the former method requires the labor of counting oscillations and the use of complex calculations. Therefore, it is better to follow a certain method by employing the centers of oscillation, so as not to be bound by unnecessary rules. Furthermore, larger spheres should be used here rather than tiny ones, because they are less hindered by the resistance of the air.

Moreover, not only spheres suspended from a thread, but also cones, cylinders, and all other solids and planes—whose centers of oscillation we displayed above—are suitable for investigating this measure. This is because the distance from the point of suspension to the center of oscillation is a fixed and identical interval for all isochronous pendulums. Nor is it only those clocks that indicate seconds or half-seconds at each swing of the pendulum that are used for this...

He is moving from the theoretical calculation of the "center of oscillation" to the practical application of the laws of physics that govern pendulum motion, specifically the relationship between a pendulum's length and the time it takes to swing.</meta>

# CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>
ON THE CENTER
OF OSCILLATION.
</margin>
...we can compare; but the objective will also be achieved with pendulums of any other length, provided that from the proportions of the wheels, or the number of teeth <note>Huygens refers to the gear ratios within the clock mechanism.</note>, the number of oscillations to be completed in a certain time is known. For once a simple pendulum has been found whose individual swings coincide with either one, two, or three beats of the clock, it will then be clear how many swings of that pendulum are completed in the space of an hour. If the number of these swings is squared, it will be as the square of 3,600 (the number of seconds <note>original: *scrupula secunda*</note> making up one hour) is to the square of that number, so the length of the discovered simple pendulum (which length must always be measured from the point of suspension to the center of oscillation) is to the length of that three-foot horary pendulum we mentioned. This is established because the lengths of any two pendulums are to each other as the squares of the times in which their individual oscillations pass; and therefore they have the inverse ratio of the squares of the numbers [of swings] completed in equal intervals of time.

For while until now that Theorem regarding the lengths of pendulums—namely, that they have a <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>A "duplicate ratio" is the ratio of the squares. For example, if the times are in a ratio of 1:2, the lengths will be in a ratio of 1:4.</note> of the times in which individual oscillations are performed—has been proven only by experience, now its demonstration is manifest from what was set forth above. Since we have shown that each swing of a pendulum suspended between <term>cycloids</term> <note>The cycloid is a specific curve that Huygens discovered allows a pendulum to remain "isochronous," meaning it takes the same amount of time to swing regardless of how wide the arc is.</note> has a certain ratio to a perpendicular fall from half the pendulum's length—namely, that ratio which the circumference of a circle has to its diameter <note>This is the ratio $\pi$. Huygens proved that the period of a cycloidal pendulum is related to the time of a free-falling object by this mathematical constant.</note>—it is easily gathered from this that the times of oscillation in two pendulums are to each other as the times of perpendicular descent from half their heights. <margin>* Proposition 3, Part 2.</margin> Since these half-heights (or even whole ones) have a duplicate ratio of the times in which they are covered by perpendicular descent, they will also have the duplicate ratio of the times that measure individual oscillations. Moreover, the smallest oscillations of a simple pendulum do not differ sensibly from the smallest oscillations of a pendulum suspended between cycloids, provided the length is the same. Therefore, the lengths of simple pendulums will also have the duplicate ratio of the times in which the smallest oscillations are completed.

But if anyone does not shrink from the labor of counting the oscillations that pass in the time of an hour or half an hour, and a clock is present whose hand shows the seconds, whatever length of simple pendulum is taken, its number of oscillations contained in one hour will be known in this way; and from there, the length of the three-foot pendulum for seconds will emerge by calculation, as before.

<summary>Huygens demonstrates the mathematical proof that the length of a pendulum is proportional to the square of its period, allowing the "horary foot" to be calculated from any pendulum of known length.</summary>

## THE PENDULUM CLOCK
### PROPOSITION XXV.
*To determine the distance which heavy bodies, falling perpendicularly, travel in a given time.*

Everyone who has investigated this measurement until now has found it necessary to consult experiments; however, because of the speed acquired by falling bodies toward the end of their motion, it is not easy to arrive at an exact determination through the methods used thus far. Yet, from our own Proposition 25 on the **Descent of Heavy Bodies**, and by knowing the length of a pendulum that swings in seconds, we can settle the matter without an experiment, through a certain logical consequence. And first, indeed, we shall seek that distance which a heavy body covers in the time of one second <note>original: *scrupulum secundum*</note>; from this, it will then be possible to gather any other measurement. Since, therefore, we have said that the length of a pendulum for measuring seconds is 3 Horary feet <note>Huygens defined a "Horary foot" as 1/3 the length of a pendulum with a period of one second.</note>: and since the time of one very small oscillation is to the time of a perpendicular fall from half the height of the pendulum as the circumference of a circle is to its diameter—that is, as 355 is to 113—if it is calculated such that the ratio of the first of these numbers to the second is the same as the ratio of the time of one second (or sixty <term>thirds</term> <note>A "third" (*scrupulum tertium*) is a historical unit of time, 1/60th of a second.</note>) to another time, the result will be 19 1/10 thirds. This is the time of descent through half the height of the pendulum, which is, of course, 18 inches. But just as the squares of the times are, so are the distances covered in those times, as was shown in the preceding proposition. Therefore, if the ratio of the square of 19 1/10 to the square of 60 (that is, 36,481 to 360,000) is made equal to the ratio of 18 inches to another value, the result will be 14 feet, 9 inches, and 6 <term>lines</term> <note>A "line" (*linea*) is a traditional measurement equal to 1/12th of an inch.</note> as the height of a perpendicular descent in the time of one second. Since, moreover, the Horary foot is to the Parisian foot as 881 is to 864, the same height, reduced to this measure, will be approximately 15 feet and one inch. And these results agree perfectly with our most accurate experiments, in which the precise moment when the fall ends is not discerned by the judgment of the ears or the eye—neither of which is sufficiently reliable here—but rather the distance covered in descending is known without any error by another method, which we shall attempt to explain here.

Half an oscillation of a pendulum suspended against a wall or an upright board indicates the duration of time consumed in falling. To ensure that its small sphere is released at the same moment as the lead weight <note>original: *plumbum*</note> intended for the fall, both are held connected by a thin thread, which is cut by applying a flame. But first, another cord is attached to the lead weight about to fall, of such a length that, when it has played out entirely, pulled by the lead, it has not yet reached the...

<meta>This page continues the experimental verification of Proposition XXV. Huygens is describing a specific apparatus designed to measure the distance a heavy body falls in a precise interval of time, using a pendulum and a marking system. He then transitions into a discussion on the effects of air resistance on these calculations.</meta>

# CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>
ON THE CENTER
OF OSCILLA-
TION.
</margin>

...the pendulum hits the wall. One end of its cord is joined to a paper ruler, or one made from thin parchment; this is applied to the wall or a board in such a way that it can easily follow the pulling cord and descend straight along its length, passing through the spot where the pendulum sphere hits the board. Therefore, once the whole cord is drawn out, an additional part of the ruler is pulled downward by the falling lead weight before the pendulum reaches the board. How large that part is, the sphere—having been lightly stained with soot and marking the ruler as it glides past <note>This is an ingenious early method of data recording: the soot-covered bob acts like a pen, leaving a physical mark on the paper strip to record the exact moment of impact.</note>—indicates. By adding the length of the cord to this, the distance traveled in falling is held as certainly defined.

Now, we understand the resistance <note>original: *occursum*</note> of the air to be as if it were nothing in these matters, so that the measure established for falling bodies may agree exactly with experiments. Nor indeed is it so great that at these heights to which we are permitted to ascend, it could introduce a noticeable difference; provided that solid bodies of metal are used, or, if they consist of lighter material, they are of a fairly large size. For the lightness of the material in those things which cut through the air while falling is compensated for by the size of the body, so that a wooden sphere, or even one made of cork, may act similarly to a leaden one: specifically, when the diameter of these has that ratio to the diameter of the leaden one which the specific gravity of lead has to the gravity of wood or cork. For then the weights <note>original: *gravitates*</note> of the spheres will be to each other as their surface areas. Nevertheless, so that bodies which differ much in intrinsic gravity may fall with equal speed, as far as can be perceived by the senses, it is by no means necessary for that proportion of diameters to be maintained. For they can be equal to each other, provided both are large enough, or they fall from a height that is not too great. Indeed, it must also be noted here that the height may be so great, or, even at a moderate height, the lightness of the projected body may be so great, that because of the resistance of the air, the acceleration of motion will eventually depart significantly from that proportion which we demonstrated in the preceding pages. For in general, for any body falling through air or another liquid, a certain limit of speed <term>terminal velocity</term> <note>Huygens is describing the concept of terminal velocity: the point where the upward force of air resistance equals the downward force of gravity, preventing further acceleration.</note> is established according to the ratio of its weight and its surface; which it can never exceed, or rather, which it can never quite reach. That speed, of course, is the same which the air or liquid would have if, by moving upward, it could support the same body floating suspended in it. But perhaps there will be an occasion to discuss these matters more fully elsewhere.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Part Five of the treatise. Having previously discussed the physics of falling bodies and standard pendulums, Huygens now introduces a different type of clock mechanism based on circular motion rather than a back-and-forth swing, leading into his groundbreaking work on centrifugal force.</meta>

## THE PENDULUM CLOCK
### PART FIVE.

*Containing another construction, derived from the circular motion of pendulums; and Theorems on Centrifugal Force.*

**T**HERE **IS** also another <term>type of oscillatory motion</term> <note>original: *Oscillatórii motus genus*</note>, besides that which we have discussed up to this point. Namely, one in which a pendulum weight is carried around the circumference of a circle. From this, we derived another clock invention at nearly the same time as the first one; it likewise relies on a certain principle of uniformity, but its use has been less common because the construction of that other one was, in a way, simpler and easier. Nevertheless, many of this current kind of which we now speak have been constructed, and not without success. In these clocks, there is a singular feature: the final hand, which marks the seconds <note>original: *secunda scrupula*</note>, is seen to be carried around with a continuous and uniform motion; whereas in our previous clock, and in all others, it moves in a sort of jerking fashion <note>Huygens is describing the "ticking" movement of a standard escapement versus the smooth sweep of a conical pendulum.</note>. Likewise, clocks constructed in this way move without any noise or sound. However, for astronomical observations, a sound repeated at every single second is not without its uses. 

I had originally intended to publish the description of these clocks only alongside those matters pertaining to <term>circular motion</term> and <term>Centrifugal Force</term> <note>original: *Vim Centrifugam*—Huygens is the first to coin this term in physics</note>; for so I choose to call it. I have much to say on that subject, which I might have the leisure to complete at this time. But, so that students of these matters may enjoy this new and useful speculation sooner, and lest it be lost by some accident, I have added this part to the others beyond my original plan. In it, the manufacture of this machine is briefly explained, and at the same time, Theorems are presented regarding centrifugal force; though the proofs for them are deferred to another time.

I did not think it necessary to display here the arrangement of the wheels which make up the internal works of the clock, since that can be easily handled by craftsmen...

# CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS

<margin>DESCRIPTION OF THE SECOND CLOCK.</margin>
...can be easily arranged and changed in various ways; but it is sufficient to explain that part which regulates its motion according to a certain principle. A figure of this part is expressed here.

<image-desc>A technical geometric diagram depicting a vertical rotational axis labeled with points K, D, G, A, E, H. A curved template, identified as a paraboloid and labeled AB, is attached to the axis at point A. A line representing a thread, labeled BGF, is attached at point B, passes through point G, and terminates at a weight F. Point F rests on a broad parabolic arc labeled EF. A horizontal line segment connects points D and B. At the top of the axis (K), there is a small mechanism referred to as a tympanidium. The base of the axis at H is set within a rectangular block.</image-desc>

The axis **D H** is to be understood as standing perpendicular to the horizon and is movable upon two pivots. To this axis at **A** is attached a plate, endowed with a certain width and curved according to the line **A B**; this is that <term>paraboloid</term> <note>original: *paraboloides*. In this context, it is a specifically curved guide or "evolute" template.</note> about which we demonstrated in Proposition 8 of Part 3, that a **parabola** is described by its <term>evolution</term> <note>original: *evolutione*. The mathematical process of "unfolding" a curve by unwinding a string kept taut against it.</note>, after a certain straight line has been joined to it. That straight line here is **A E**; moreover, the line **E F** represents the parabola described from the evolution of the whole **B A E**. The thread applied to the curve **B A**, from whose end point the parabola is described, is **B G F**. The weight attached to it is **F**. While the axis **D H** turns upon itself, the thread **B G F**, extended in a straight line, carries the small sphere **F** around with it, so that it traverses circles parallel to the horizon. These circles will be larger or smaller, depending on whether the axis **D H** is driven with a greater or lesser speed by the wheels of the clock acting on the <term>tympanidium</term> <note>original: *tympanidium*. A small drum or geared pinion used to transmit motion.</note> **κ**; but they are driven in such a way that all the circles are contained within the surface of a <term>parabolic conoid</term> <note>original: *conoidis parabolici*. The three-dimensional shape formed by rotating a parabola around its axis; today we call this a paraboloid of revolution.</note>. And by this very fact, the times of the circuit are always equal...

<summary>Huygens describes the mechanical components of his "second clock," detailing how a parabolic metal guide ensures the rotating pendulum maintains a constant time of revolution (isochronism) regardless of the circle's diameter.</summary>
<keywords>conical pendulum, paraboloid, isochronism, evolution of curves, horology, tympanidium, parabolic conoid</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues Huygens' technical description of the "conical pendulum" clock, specifically detailing the mathematical proportions of the parabolic guide and the mechanical construction of the rotation axis. It then transitions into a new section on the fundamental laws of centrifugal force.</meta>

...will result, as will be seen from what we shall say later regarding this motion.

<margin>DESCRIPTION OF THE SECOND CLOCK.</margin>
If we wish for each rotation to mark half-seconds <note>original: *secundorum scrupulorum semisses*. A "scrupulum secundum" is the historical term for a "second" of time.</note>, the <term>latus rectum</term> <note>The "straight side" or parameter that defines the width of a conic section; here it determines the specific shape of the parabola.</note> of the parabola **E F** must be $4 \frac{1}{2}$ inches of our **Horary foot** <note>Huygens used a specific "Horary foot" based on the length of a pendulum that swings once per second.</note>; this is half the length of a pendulum whose individual oscillations would take half a second. From the latus rectum of the parabola depends the size of the latus rectum of the **paraboloid A B**, which contains $27/16$ of the former; likewise, the length **A E** is half of the latus rectum of the parabola. If, however, we desire that full seconds be completed in each rotation, then the previous values—both the latera recta and the line **A E**—must be quadrupled.

Furthermore, although we have so far described the thread **B G F** as if it were a single and simple line, it should be known that it is far better for it to be double at the upper part and to meet in an angle of 20 or 30 degrees toward **F**. For this purpose, the width of the plate **A B** at **B** must be as great as is sufficient for that divergence of the threads, or the plate itself must be made bifid <note>split into two parts.</note>. In this way, the circular motion of the weight **F** is maintained without any other support, and it keeps both attached threads extended in a straight line; this would not happen if it were held by only a single thread. It must be noted, however, that force is required from the clock's wheels—moved either by a weight or another power—to maintain this circular motion. This force reaches the axis **K H** through the small drum <note>original: *tympanidium*</note> **K** and, with the slightest effort, preserves the motion once imparted to the sphere **F**.

To make this as easy as possible, the revolution of the axis **K H** must be extremely free. It has been found that this is achieved by no better means than if the lowest part consists of hardened steel and rests upon a flat **diamond surface**; even the smallest particle of diamond suffices here, to be placed beneath the perforated plate.

Furthermore, in place of the thread **B G F**, where it must be applied to the curve **A B**, it will be permissible to use a thin chain of gold or another metal, so that the length may be better preserved without variation. We have also tried this in the first clock, where the pendulum is suspended between **cycloids**. But in that case, the continuous bending of the chain, due to the friction of the rings—however small—impedes the free movement of the pendulum significantly.

# ON CENTRIFUGAL FORCE
## from circular motion, Theorems.

->I.<-

If two equal moving bodies traverse unequal circumferences in equal times, the centrifugal force will be in ma-

<meta>catchword: ma- (continued on next page as 'magnitudine')</meta>

<summary>Huygens provides the technical specifications for his conical pendulum clock, including the use of a diamond pivot and gold chains for precision, before beginning his landmark treatise on centrifugal force.</summary>
<keywords>conical pendulum, latus rectum, Horary foot, diamond pivot, gold chain, centrifugal force, circular motion, mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific mechanical design of the "parabolic" clock to a formal mathematical treatise on the laws of centrifugal force. It presents the fundamental propositions that underpin the physics of circular motion, moving from basic ratios of speed and diameter to the properties of motion within a parabolic surface.</meta>

<header>CHRISTIAAN HUYGENS</header>

<margin>
ON CENTRIFUGAL 
FORCE.
</margin>

*...in the larger circumference to that which is in the smaller, just as the circumferences themselves are to each other, or their diameters.* <note>This sentence concludes Proposition I from the previous page, establishing that for bodies moving in the same amount of time, the centrifugal force is proportional to the diameter of the circle.</note>

### I I.

If two equal moving bodies are carried at an equal speed in unequal circumferences, their centrifugal forces will be in the inverse ratio of their diameters. <note>In modern terms: $F = mv^2/r$. If $v$ is constant, $F$ is inversely proportional to the radius/diameter.</note>

### I I I.

If two equal moving bodies are carried in equal circumferences at unequal speeds, but both with uniform motion <note>original: *motu æquabili*; motion at a constant rate.</note> (as we wish to be understood in all these cases), the centrifugal force of the faster body will be to the force of the slower in the <term>duplicate ratio</term> <note>The "duplicate ratio" is the classical mathematical term for the square of the ratio. Thus, if the speed doubles, the force quadruples.</note> of their speeds.

### I V.

If two equal moving bodies, carried around in unequal circumferences, have equal centrifugal force, the time of the circuit in the larger circumference will be to the time of the circuit in the smaller in the <term>subduplicate ratio</term> <note>The "subduplicate ratio" refers to the square root of the ratio.</note> of the diameters.

### V.

If a moving body is carried in the circumference of a circle at that speed which it acquires by falling from a height equal to a quarter of the diameter, it will have a centrifugal force equal to its own gravity <note>original: *gravitati æqualem*; its weight.</note>; that is, it will stretch the cord by which it is held at the center with the same force as when it is suspended from it.

### V I.

In the hollow surface of a <term>parabolic conoid</term> <note>A paraboloid of revolution, shaped like a bowl formed by rotating a parabola.</note> which has its axis erected perpendicularly, all circuits of a moving body traversing circumferences parallel to the horizon—whether they be small or large—are performed in equal times. These individual times are equal to two <term>oscillations</term> <note>Huygens defines an oscillation as a single "swing" (half a period).</note> of a pendulum whose length is half of the <term>latus rectum</term> <note>The parameter that determines the "width" or "spread" of the parabola.</note> of the generating parabola.

### V I I.

If two moving bodies, suspended from unequal threads, are rotated so that they traverse circumferences parallel to the horizon while one end of the thread remains fixed; and if the heights of the cones whose surfaces the threads describe by this motion are equal, then the times of the rotations will also be equal. <note>This describes the "conical pendulum," noting that the time of rotation depends only on the vertical distance from the point of suspension to the plane of the circle, not the length of the string.</note>

### V I I I.

<summary>Huygens outlines the foundational mathematical laws of centrifugal force, demonstrating how force relates to speed, diameter, and weight, culminating in the proof that a parabolic surface creates perfectly timed (isochronous) rotations.</summary>
<keywords>centrifugal force, uniform motion, duplicate ratio, subduplicate ratio, parabolic conoid, conical pendulum, latus rectum, isochronism</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues Christiaan Huygens' "Treatise on Centrifugal Force," which was appended to his 1673 work "Horologium Oscillatorium." Having established the basic ratios between speed, diameter, and force on the previous page, Huygens now applies these principles to conical pendulums—pendulums that swing in a circular path rather than a simple arc—culminating in the final propositions of the work.</meta>

<header>THE PENDULUM CLOCK</header>
<sig>X</sig>

<margin>
ON CENTRIFUGAL 
FORCE.
</margin>

### V I I I.

*If two moving bodies, as before, are rotated with a conical motion <note>A conical motion occurs when the pendulum bob travels in a horizontal circle, causing the string to trace the shape of a cone.</note>, suspended by strings of equal or unequal length; and if the vertical heights of these cones are unequal; the times of their revolutions will be in the subduplicate ratio <note>The square root of the ratio. In modern terms: $T \propto \sqrt{h}$.</note> of those heights.*

### I X.

*If a pendulum, moving in a conical motion, completes very small circuits; the time of each individual circuit, compared to the time of a vertical fall from twice the pendulum’s height, has the same ratio as the circumference of a circle to its diameter; and therefore, these times are equal to the time of two small lateral oscillations <note>A "lateral oscillation" is a standard back-and-forth swing. Huygens is proving that a circular swing takes exactly as long as two side-to-side swings.</note> of that same pendulum.*

### X.

*If a moving body is carried along a circumference and completes each circuit in the same time that a pendulum, with a length equal to the radius <note>original: *semidiametri*; the distance from the center to the edge.</note> of that circumference, would complete a very small conical circuit or two small lateral oscillations; it will possess a centrifugal force equal to its own weight <note>original: *gravitati*; the force of gravity acting upon it.</note>.*

### X I.

*For any pendulum moving with a conical motion, the time of one circuit will be equal to the time of a vertical fall from a height equal to the pendulum's string, when the angle of the string's inclination to the horizontal plane is approximately 2 degrees and 54 minutes. More exactly, this occurs when the sine of that angle is to the radius as the square inscribed in a circle is to the square of its circumference.*

### X I I.

*If two pendulums of equal weight, but unequal string lengths, are rotated with a conical motion, and the vertical heights of the cones are equal; the forces with which they pull their strings will be in the same ratio as the lengths of the strings.*

### X I I I.

*If a simple pendulum is swung with its maximum lateral oscillation—that is, if it descends through a full quarter-circle <note>Falling from a horizontal position (90 degrees) to the bottom of the arc.</note>—it will pull its string with three times more force when it reaches the lowest point of the circle than if it were simply hanging at rest from the string.*

->T H E   E N D.<-

<vocab>conical motion, centrifugal force, pendulum, oscillations, heights, circuit, weight (gravity), sine, radius</vocab>

<summary>This concluding page of the treatise establishes the mathematical relationships governing conical pendulums and calculates the specific conditions under which centrifugal force equals gravity. It ends with the significant observation that a pendulum swinging from a horizontal position exerts three times its resting weight at the bottom of its arc.</summary>

<keywords>centrifugal force, conical pendulum, oscillation period, gravity, mechanical laws, Christiaan Huygens, circular motion</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page serves as the "Corrigenda" or errata list for Christiaan Huygens' seminal 1673 work, "Horologium Oscillatorium" (The Pendulum Clock). Following the mathematical proofs regarding centrifugal force and conical pendulums, this list ensures the technical accuracy of the complex geometric proofs and diagrams provided in the main text. It is typical for high-level scientific treatises of this era to include such corrections to account for typesetting errors in dense mathematical notation.</meta>

## CORRECTIONS.

Page 5, line 9 from the bottom, for N read R; which letter was omitted in the diagram.
Page 6, line 4 from the bottom, for x write a.
In the same place, the last line, for E write e. And likewise on the following page, line 2.
Page 7, line 2 from the bottom, for M K write k.
Page 16, line 13, after "or a thirtieth" <note>original: *tricesimamve*</note>, add "or even smaller."
On the same page, lines 23 and 24, the letters A, B, and C are cited, which were omitted in the diagram. On that same line 24, after "from point c," add "the center of oscillation" <note>The center of oscillation is the point on a pendulum where the entire mass seems to be concentrated, determining its swinging period.</note>.
Page 26, line 3, read "square" <note>original: *quadrato*</note>.
Page 56, line 7 from the bottom, for o A read s A.
Pages 81 and 84, in the figure on the left, the line T x should not have been drawn from T, but rather the straight line v x from v, parallel to L K.
Page 85, line 11 from the bottom, after K M, L N, add "of which the latter will be greater."
Page 86, line 1, for a a x read a x x, and delete <term>δ</term> y.
Page 87, line 10 from the bottom, for F D write B D.
In the same place, line 2 from the bottom, for F H K write A R I.
Also, line 3 from the bottom, read "extended" <note>original: *continuatæ*; referring to geometric lines continued beyond their initial points</note>.
Page 95, line 1, for B write G.
On the same page, line 5 from the bottom, read "we intend" <note>original: *volumus*; indicating a correction to the author's intended phrasing</note>.
Page 99, line 12, delete "just as Q Q" <note>original: *velut*</note>.
Page 104, line 10, for A D read M D.
Page 107, in the figure on the right, point H ought to have been on the other side of point ε.
Page 111, lines 4, 6, and 7 from the bottom, for z z m read z x m.
Page 112, line 2, for <sup>m m l l</sup>/<sub>θ</sub>, read <sup>m m - l l</sup>/<sub>θ</sub>.
Page 123, line 20, for "and H the center of gravity" <note>original: *centrum gravitatis*</note>, read "and o H subcentric" <note>original: *subcentrica*; this likely refers to a specific distance or line related to the center of oscillation or the axis of the pendulum</note>.
